That audit verified the deferrals; this one verifies the records' claims about the code. The register's own preamble says that nothing in it needs re-verifying against the code because nothing in it claims anything about the code — which is true of the register and false of the records it now points at, since each of those states the deferral in full, gives its code evidence, and fixes a completion trigger. Those are exactly the checkable claims this campaign exists to check.

A pin is therefore a statement about the record, not about the register entry: still-deferred, already-landed, obsolete, or record-inaccurate — the last being the case where the gap is real but the record's account of it has drifted from the code. A field that exists on the health surface but is fed by a constant is still-deferred, and keeping that distinction sharp is most of the work of this pass.

**Table (The originally audited deferrals, pinned)** · `tab:assayer:deferral-pins`

At the audit cut, every entry of the register was resolved into the record that owns it, the record's statement and completion trigger were read, and the code the record points at was read. All twenty-eight citations resolved, and every one resolved to a mint rather than to a further citation. This is now a qualified historical census: the Schur skip-counter row has since completed and moved into the register's retirements; the pending-buffer pair and the identity cluster have since joined it, followed by the Ledger depth, delta and maturity health entries, the five delivered health entries and the five completed metric entries below. The profiled label-parallelism entry and synchronisation-error visibility entry have since completed and joined those retirements. Two more have left the open set since, by different routes: per-Sentinel informativeness completed, its surfaced constant replaced by the measured mean slot weight, and encoding effectiveness was retired as superseded design history rather than built, the reading that replaced it being that same informativeness value.

At the audit cut, every one of the twenty-eight was still deferred, and no record's statement, code evidence, or completion trigger was found inaccurate. That was a stronger result than the pass expected, and it had a mechanical cause worth recording: the migrated records cite files without line numbers, so their evidence cannot rot on a line shift. The three entries then marked as surfaced constants were the shape the pass was built to catch — a health field that exists, is exported as a live metric, and is fed a literal — and in all three the owning record said so rather than claiming the field was absent. The completed pins this table no longer projects as open carry their evidence in their owning records and retirement entries.

**Remark (The register's exemption is drawn too wide)** · `rem:assayer:register-overclaim`

The register's preamble states that nothing in it needs re-verifying against the code, because nothing in it claims anything about the code. That is true of the twenty-eight entries and false of the file, because the retirements section is not a citation list and says so itself: it makes about a dozen claims about the code, each pinned to a line number, and several of those numbers have already drifted by a handful of lines against the current tree. The claims remain correct in substance — all three retirements were re-verified and all three stand — but the exemption should be scoped to the entry list rather than asserted over the file. This is a finding against the register's prose and is recorded here rather than repaired, because the audit does not rewrite the records it audits.

The one entry the earlier audit flagged as ambiguous has since been settled on the narrower reading. Per-report acknowledgements alone did not satisfy *accumulated per Sentinel*; Ledger health now reads process-lifetime creation and deletion counters from each Sentinel slot (`entry:memory:cell-deltas`). The retirement therefore closes the gap the audit identified rather than changing the completion trigger's meaning.

## First tranche: the Outcome Ledger · `sec:assayer:audit-tranche-ledger`

The cluster is `src/ledger/` — the entry type, the cell-set maintenance, the routing, the collector, and the per-Sentinel store — together with the two call sites outside the directory that constitute the Ledger's read and write paths. It was taken against the Outcome Ledger chapter of the specification and against the memory record, which is the record that fixes this cluster's conceptual choices; the retention record governs the pending buffer rather than the Ledger and enters only where the two are confused.

The cluster was chosen because the founding worklist points at it hardest and because its specification is unusually checkable: the chapter fixes six parameters with defaults and constraints, an eight-step write algorithm, a two-mode decay that it says must agree exactly, and three entry-lifecycle algorithms. A tranche that could not adjudicate this cluster could not adjudicate any.

**Table (What the cluster conforms to)** · `tab:assayer:ledger-conformant`

Recorded first, because an audit that lists only its findings misrepresents what it read. Each row was checked against the cited environment and holds.

| Checked | Authority | Evidence |
| --- | --- | --- |
| The four shipped Ledger defaults | (`tab:config:ledger`) | `src/config/types.rs:332` |
| Write-time decay precedes the averaging step | (`alg:ledger:all-layers-update`) | `src/ledger/entry.rs:166` |
| Read-time decay is pure and agrees with write-time | (`def:ledger:time-decay`) | `src/ledger/entry.rs:129` |
| The write visits every containing layer, deepest first | (`alg:ledger:all-layers-update`) | `src/ledger/routing.rs:146` |
| Creation zeroes every average and inherits nothing | (`alg:ledger:entry-creation`) | `src/ledger/entry.rs:106` |
| Deletion is outright, with no merge into the ancestor | (`just:ledger:no-state-transfer`) | `src/ledger/cell_set.rs:139` |
| The root is exempt from deletion and from collection | (`def:ledger:root-semantics`) | `src/ledger/cell_set.rs:131` |
| An unbroken absence run is required before deletion | (`alg:ledger:entry-deletion`) | `src/ledger/cell_set.rs:100` |
| Per-axis rows come into existence on first write | (`dec:memory:lazy-axis-rows`) | `src/ledger/entry.rs:191` |
| Collection judges emptiness across per-axis rows too | (`alg:ledger:garbage-collection`) | `src/ledger/entry.rs:211` |

The specification's own remark under (`alg:ledger:entry-deletion`) — that an implementation naming the absence threshold for a suspension it does not perform should correct the name — is answered: the code calls the counter an absence count and the threshold an absence threshold, and performs deletion. Nothing needs renaming.

**Entry (The assessment path never leaves the root entry)** · `entry:assayer:wl-ledger-root-only-read`

**Locus** `src/extraction/mod.rs:295`, where the Ledger read for a request computes its key at depth zero and looks that key up directly. A labelled notice stood there asking for exactly the routing this entry names, and this entry cited it. The repair below ended the defect and took the notice with it, so the citation goes too, under the one amendment the worklist grammar forces rather than chooses (`conv:assayer:worklist-grammar`). Nothing else here is amended.

**Disposition** divergent-code-wrong. The specification says feature extraction resolves to the deepest available layer (`def:ledger:purpose`), and gives the root the role of fallback for coordinates *without* a finer entry rather than the role of answer for all of them (`def:ledger:root-semantics`). The memory record states the same choice and names the file that implements it correctly (`dec:memory:depth-walk`). The depth walk exists and is correct at `src/ledger/routing.rs:72`; the assessment path does not call it.

**Severity** high. Every request's Ledger features carry the domain-wide root average rather than the cell's own history, so the one capability the chapter says no other pathway can supply — remembering that a particular cell was dangerous after the measurement baselines have adapted (`summ:ledger:value`) — is absent from the surface that decides. The whole analytic half of the chapter reasons about per-cell rates (`thm:ledger:materiality`) that never reach the model. Two further consequences follow. The immaturity flag raised into the alarm summary at `src/extraction/mod.rs:232` is derived from the same lookup, so it reports per-cell immaturity as false whenever the Sentinel has a root at all, which is always. And the guidance path does walk depth correctly at `src/guidance/mod.rs:324`, so the starvation score (`def:ledger:starvation-score`) and the feature vector are computed from different entries for the same coordinate.

**What conformance would mean** the read at `src/extraction/mod.rs:295` calls the depth walk instead of computing a depth-zero key, and the immaturity flag becomes a statement about the entry the walk actually reached.

**Test coverage** none. The depth walk is tested thoroughly in its own module and in the ledger suite, and nothing asserts that extraction uses it — which is precisely why a correct routing function and an assessment path that ignores it have coexisted.

**Repaired** at `26a5ad29`, by no numbered wave but by the lane that touched the file, which is what the closing proposal asked of every residual item justifying no wave of its own (`proposal:assayer:repair-waves`). Both halves of the conformance line landed together, as the entry has it: the read takes the depth walk, by key rather than by reference so that the rootless Ledger the assessment path still admits reports absence instead of panicking, and the immaturity flag becomes the specified predicate over the entry the walk reached. The coverage gap this entry named is closed at the same commit — the tests that were missing are the ones asserting that extraction uses the walk, and they are now the pair that separates a deep cell's own history from the root's, either way round.

**Entry (The collection sweep has no caller)** · `entry:assayer:wl-ledger-sweep-unscheduled`

**Locus** `src/ledger/gc.rs:142` and `src/ledger/gc.rs:204`. Every call site of either entry point in the workspace is a test or a test-support fixture.

**Disposition** divergent-code-wrong. The specification's default strategy is a maintenance task that scans each Sentinel's Ledger on a fixed cadence, hourly by default (`alg:ledger:garbage-collection`), and the memory record states flatly that stale entries are removed by a periodic sweep (`dec:memory:periodic-sweep`). The sweep is implemented, bounded in both phases exactly as that record requires, and never run.

**Severity** medium. The specification is explicit that collection is a storage economy and never a correctness mechanism, so nothing computed is wrong; what is lost is the bound on the store's growth, since below the root an entry now leaves only by the absence rule, which requires the Sentinel to go on reporting a geometry that omits it. Two configuration knobs are inert in consequence — the collection horizon and the collection floor at `src/config/types.rs:323` reach no consumer — so a host tuning retention is tuning nothing.

**What conformance would mean** the maintenance loop invokes the batched sweep per Sentinel on the configured cadence, reading the horizon and floor from the Ledger configuration rather than from a caller's literals.

**Test coverage** the collector's predicates are well tested; that it is ever invoked outside a test is not.

**Repaired** in wave three at `149ec48d`, after the cursor rider below and in that order, as the severity coupling requires. A dedicated maintenance thread — the checkpoint scheduler's shape, shut down by the same dropped-control mechanism — invokes the batched sweep per Sentinel hourly, reading the floor and horizon from the Ledger configuration and the present through the injected clock, so the two retention knobs now reach a consumer. A scheduler-level test pins that collection actually collects and that the recent and the root survive.

**Entry (The batched sweep addresses a hash map by position)** · `entry:assayer:wl-ledger-positional-cursor`

**Locus** `src/ledger/gc.rs:286`, where a scan window is taken by skipping a running count of already-scanned survivors into the entry map's iteration order.

**Disposition** divergent-code-wrong, and the argument needs care because the two entry points differ. In the borrowed form the compensation is sound: the map is not mutated by anyone else for the duration, removal preserves the relative order of the survivors, and subtracting the removed count from the cursor lands the next window exactly where the previous one ended. In the lock-aware form — the one the module's own guidance tells callers to prefer — the lock is released between the scan and the deletion and again between windows, so a concurrent insertion may resize and rehash the table. After that the cursor addresses arbitrary positions: entries never inspected are skipped, and inspected ones are inspected again. A sweep that can skip entries does not scan the Ledger, which is what (`alg:ledger:garbage-collection`) asks of it.

**Severity** low today and medium latent. No entry is wrongly deleted, because every candidate is revalidated under the write lock at `src/ledger/gc.rs:308` before removal, so what is lost is the completeness of the sweep and not the correctness of any deletion. And the defect is currently unreachable in a deployment, because of (`entry:assayer:wl-ledger-sweep-unscheduled`): nothing calls the sweep at all. Fixing that entry without this one would turn a dormant defect into a live one, and the two should therefore be closed together.

**What conformance would mean** the sweep carries a cursor that survives rehashing — a resume key rather than an offset, or a snapshot of the key set taken once per sweep and consumed across windows.

**Test coverage** the batched sweep is exercised at `src/tests/ledger.rs:744` with limits chosen to force several windows, but under no concurrent writer, which is the only condition under which the defect appears.

**Repaired** in wave three at `6e596a4d`, riding the sweep's caller as this entry directs and landing first so the defect never went live. The second of the two offered forms: the lock-aware sweep snapshots the key set once per sweep and consumes it across windows, revalidating each key under its lock, so a rehash between windows misdirects nothing; the borrowed form's sound compensation is untouched. A concurrent-writer test pins completeness under continuous insertion, and the bounded-windows test now counts the snapshot acquisition alongside its scan window.

**Entry (The absence threshold is truncated behind a guard that does not exist)** · `entry:assayer:wl-ledger-absence-cast`

**Locus** `src/api/report.rs:81`, where the configured absence threshold is narrowed to a byte under a lint exemption whose stated justification is that the value is validated at configuration time to fit.

**Disposition** divergent-code-wrong. No such validation exists: the builder's numerical pass at `src/api/builder.rs:195` reaches exactly one Ledger field, the averaging rate at `src/api/builder.rs:290`, and never the absence threshold. **Both line citations are corrected in place by the public surface tranche** (`rem:assayer:api-guidance-cross-cites`), which owns the builder: the pass begins at `src/api/builder.rs:195` and its one Ledger field is at `src/api/builder.rs:290`, and the substantive claim was re-derived branch by branch rather than transcribed and holds exactly as written. The specification constrains the threshold to at least one (`tab:config:ledger`), so a value that narrows to zero is out of range by the corpus's own table, and nothing checks it.

**Severity** medium. It takes a host configuring a value above two hundred and fifty-five to trigger, but the consequence is severe and silent: a threshold of zero makes the deletion predicate at `src/ledger/cell_set.rs:132` true for every non-root entry, so the entire Ledger below the root is destroyed on every report cycle and every cell's history restarts from neutral. The finding is really about the false assurance — a comment that asserts a guard the code does not have is worse than an unguarded cast, because it stops the next reader looking.

**What conformance would mean** the builder validates the absence threshold against the specified lower bound, at which point the cast's justification becomes true.

**Test coverage** none; the ingestion tests set the threshold through the narrow type directly and never exercise the configuration path.

Re-verified by the thirteenth tranche from the configuration side, where the narrowing's source lives. The corrected line citations hold, the threshold is genuinely wired rather than inert — `src/api/report.rs:83` reads `config.ledger.n_absent` and it reaches the deletion predicate — and the entry is now the worst-consequence member of a larger set: it is one of twenty-four tabulated parameters the builder's pass never reaches (`entry:assayer:wl-config-unreached-constraints`).

**Entry (The starvation window is a constant where the corpus makes it a parameter)** · `entry:assayer:wl-ledger-starvation-window`

**Locus** `src/guidance/mod.rs:42`, where the recent-eligible-label window is a module constant.

**Disposition** divergent-code-wrong, narrowly. The value is right — two hundred, as tabulated — but (`tab:config:ledger`) lists the window as a configuration parameter with a default and a lower bound, and the package offers no field for it. The specification elsewhere is careful to say when a tabulated row describes a surface the package does not offer; it does not say so here, so this is an unrecorded divergence rather than a known one.

**Severity** low. Nothing computes a wrong number today. The row is the denominator of the starvation score (`def:ledger:starvation-score`), so a deployment whose labelling capacity differs from the assumed one cannot move the threshold at which a cell stops counting as starved.

**What conformance would mean** the window joins the Ledger configuration with its tabulated default and its lower bound checked, and the guidance path reads it from there — **at two sites rather than one**, the public surface tranche having found an integer twin at `src/guidance/mod.rs:45` that caps the count before the division (`rem:assayer:api-guidance-cross-cites`).

**Test coverage** the score is tested against the constant, which is the tautological shape: the test would pass for any value the constant took.

**Entry (The entry carries a tenth field the state table does not list)** · `entry:assayer:wl-ledger-tenth-field`

**Locus** `src/ledger/entry.rs:98`, the consecutive-absence counter.

**Disposition** unspecified, and the corpus is the party with the gap. The state table says every entry at every depth carries the same nine fields (`tab:ledger:entry-state`) and lists nine; the deletion algorithm's first step requires counting consecutive report cycles in which the interval does not appear (`alg:ledger:entry-deletion`) and never says where that count lives. The code's answer — a counter on the entry — is the obvious one and may well be the intended one, but the corpus does not say it, so the field is neither conformant nor divergent.

**Severity** low. Nothing is computed wrongly. The consequence is that a reader sizing an entry from the table gets the wrong answer, and that a future implementation could hold the count elsewhere without contradicting anything written down.

**What conformance would mean** the state table gains the field, or the deletion algorithm says where the count is held. This is a corpus amendment and therefore not this campaign's to make; it is recorded for the record pipeline.

**Entry (Two routing siblings disagree about a rootless store)** · `entry:assayer:wl-ledger-rootless-disagreement`

**Locus** `src/ledger/routing.rs:72` and `src/ledger/routing.rs:91`. The first falls through to a root accessor that panics when no root is present; the second falls through to fabricating the root key and returning it, so a caller receives a key naming an entry that does not exist.

**Disposition** unspecified. The corpus fixes that the root is permanent and created at Sentinel registration (`def:ledger:root-semantics`), which makes a rootless store a state the corpus says cannot arise; it does not say what happens if it does. The store nonetheless permits the state — construction leaves the root absent until a separate call installs it, which the store's own notice at `src/ledger/sentinel_ledger.rs:131` already flags — so the question is reachable in the type even though it is excluded by the design.

**Severity** low. The finding is the disagreement rather than either behaviour: two functions documented as differing only in return type answer the same impossible input in two incompatible ways, and a caller cannot choose between them on the documentation.

**What conformance would mean** the root is installed by construction, per the notice already standing at the store, after which both fall-throughs become unreachable and can say so.

## Second tranche: the runtime owner · `sec:assayer:audit-tranche-owner`

The cluster is `src/owner/` — the label path, the axis and dimension registrations, the command surface, and the maintenance thread — taken against the three records the scope map assigns it (`tab:assayer:audit-scope-map`): ownership, which fixes custody and the direction information travels; concurrency, which fixes what may run beside what; and ordering, which fixes the label path as one function in ordered groups. Those records decline to restate the enumerations the specification owns (`conv:ordering:granularity`), so the runtime, assessment and label operations chapters carry most of what is checkable here and the records carry the shape it must hold.

The cluster was taken second because the founding worklist points at it harder than at anything else: three of its entries have their locus in this directory and a fourth has its only call site here. It is also the cluster where the ledger tranche's closing lesson applies most directly, because `src/owner/` is where the package's periodic work would be scheduled if anything scheduled it.

**Table (What the owner cluster conforms to)** · `tab:assayer:owner-conformant`

Recorded first, because an audit that lists only its findings misrepresents what it read. Forty-nine rows were checked across the cluster and hold; the selection below is the load-bearing part of that set, and the configuration wiring is summarised beneath it rather than tabulated row by row.

| Checked | Authority | Evidence |
| --- | --- | --- |
| The seventeen label steps run in the specified order, none skipped or reordered | (`alg:runtime:update-path`) | `src/owner/label_path.rs:404` |
| Elapsed decay is computed once per label and applied to each model exactly once | (`dec:ordering:decay-at-head`) | `src/owner/label_path.rs:411` |
| Time and label decay combine into one factor rather than two multiplications | (`dec:posterior:combined-factor`) | `src/owner/label_path.rs:530` |
| Both publications fall at the end of the path, on all three exit paths | (`dec:ordering:publish-at-end`) | `src/owner/label_path.rs:1012` |
| Learned state reaches readers only as a whole snapshot installed by one swap | (`dec:concurrency:snapshot-swap`) | `src/owner/label_path.rs:1227` |
| The operational model updates unconditionally; sister and anchor are gated | (`alg:runtime:update-path`) steps 9–10 | `src/owner/label_path.rs:559` |
| The global tracker moves on every label, the eligible tracker only on eligible ones | (`tab:weighting:trackers`) | `src/owner/label_path.rs:470` |
| The statistics are updated from the raw vector, not the standardised one | (`alg:standardisation:label-time-procedure`) step 5 | `src/owner/label_path.rs:869` |
| The drift residual is taken against the assessment-time prediction | (`alg:monitoring:drift-cusums`) | `src/owner/label_path.rs:883` |
| No Companion state is read or written anywhere on the Core label path | (`inv:companion:boundary`) | `src/owner/label_path.rs:719`, by absence |
| The path holds no handle outside itself and calls nothing back | (`dec:ownership:feed-forward`) | the whole file |
| Axis registration extends the models by exactly the specified width | (`alg:registry:axis-registration`) step 1 | `src/owner/lifecycle.rs:417` |
| The Sentinel slot is the occupancy indicator plus the extraction block | (`def:extraction:slot`) | `src/owner/lifecycle.rs:277` |
| The anchor is never extended or marginalised by any lifecycle event | (`def:risk:model-triple`) | `src/owner/lifecycle.rs:299` |
| Deregistration marginalises by the regularised Schur complement, with the diagonal block as a reported fallback rather than an error | (`alg:gaussian:regularised-schur`) | `src/owner/lifecycle.rs:365` |

Eleven pipeline configuration parameters were additionally checked one by one against their tabulated defaults and pass through the wiring unchanged (`src/owner/label_path.rs:219`). The `Default` implementation beside that wiring is a separate surface and is not clean; it is recorded below.

**Entry (Extension appends where the rebuild does not)** · `entry:assayer:wl-owner-layout-misalignment`

**Locus** every `extend_all_full_dim` call — `src/owner/lifecycle.rs:299`, `:607`, `:784` — taken together with the unconditional canonical rebuild at `src/owner/lifecycle.rs:947`. No notice stands here, and the item is new.

**Disposition** divergent-code-wrong against (`inv:dimension:version-consistency`), which requires that at every published version the map, the model parameters and the standardisation statistics share one dimension *and one index semantics*, and which argues the invariant is unconditional because a vector standardised under one index semantics and scored under another is meaningless position by position rather than approximately right.

Extension appends at the end; the rebuild lays the map out in the canonical block order — bias, aggregate, identity per-dimension, identity cross-dimension, signals, Sentinel slots (`src/feature/dimension_map.rs:285`). The two agree only when registrations happen to arrive in that order. Register one Sentinel and then one identity dimension and they do not: the Sentinel's occupancy indicator and its extraction positions are *learned* at the coordinates immediately after the aggregate block, and from the moment of the second rebuild the map tells every reader that those coordinates are the identity blocks and that the Sentinel lives some sixteen positions higher. Both totals agree, which is the whole difficulty — the two guards that stand here compare the map's width against the model dimensions and pass (`src/owner/lifecycle.rs:978`). They check the width and never the layout, which is why this has survived. No translation layer exists anywhere in the workspace.

The corpus is under some strain at this point, and the strain does not rescue the code. It asserts append-at-end allocation, a canonical block order, and no translation layer, and the three are jointly satisfiable only under an ordering constraint nothing states. Under (`conv:assayer:audit-measure`) that is not a spec-stale verdict: the invariant is unconditional, and the code is the party that both appends and rebuilds canonically.

**Severity** high, and by recomputation the most consequential thing this wave found. It corrupts every learned weight of every full-dimension model, silently, from the first out-of-canonical-order registration onward — the parameters, not a diagnostic. The trigger is ordinary rather than exotic: registering an identity dimension after any Sentinel does it, through the public API, in the order a host would naturally use.

**What conformance would mean** either the rebuild returns an index permutation applied to the parameters and the standardisation vectors, or extension inserts at the block boundary the map will assign rather than at the end; and whichever is taken, the rebuild's post-condition is strengthened from a width comparison to a block-boundary comparison against the pre-event map.

**Test coverage** none, and one test sits directly on the failing ordering without an oracle: it submits the Sentinel and then the identity dimension and asserts only that the three standardisation vectors have equal length, which the resize guarantees unconditionally. Every test that does pin block positions registers the identity dimension first — in canonical order — and so passes.

**Repaired** in wave seven at `3dfdb31c`, as the permutation (`rem:assayer:feature-extension-order`) settled. `PositionKey` names a coordinate by what it means rather than where it sits, at the finest granularity stable under an extension; two maps enumerate their positions and the permutation is the lookup between them. It is applied to the operational and sister models, every outcome-axis model, and the three standardisation vectors — the whole set the invariant binds to one index semantics — and is the identity whenever the arriving order was already canonical. The refusal is the strengthened post-condition: it compares the block layout rather than the total.

Three things the repair settled are worth carrying. The first is that the construction generalises past the entry's own framing: the handlers leave the vector in append-and-compact order, so a surviving coordinate's post-event position is its rank among the pre-event keys the rebuild still issues, which is one construction for extension, marginalisation, and both at once. The first cut guarded against a shrinking vector instead, and every deregistration took the refusal branch — whose debug assertion did not fail a test but *hung* one, the submission's completion never crossing the channel; the refusal is now a warning that leaves the parameters where the handlers left them. The second was recorded as a case that still refuses rather than rearranges — deregistering a spatial outcome axis renumbers every later axis's outcome-memory pair, and the pair is keyed by that number — and it is corrected below: it does not refuse, and the reason it was thought to was never measured. The third is that the competitive-cell entry pushed its standardisation classes indicator-first where the rebuild lays the interaction block ahead of the indicators — a latent ordering divergence the permutation surfaced, corrected with it.

The second of those three is measured at `bc072780` and was wrong twice over. Its stated cause has not held since `9560d68e`: the map does record which axes are spatial and where each one's pair sits, in `slot_axis_offsets`. And its stated effect never held. A deployment of two Sentinels, one identity dimension and three spatial axes was given a distinguishable value at every coordinate of the posterior mean, the covariance diagonal and both standardisation vectors — the covariance planted diagonal, so the Schur correction is identically zero and the kept block a marginalisation returns is the kept block it was handed, leaving the rearrangement as the only thing the reading can be measuring — and then the middle axis was retired through the real lifecycle submission. The permutation is not refused. It is the *identity*, and every surviving axis's pair, in every Sentinel slot, carries that axis's own planted values across all five channels the invariant binds, the precision matrix included; the retired axis's values appear nowhere in the vector. The identity block's per-axis triple, keyed by the axis rather than by a rank, agrees as the control beside it. The retirement of the first-ranked axis and of the last-ranked axis were measured the same way and agree.

The cancellation is exact for a reason rather than by coincidence, and the reason is stated where the construction lives (`src/feature/permutation.rs:265`). The removal takes the retired axis's own pair out of every slot and the compaction closes the gap; the key filter drops the last-ranked pair key instead, wherever the retirement happened. Those are different positions, so the construction's stated assumption is false coordinate for coordinate. What holds is weaker: both drops remove the same count from one contiguous run whose positions are ordered by rank on both sides, the compacted run holds the surviving axes' pairs in the order the authoritative model map keeps them — order-preserving since (`entry:assayer:wl-owner-dereg-scan`) — and the rebuilt map gives tail rank `r` to the `r`-th axis of that same order. One ordering read twice. The distance between the two drops does not enter the argument, which is why the first-ranked retirement, where they fall at opposite ends of the tail, behaves as the middle one does.

The argument's premises are its boundaries: the pair run must be the slot's only rank-keyed run, the model map's removal must stay order-preserving, and the removal must be planned against one entry layout. The third premise was originally written as one retirement per submission, which is where the measurement found something new (`entry:assayer:wl-owner-dereg-scan`); the applier now supplies it structurally rather than by restricting what a submission may carry. A submission's events are gathered into maximal chains of operations that are order-free with respect to one another, and a chain's removals all name their positions against the layout the previous publication left, so the entry layout a chain plans against is one layout for every operation in it whether the chain carries one retirement or several (`src/owner/lifecycle.rs:196`).

The checkpoint generation moves to eleven at `3ff8e370`, once for the whole wave: the payload stores the model parameters and the standardisation vectors indexed against the map, a predecessor wrote them in the order the extension appended, this build reads them in the order the rebuild lays out, and nothing in the bytes distinguishes the two.

**Entry (Axis deregistration finds its features by searching for a class)** · `entry:assayer:wl-owner-dereg-scan`

**Locus** `src/owner/lifecycle.rs:524`.

**Disposition** divergent-code-wrong against (`alg:registry:axis-deregistration`) step 1, which requires removing *the axis's* own features — its triple in each registered identity dimension and its outcome-memory pair per Sentinel. The identity half is done by address, from the map, and is one of the conformant rows above. The Sentinel half is done by search: the loop walks the class vector from the end and takes the first positions whose class is one of the two outcome-axis classes. Nothing in that predicate mentions the axis being deregistered.

Register some Sentinels, register axis A, register axis B, then deregister A. A's positions were appended before B's, so the reverse scan collects B's and stops. Every full-dimension model is then marginalised on B's coordinates while A's remain, and the rebuild — which does not consult the model — publishes a map saying A is gone and B is present. The parameters say the opposite. The same predicate misfires a second way: identity-dimension registration pushes those same two classes (`src/owner/lifecycle.rs:620`), so where a dimension was registered after the axis, the scan can consume identity positions before it reaches any Sentinel one.

**Severity** high. It takes the Schur complement on the wrong partition, which destroys the wrong learned parameters silently and unrecoverably. It is bounded to deployments that retire an axis while another axis or a later identity dimension is live, which the corpus treats as ordinary rather than exceptional.

**What conformance would mean** the per-Sentinel positions are addressed from the map, as the identity ones already are, and the class scan is deleted.

**Test coverage** none. The one deregistration test registers a single axis, so the scan cannot pick the wrong one, and asserts only that the model shrank — not that it shrank on the right coordinates. It also reaches its precondition by writing the map's Sentinel slots by hand, which is the test conceding that the map and the model disagree at that point.

**Repaired** in wave seven at `35ea0ac3`. The per-Sentinel positions are addressed from the map — the slot's outcome-memory offset advanced by the axis's own rank among the spatial axes — and the class scan is deleted. The repair found a second defect at the same locus and took it: the axis model was removed with a swap that moved the last axis into the removed slot, silently renumbering every surviving axis's rank, which is what places both its identity triple and its outcome-memory pair. The removal is now order-preserving.

**Repaired** at `d91e7a9c`, closing the case found at `f4b716ff`: two spatial axes retired in one submission addressed a layout that was already gone. The repair above addressed the per-Sentinel positions from `working.dimension_map`, and that map was refreshed by the rebuild, which ran once after all of a submission's events rather than after each one. The axis's rank, by contrast, was read from the authoritative model map, which every handler updates as it runs. A submission carrying two retirements therefore mixed the two: the second handler took a fresh rank into stale slot positions, and addressed coordinates in the layout the first retirement had already compacted away. Measured on two Sentinels, one identity dimension and three spatial axes, retiring the first two in one submission: the removal was offered as `[27, 28, 29, 101, 102, 168, 169]` against a model the first retirement had already narrowed to 168 coordinates, and the last index was past its end. The debug assertion on the marginalisation's own precondition caught it (`src/model/bayesian.rs:444`), so the failure was loud where assertions are compiled in and a read past the end of the kept partition where they are not. It never reached the layout permutation: the removal was refused before any rearrangement was computed.

The record left the choice of repair open, between three: track the compaction the handler performs, rebuild the map per event at the cost the batch contract exists to avoid, or narrow the contract to one spatial-axis retirement per submission and enforce it at the surface. What ships is a fourth, and it makes the class of defect structurally unreachable rather than closing this instance of it. The submission's ordered queue is gathered into maximal chains of operations that are order-free with respect to one another, and each chain is applied as ONE plan computed entirely against the layout the previous publication left: every operation in it names its positions against that one entry layout, their union is marginalised in a single Schur complement, the standardisation vectors are compacted once over the same union, the map is rebuilt once and one snapshot is published (`src/owner/lifecycle.rs:460`). Inside a chain there is no intermediate state for a map read to be stale against, because the state changes once, at the end, for all of them; and an operation that is not order-free seals the chain and begins from a freshly published layout, which is the state a submission carrying it alone would have handed it.

The commutation predicate is argued where it is constructed and is deliberately conservative — an unproven pair breaks the chain, and breaking is never wrong (`src/owner/lifecycle.rs:167`). Removals commute with removals: each addresses its positions by its own entity's identifier in the entry map, a coordinate two of them both name is removed once under either order, and Gaussian marginalisation composes, so the union in one Schur complement leaves the survivors where two sequential complements would have (`thm:gaussian:marginalisation`). The one exception is two identity-dimension removals, whose index set is not a function of the departing entity alone: the cross-dimension aggregate leaves with the *last* dimension, and "last" counts what is still standing. Registrations are order-free with nothing, themselves included, because their width is read from live entity state and their coordinates land where the append put them — append order is rank order is layout. A registration is therefore a chain of one, and that is what gives a same-identifier registration and retirement in one submission a defined behaviour without a special case: the registration publishes, and the retirement removes what it published.

The handler stopped recomputing a rank at all. Both halves of an axis removal are now addressed from the entry map by the axis's own identifier — the identity triple at `identity_axis_offset`, the outcome-memory pair at the `slot_axis_offset` the slot-placement repair recorded (`entry:assayer:wl-feature-frozen-slot-truncation`). The rank was the one quantity in the handler that moved as a submission ran, and removing it makes the two halves of a removal one layout by construction.

**What changed for consumers** is publication count, and it is the batch contract's own visible half that is restated rather than abandoned (`dec:construction:compound-batch`). A submission whose events split into k chains publishes k snapshots and advances the version counter k times before signalling completion once. The map a chain boundary exposes is not an accidental intermediate: it is a complete canonical layout for a prefix of the submission, and it is precisely the map that would have stood had the submission been split at that boundary. What a per-event publish would have exposed — a layout that depends on an ordering the events themselves do not have — is exactly what a within-chain publish would expose, and no chain publishes within itself. The event-atomicity the publication invariant states is untouched: an event's effect appears whole in the snapshot of the chain it belonged to (`inv:guarantee:lifecycle-publication`).

**Diagnostics** follow the same reading. One chain is one Schur computation over one union of positions, and its error partition is a property of that computation; splitting it back out per index set would mean attributing a correction that was never computed per index set. Each of a chain's operations therefore receives the chain's single aggregate and names the chain it belonged to, and the health channel emits one completion event per chain rather than one per result, which is what keeps the lifetime ledger counting computations rather than operations (`entry:health:marginalise-event`).

**Coverage** the test that pinned the refusal is now the green witness of the repair: two spatial axes retired in one submission land in one chain and one marginalisation, the surviving axis keeps its own memory in every Sentinel slot across all five channels the invariant binds, and neither retired axis's memory is left anywhere in the vector (`claim:lifespan:two-spatial-axes-retired-in-one-submission-apply-as-one-chain-and-the-survivor-keeps-its-own-memory`). The edge the design creates has its own — retire, register, retire publishes three snapshots at three consecutive versions and the survivor still reads its own memory at the end (`claim:lifespan:a-submission-that-breaks-its-chain-publishes-once-per-chain-and-every-survivor-keeps-its-own-memory`). The predicate itself is pinned at the gatherer (`claim:lifespan:consecutive-removals-of-different-entities-gather-into-a-single-order-free-chain`), (`claim:lifespan:a-registration-seals-the-open-chain-and-forms-a-chain-of-one`), (`claim:lifespan:two-identity-dimension-removals-are-never-gathered-into-one-chain`), and the diagnostics reading at (`claim:lifespan:the-operations-of-one-chain-each-receive-that-chains-single-marginalisation-aggregate`).

**The sweep the repair licensed.** Every consumer that read positional geometry from `working.dimension_map` while an application was in flight carried the same latent staleness, and the chain design cures each of them at the same place rather than one at a time. Four were live defects on a multi-event submission before it. The standardisation compaction computed its kept set against the current vector length while its removed indices came from the un-shortened map, so a second removal in a submission compacted a vector the first had already shortened; there is now one compaction per chain, over the entry width the positions were addressed in. The hibernation archive read the departing block out of parameters a previous removal in the same submission had already compacted, so it stored another entity's coordinates under the departing entity's identifier; the chain marginalises once, after all planning, so every archive a chain takes is read off the entry parameters. Sentinel registration counted the standing population and completed named pairs from a map that did not yet hold a Sentinel registered earlier in the same submission, and axis registration counted Sentinels and identity dimensions the same way; both are now chains of one and read a freshly published map. Identity-dimension registration added the cross-dimension aggregate block whenever the map held no dimensions, so two registered together each added one and the rebuild's width check failed; sealed apart, the second sees the first. The three positional readers outside the applier — the label path's slot and identity-block reads and the bootstrap's slot lookup — run on the same single steward thread and never overlap an application, and are unaffected.

**Entry (A revert rebuilds every model from default configuration)** · `entry:assayer:wl-owner-revert-defaults`

**Locus** `src/owner/label_path.rs:1062`, inside the state revert.

**Disposition** divergent-code-wrong against (`tab:config:risk-model`), whose rows the configuration chapter asserts ship as tabulated — meaning host-settable rather than merely present — and against (`req:host:construction`), which fixes that the Core is built from the host's declared model parameters.

The revert is reached from the two integrity guards. It then constructs a default model configuration and a default regularisation configuration and hands both to the working-copy rebuild, which uses the prior precision to rebuild every model and passes the regularisation to each. Both substituted values are wrong for any host that configured anything but the default, and the pipeline's own correctly-threaded regularisation is sitting unused in the same function's arguments. The recomputation interval beside them *is* passed correctly, which is what makes the other two read as an oversight rather than a decision.

**Severity** high. Not the frequency — a guard firing is rare — but that the substitution is silent, permanent for the process, and lands on the parameters governing every subsequent update. A deployment that declared a prior precision of one half is running at a tenth afterwards, so every posterior update is scaled against a prior the deployment never chose, and nothing in the health surface says so.

**What conformance would mean** the two constructions become the configured values, the pipeline threading a model configuration through as it already threads the recomputation interval.

**Test coverage** none, and none is reachable: no test in the workspace drives either guard's positive branch at all, so nothing observes the substitution. The one test named for detecting the condition concedes in its own comment that it cannot inject one, and exercises only the negative case.

**Entry (The class-assignment function the corpus requires was never written)** · `entry:assayer:wl-owner-absent-class-assignment`

**Locus** `src/owner/lifecycle.rs:137`, the resize fallback. A labelled notice stood at it, and the wave-seven class derivation took it: the fallback is gone and every class is recomputed from the rebuilt map.

**Disposition** divergent-code-wrong against (`req:standardisation:class-assignment`), which requires the class at each position to be derived from three authorities — the map assigns the block and the slot, the fixed extraction layout the class within a slot, the signal schema the signal block — and which states the point of deriving rather than storing: a position cannot carry a stale class, because the class is recomputed wherever the map is. The code stores classes in a vector maintained by appends and, when that vector is short, fills the gap with the generic standardising class, chosen by the resize call from no authority at all.

The notice names the function that would discharge the requirement. **That function does not exist.** Three references to it stand in the tree — at this locus, at `src/feature/standardisation.rs:369`, and at `src/owner/thread.rs:153` — and all three are comments. So the corpus's derived class rule is unimplemented package-wide rather than mis-wired at one site, and three separate notices prescribe a remedy that was never built. It is divergent rather than unspecified because the requirement is written and binding; what is unspecified is only which repair is taken.

**Severity** medium. The generic class carries the neutral moments, so an affected position is standardised as though already standardised, and the effect decays over about one standardisation half-life as the running statistics replace the priors. It is medium rather than low because it is the mechanism that converts the two high-severity entries above from loud failures into quiet ones: the length invariant the package does check is restored before anything can notice.

**What conformance would mean** the class vector is recomputed from the rebuilt map after every lifecycle submission rather than resized — which is that function existing and being called.

**Test coverage** none. The one test here asserts the three vectors are the same length, which the resize guarantees whatever class the fallback picks.

**Entry (The calibration refit answers to two of its five specified triggers)** · `entry:assayer:wl-owner-refit-triggers`

**Locus** `src/owner/label_path.rs:783`, the trigger expression, together with `src/owner/lifecycle.rs:312`, the single flag-setting call. Two notices bear: (`todo:code:mark-early-refit-pending-after-sentinel`) at `src/owner/lifecycle.rs:177`.

**Disposition** divergent-code-wrong, and the item is one defect seen from two ends, which is why it is recorded once. From the label path: of the five triggers (`tab:platt:refit-cadence`) fixes, the sole production call site implements the periodic one and anchor reactivation, and the lifecycle, drift and manual triggers have no caller anywhere in the workspace. From the lifecycle side: (`dec:calibration:unconditional-refit`) asserts that any registration or deregistration of a Sentinel, an outcome axis or an identity dimension sets the early-refit flag, naming six event kinds and citing this file; the flag is set by one of the six. The two censuses agree on the mechanism — the flag-setting call has exactly one production call site, and none of the five silent handlers so much as takes the tracker as a parameter.

The record's *unconditional* half holds wherever the flag is set, and the fossil the supersession register warns of is genuinely absent: the call sits inside no conditional, and the refit predicate carries no third term. That negative result is recorded because the register asked for it.

The drift trigger is the pointed one, because the coupling exists in the reverse direction only. A large calibration shift resets the drift accumulators; a drift reset does not refit. The corpus specifies the two as a mutual pair and the package ships a one-way edge, and the missing direction is the one that keeps calibration honest after the monitor has noticed the scores moved.

**Severity** medium. Nothing computed is wrong; what is lost is that calibration no longer answers the events the table says change the score distribution without warning. After a registration that moves the dimension, the score-to-probability mapping stays on the periodic cadence, so probabilities a host reads as frequencies are miscalibrated for up to that many labels. They remain correctly ordered throughout, which is what keeps this below the entries that corrupt parameters.

**What conformance would mean** the flag is set once in the event dispatch on the six matching variants — the tracker is already in that function's scope, so no handler signature need change — the drift reset sets it too, and a host-facing recalibration entry point exists.

**Test coverage** covered for the one case that works and absent for the rest: one test asserts the flag goes from clear to set on Sentinel registration, and none asserts it on any deregistration or any axis event.

**Table (The cluster's remaining divergences)** · `tab:assayer:owner-remaining`

Eight further divergences were found and are recorded compactly, each having been checked against the cited authority in the same way as the entries above. Four were already labelled notices.

| Divergence | Authority | Locus | Severity |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Every drift accumulator is fed one blended residual, and axis accumulators move on labels that reported no axis | (`alg:monitoring:drift-cusums`) | `src/owner/label_path.rs:883` | medium |
| The calibration record ships five of the seven specified fields, the absent uncertainty being the input empirical coverage needs | (`constr:platt:buffer`) | `src/owner/label_path.rs:766` | medium |
| The drift configuration is unreachable — the wiring ignores its source and the host configuration has no field | (`tab:config:monitoring`) | `src/owner/label_path.rs:244` | medium |
| A Sentinel slot's extraction positions are all given the generic class | (`req:standardisation:class-assignment`) | `src/owner/lifecycle.rs:301` | medium |
| A calibration shift resets every accumulator, not the affected regime's | (`tab:monitoring:drift-resets`) | `src/owner/label_path.rs:837` | low |
| The publication interval is not a knob and the path publishes unconditionally | (`req:publication:interval`) | `src/owner/label_path.rs:1012` | low |
| The identity outcome rate is a constant where the corpus makes it a parameter | (`tab:config:identity`) | `src/owner/label_path.rs:232` | low |
| Competitive cell entry extends at a literal prior where its three siblings thread the configured one | (`thm:gaussian:extension`) | `src/owner/lifecycle.rs:782` | low |
| The pipeline's own default surface ships one forgetting rate at a fifth of its tabulated horizon | (`tab:config:risk-model`) | `src/owner/label_path.rs:193` | low |
| Two cross-dimension positions carry the wrong class | (`req:standardisation:class-assignment`) | `src/owner/lifecycle.rs:627` | low |

The last two need their severity argued rather than asserted, and in both cases the argument lowers it. The default-surface rate is never constructed on any production path — the only production construction is the wiring from the host configuration — so no deployment computes a wrong number through it; it is registered at all because a test pins it, which is recorded below. The literal prior is numerically identical to the shipped default, so the divergence is semantic and bites only a host that retunes the prior, at which point every competitive indicator enters at a precision the rest of the model does not share.

**Entry (A checkpoint processes observations while commands are still queued)** · `entry:assayer:wl-owner-checkpoint-preemption`

**Locus** `src/owner/thread.rs:326`, the label drain inside the checkpoint command handler.

**Disposition** divergent-code-wrong against (`dec:concurrency:channel-preemption`), which states that the command channel drains completely before any observation is processed and says of the preemption that it is unconditional — not a priority hint, and not a heuristic about which queue looks busier. The steward's own loop honours that: it drains commands to empty, then waits on a biased selection, which is precisely the discipline the collapsed-loop register kept and not the unbiased one it discarded (`reg:concurrency:collapsed-loop`). The exception is inside the loop rather than in it. Handling a checkpoint drains every pending label before writing, and a second command sitting behind the checkpoint waits through that drain.

**The observable sequence.** Submit a checkpoint, then a registration, then labels. The command channel holds the checkpoint and the registration; the checkpoint is handled first and drains the labels that have arrived, including labels submitted after the registration. Those labels are processed against the pre-registration dimension, and the registration is applied afterwards — which is the lifecycle-publication guarantee (`inv:guarantee:lifecycle-publication`) failing in the one direction the preemption exists to prevent, a registration overtaken by the labels that follow it.

**Severity** medium. It requires a checkpoint concurrent with a registration, so it is not the common path, and the labels affected are dropped from the update rather than misapplied — the dimension guard on the label path declines an update whose vector does not match the model width, silently. What is lost is a stated guarantee and a handful of labels, not a corrupted parameter.

**What conformance would mean** the checkpoint's flush drains labels only after the command channel is empty, or the corpus records the flush barrier as a stated exception to the preemption and says what it costs.

**Test coverage** none; the flush barrier is tested for the property it is for — that a checkpoint reflects every label sent before it — and not for what it does to a command queued behind it.

**Entry (A partial checkpoint truncates a journal that holds what it omits)** · `entry:assayer:wl-owner-checkpoint-truncation`

**Locus** `src/owner/thread.rs:348` for the checkpoint's contents and `src/owner/thread.rs:363` for the truncation. Both stood disclosed by notices — the identity-coordination one at the contents, the sequence-aware-truncation one at the call — and the wave-five repair took both with the defects they disclosed.

**Disposition** divergent-code-wrong against (`dec:durability:checkpoint-journal`), which fixes what survives a restart as two things — a periodic *whole-state* checkpoint and a journal of the labels in flight since it. The checkpoint is not whole-state: the Ledger and identity payloads are passed as empty collections, and the notice above them concedes in terms that this makes the checkpoint only partial. The truncation is then taken over the whole journal file rather than up to the sequence the checkpoint reached, and the notice beside it asks for exactly that repair, naming the coordination with concurrent appends that is missing.

The two combine into a hole neither has alone, and the combination is the finding. A label appended to the journal after the checkpoint's state was captured is not in the checkpoint, because the capture preceded it; it is not in the journal either, because the truncation that follows removes the whole file. So a label the host was told had been accepted survives in neither artefact, and (`cor:durability:replay-exactness`) — which the record derives as a consequence rather than asserts — has nothing to stand on for that label.

**Severity** high. It is silent, unrecoverable data loss across a restart, on the path whose entire purpose is that a restart loses nothing but assessments in flight. That the checkpoint also omits the Ledger and identity state compounds it: what does survive is a model whose supporting state is reconstructed from nothing.

**What conformance would mean** the truncation is taken at the sequence the checkpoint recorded and under the same lock the append path takes, and the payload carries the Ledger and identity state the record calls whole.

**Test coverage** none of the loss. The checkpoint path is tested for its acknowledgement — that a requester learns whether the write succeeded — and nothing exercises a label appended concurrently with a truncation. **This entry is extended rather than corrected by the persistence tranche** (`rem:assayer:persistence-cross-cites`), which owns the truncation site and adds three facts. The defect stood disclosed at its implementation as well as at its call site, under a third notice the repair took with the whole-file truncation it disclosed. The truncation takes no lock and consults no sequence, so there is no partial coordination to build on. And its consequence compounds into a second and larger loss through the sequence numbering, registered at (`entry:assayer:wl-persistence-seq-restart`), with which it should be scheduled.

**Sequencing** child of (`entry:assayer:wl-persistence-partial-payload`), whose payload this entry's empty vectors are the write-site half of, and paired with (`entry:assayer:wl-persistence-seq-restart`), whose sequence restart the truncation's loss compounds into. The pairing is not a convenience: repairing the truncation without the numbering leaves the replay filter measuring against a mark the writer has already reused, and repairing the numbering without the truncation leaves a journal truncated below what the checkpoint omits. Neither half is safe alone.

**Repaired** in wave five, both halves. The write site captures at `cb0271d0`: the checkpoint carries the real Sentinel ledgers and identity graph/cell payloads, coordinated with identity maintenance through the prepare-and-publish command that was written for the purpose and called from nowhere. The truncation lands at `529cc01d`: the journal truncates at `last_processed_label_seq` through the same mutex the append path holds, retaining every entry above the mark, and the whole-file function is deleted with the defect it disclosed. The falsifier holds three absorbed and two unabsorbed entries — before, the whole file emptied and the two unabsorbed entries were in neither artefact; after, they survive in order and the writer numbers the next append above them. All three notices are taken.

**Entry (A completed batch initialisation is discarded if the channel is full)** · `entry:assayer:wl-owner-batch-init-drop`

**Locus** `src/lib.rs:1026`, and the skipped observation above it at `src/lib.rs:1020`. The locus lies outside both clusters this wave audits; it is recorded here because it is the model owner's command channel and was reached by this tranche's census, and it is flagged for the tranche that will own `src/`.

**Disposition** divergent-code-wrong on two counts, and the second is the serious one. The accumulation skips an observation whenever the lock is contended, where (`alg:standardisation:batch-initialisation`) step 1 says each raw assessment-time vector is accumulated — a universal quantifier the code answers with a sample. That alone makes the resulting statistics depend on scheduling, so two runs over identical traffic produce different standardisation.

Step 3 of the same algorithm applies the empirical mean and variance through the model owner's command channel. The code sends without blocking and discards the result, then clears the accumulator and marks the initialisation inactive regardless of whether the send succeeded. So a full channel at that instant discards the whole batch initialisation permanently: the statistics are gone, the accumulator is released, nothing retries, and no counter records it. That is also the flat contrary of (`dec:concurrency:no-silent-drop`), which says overflow on either channel is an error returned to the caller and that no item is discarded without the submitter learning.

**Severity** high for the discard and medium for the skip. The discard leaves every position standardised against its class prior for the life of the process, where the algorithm's whole purpose is that the priors are replaced wholesale by empirical statistics after the first hundred assessments — and it leaves no trace a host could read.

**What conformance would mean** the completion is delivered rather than attempted, or a failed send leaves the accumulator intact so the next completion retries; and the observation is taken under a lock that waits, or the corpus records that the sample is best-effort and says what that costs.

**Test coverage** none on either path. No test saturates the command channel at a completion boundary, and none observes a skipped accumulation.

**Repaired** in wave four at `7a4bd65a`, both counts, each by an option the conformance line offered. The discard closes by retention — a failed send leaves the accumulator intact and the next completion retries — with the saturating falsifier recorded on the sibling entry (`entry:assayer:wl-batch-init-discard`). The skip closes the second way: the corpus now records that step 1's sample is best-effort under contention and what that costs, a lock that waits being refused by the non-blocking guarantee; the same added passage states why the release, unlike the observation, is not exempt from the no-silent-drop decision.

**Remark (The cadence the Ledger sweep is missing already runs)** · `rem:assayer:owner-no-cadence`

The ledger tranche found the collection sweep implemented and never invoked (`entry:assayer:wl-ledger-sweep-unscheduled`), and said conformance would mean a maintenance loop invoking it per Sentinel on the configured cadence. This cluster is where such a loop would be looked for and does not hold one: the steward thread is a drain over two channels, and it schedules nothing periodic at all — the only thing in it that mentioned maintenance was a notice about coordinating with the identity side, since taken by wave five's checkpoint coordination.

The loop exists elsewhere. The package's one production maintenance thread belongs to the identity cluster, is spawned during construction (`src/lib.rs:500`), and is genuinely periodic: it waits on its command channel with a timeout and carries both a short cycle interval and a longer decay interval (`src/identity/maintenance_loop.rs:299`).

That sharpens the ledger entry rather than adding to it, and it moves the entry away from the reading its own wording invites. The obstacle to collecting the Ledger is not that the package lacks a cadence to hang the sweep on; a cadence exists, runs in every deployment, and ticks in the same process. What is missing is the call — which makes that entry a smaller repair than it looks and places it in a cluster neither it nor this tranche owns.

**Remark (What the census turned up in this cluster)** · `rem:assayer:owner-census`

The ledger tranche's closing advice was to ask who invokes a function rather than what it does, and this cluster answers the question in a shape that tranche did not meet. There is no uncalled machinery here: every lifecycle handler and every label helper is reached from the one production entry point, and that negative result is the census's main finding. What the census turned up instead is a *trigger* with one caller for six specified events — recorded above at (`entry:assayer:wl-owner-refit-triggers`) — which is the same blind spot seen from the other side. A function nothing calls and a function called on one of the six occasions the corpus names are both invisible to a reading of the function itself, and only an exhaustive count of call sites distinguishes either from working code.

Three narrower results are worth carrying. This cluster is the sole production reach of four functions in the risk cluster, two of which carry high-severity divergences, so the odds-ratio weight and the inverted eligibility rows reach a deployment through a handful of lines here and nowhere else — which is where a repair to either becomes observable. The Ledger's entire production write path is one line of the label path, which pairs with the ledger tranche's finding that its read path never leaves the root. And the label path carries a module-wide suppression of the compiler's dead-code warning, which is exactly the suppression that would hide an uncalled function in the file from the build; the census was done by search for that reason rather than from warnings, and a cluster that suppresses the warning is a cluster where the census question cannot be delegated to the compiler.

**Remark (The cluster's central decision is covered twice and observed never)** · `rem:assayer:owner-hollow-preemption`

The test-pin question returns a sharper result here than it did for the ledger, and it explains how (`entry:assayer:wl-owner-checkpoint-preemption`) survived. The command-before-observation discipline is the thing this cluster exists to get right — the module's own prose calls the ordering the whole point — and two tests are named for it. Neither observes it.

The first queues five labels, sends a lifecycle command, and asserts that the lifecycle completes. Its body carries a comment explaining that it uses an empty event list so as not to change the model's dimensions, because real lifecycle events would make the pre-queued labels stale — so the test deliberately removes the condition the preemption exists to protect, and its authored claim describes the scenario the body avoids. The second binds its label sender to a discarded name and sends no label at all, then asserts that three lifecycle completions arrive; a test named for draining commands before any waiting label is touched runs with nothing waiting.

This is the no-oracle class of (`entry:assayer:wl-weak-tests`) standing over a cluster's central guarantee rather than over a peripheral one, and it is the reason a violation could be added to the checkpoint handler without anything going red. Two further pins in this cluster defend divergences outright: a defaults test asserts the shared tracker rate the founding worklist says should not exist and asserts a forgetting rate at a fifth of its tabulated horizon, and a wiring test whose stated purpose is to rule out silently dropped configuration fields checks fourteen fields while skipping exactly the three that are broken. The second is the sharper, because it is a test built to catch this class of defect standing over three live instances of it.

## Third tranche: the risk surface · `sec:assayer:audit-tranche-risk`

The cluster is `src/risk/` — the blend that combines the model outputs, the calibration state, and the Companion challenge estimate — taken against the calibration and challenge records and the risk and companion chapters of the specification (`tab:assayer:audit-scope-map`).

The cluster is unusual in one respect that shapes every disposition in it, and the governing record states it before the audit could: the challenge record's own first caveat says that every decision it makes is implemented and that nothing calls it (`cav:challenge:unwired`). A tranche reading this cluster is therefore reading a shipped library surface rather than a running path for about a third of its extent, and the who-calls-this question that the ledger tranche recommends (`reg:assayer:audit-tranches`) returns an answer the corpus has already conceded. Where that is so, the finding is recorded as conformant with a record that admits the gap, and never as a fresh divergence.

**Table (What the risk cluster conforms to)** · `tab:assayer:risk-conformant`

Forty-six rows were checked across the cluster and hold. The Companion rows are recorded first, because the cluster's most conspicuous property — that nothing calls it — is a requirement met rather than a defect found.

| Checked | Authority | Evidence |
| --- | --- | --- |
| No Core structure holds Companion state, and no Core path routes a label to it | (`inv:companion:boundary`) | `src/risk/challenge.rs`, by absence |
| An estimate query always returns the prior, an override, or a posterior, with no error channel | (`dec:challenge:infallible-estimation`) | `src/risk/challenge.rs:300` |
| An override leaves the underlying estimate accumulating beneath it | (`dec:challenge:override-accumulates`) | `src/risk/challenge.rs:262` |
| Injected evidence enters the same counts as observed evidence, and is capped | (`dec:challenge:capped-injection`) | `src/risk/challenge.rs:236` |
| Decay blends toward the prior rather than scaling the counts down | (`rem:challenge:decay-by-blending`) | `src/risk/challenge.rs:221` |
| The class-rate tracker moves toward each label's own indicator and is held off both endpoints | (`tab:weighting:trackers`) | `src/risk/challenge.rs:582` |
| The refit takes the buffer and the configuration and mutates nothing it read | (`dec:calibration:pure-refit`) | `src/risk/calibration.rs:341` |
| Fitting is a per-regime search on a transformed parameter | (`dec:calibration:per-regime-search`) | `src/risk/calibration.rs:483` |
| Below the minimum sample count no refit happens at all | (`req:platt:minimum-samples`) | `src/risk/calibration.rs:219` |
| A large calibration shift resets the drift accumulators rather than adjusting them | (`dec:calibration:drift-reset`) | `src/risk/calibration.rs:112` |
| The regime transition function is defined once and shared by the fit and the blend | (`dec:calibration:shared-transition`) | `src/numerics.rs`, used at `src/risk/calibration.rs:437` |
| The recency discount is anchored at the newest entry so the weights stay bounded | (`alg:platt:fitting`) | `src/risk/calibration.rs:435` |
| The buffer is bounded and evicts from the front | (`constr:platt:buffer`) | `src/risk/calibration.rs` |
| Eleven calibration defaults ship exactly as tabulated, recomputed one by one | (`tab:config:calibration`) | `src/risk/calibration.rs` |

**Remark (Three registered items re-verified, and each is worse than registered)** · `rem:assayer:risk-reverification`

The three founding entries whose locus is this cluster all survive, none is refuted, and in each case the audit found the registered account understated the defect. They are not restated as new entries; what follows is what changed.

The importance weight (`entry:assayer:wl-importance-weights`) stands at its registered locus, and the recomputation was performed twice independently and agreed. At the corpus's tabulated class rate the specified pair stands in a ratio of about nine and the shipped pair in eighty-one, which is that ratio squared; the squaring holds at every rate rather than at the tabulated one, the shipped ratio being the specified ratio multiplied by the odds. The registered entry says the balance property is lost, and the sharper statement is that it is *inverted*: at the tabulated rate the corpus gives each class half the gradient and the shipped form gives the rare class nine tenths of it. The ceiling binds at about twice the specified rate, as registered. That entry's coverage line has been corrected in place, above, because it was wrong.

The eligibility predicate (`entry:assayer:wl-eligibility-predicate`) stands, and inverts **three** of the table's rows rather than the two the notice and the entry name. The third is the row the notice does not reach: a passed Challenge is unconditionally eligible in (`tab:eligibility:training`), on the stated ground that the request proceeded, and the predicate admits it only under the ground-truth flag. So the shipped predicate is wrong on every Challenge row and on the Slow row, and correct only on Allow, Block and the ground-truth convention. The registered claim about the policy field was verified on all three of its parts — it ships, it defaults as tabulated, and nothing production reads it — and the predicate is declared in a form that could not consult it if it were threaded. Its single call site is the label path, whose own notice records the missing threading, so the defect carries two notices rather than one.

The companion read decay (`entry:assayer:wl-companion-read-decay`) stands on both halves. The magnitude was recomputed: a balanced channel read after a long idle period returns a variance about two and a third times tighter than the corpus's, which is the direction the corpus flags as the one in which staleness is not conservative. The registered finding that the notice's own prescribed remedy is wrong is confirmed — it asks for read access to be made clocked *and mutable*, and the corpus forbids the second half in terms.

That notice has been corrected, and it is the only change to code this wave made. The freeze's notice-improvement exception permits it and the founding entry asked for it, the argument being that a notice prescribing a corpus violation will be implemented as written by whoever closes it. The notice now asks for read access to be clocked but not mutable, says what to do instead — decay the pseudo-counts into locals as a pure function of elapsed time, leaving the stored state untouched — and cites the requirement it answers to. Its opening words are unchanged, so its label is unchanged and the notice census still resolves to the same count.

**Entry (The risk basis reports an unweighted count where the corpus specifies a weighted one)** · `entry:assayer:wl-risk-unweighted-samples`

**Locus** `src/risk/calibration.rs:252`.

**Disposition** divergent-code-wrong against (`schema:risk:basis`), which specifies the effective sample counts as *weighted* samples, the weight being the regime membership the transition function assigns. The snapshot ships a count taken against a hard threshold instead, so a buffer whose entries all sit just below that threshold reports no anchor evidence at all where the specified quantity is substantial. The sharper half is that the correct quantity is already computed a few hundred lines away and has no production reader.

**Severity** high. The number leaves the package on the risk basis a host acts on and reaches two further surfaces — the maturity flag and the frozen-regime indicator — so a deployment reads an anchor as having no evidence and no maturity when by the corpus's own definition it has both.

**What conformance would mean** the snapshot carries the weighted counts the fit already computes, rather than a threshold tally computed beside them.

**Test coverage** none.

**Repaired** in wave two at `0b509f43`. The snapshot computes the weighted counts — regime share times importance weight, the floor's own quantity — and they flow as floats through the risk basis and both downstream surfaces. The coverage gap closed with the pin the entry's scenario implies: a buffer of just-below-threshold records now reports the recomputed weighted evidence where the tally reported none.

**Entry (The fit's sample floor is discounted and its own pre-check is not)** · `entry:assayer:wl-risk-recency-floor`

**Locus** `src/risk/calibration.rs:457`, the effective sample count, against the pre-check at `src/risk/calibration.rs:219` which states in its own comment that it intentionally omits the recency weighting.

**Disposition** divergent-code-wrong against (`constr:platt:buffer`), which fixes the sample count the floor is compared against and does not discount it by recency. The fit's count sums the regime weights, and those weights carry the recency factor, so at a full buffer the count runs at about five sixths of the specified one and the floor is crossed later than the tabulated sample count requires.

The consequence is a control-flow trap rather than an arithmetic one, and it is what makes this an entry rather than a table row. The pre-check computes the conformant, undiscounted count and therefore passes while the fit's own floor still fails.
