Recorded first, because an audit that lists only its findings misrepresents what it read. Sixteen rows were checked across the cluster and hold, and the table is the whole of that set rather than a selection from it — this is a small cluster and the count is small honestly.

| Checked | Authority | Evidence |
| --- | --- | --- |
| A dedicated thread requests a checkpoint on the configured interval and writes nothing itself | (`dec:durability:checkpoint-journal`) | `src/persistence/scheduler.rs:56` |
| The scheduler leaves its interval promptly when its control channel is dropped, rather than sleeping through it | (`dec:durability:checkpoint-journal`) | `src/persistence/scheduler.rs:58` |
| A submission is sanitised before it is journalled, never after | (`conv:durability:sanitise-before-journal`) | `src/api/label.rs:112`, journalled at `src/api/label.rs:133` |
| The journal append and the channel hand-off happen inside one mutex, so journal order is channel order | (`cor:durability:replay-exactness`) | `src/api/label.rs:134`, released at `src/api/label.rs:145` |
| The assessment path performs no persistence work of any kind — no append, no counter, no deferred flush | (`dec:durability:assess-no-persistence`), (`inv:guarantee:assess-only`) | the cluster, by census: the sole journal caller is `src/api/label.rs:132` |
| Elapsed decay is applied once, to the checkpointed state, before any journalled label is handed back for replay | (`dec:durability:decay-once`) | `src/persistence/recovery.rs:117`, before `src/persistence/recovery.rs:178` |
| Model time-decay scales the precision down and the covariance reciprocally up, leaving the mean where it was | (`alg:temporal:lazy-application`) | `src/persistence/recovery.rs:121`, into `src/model/bayesian.rs` |
| A Ledger average and an identity cell average are scaled toward zero rather than rescaled as a posterior is | (`alg:temporal:lazy-application`) | `src/persistence/recovery.rs:130`, `src/persistence/recovery.rs:148` |
| The three time-indexed rates the inventory separates are read separately, each from its own configured field | (`tab:temporal:component-rates`) | `src/persistence/recovery.rs:119`, `:127`, `:145` |
| The class-rate priors cross the restore undecayed, carrying no time-indexed rate | (`dec:weighting:no-time-decay`) | `src/snapshot/working.rs:528`, and `src/persistence/recovery.rs` by absence |
| The restore's elapsed interval is obtained through the timestamp interface, which floors it at zero and ceilings it at a year without the caller reapplying either | (`dec:clock:embedded-clamp`) | `src/persistence/recovery.rs:115`, into `src/types.rs:367` |
| The checkpoint's own timestamp is taken from the owner's injected clock rather than the wall clock | (`dec:operational:temporal-governance`) | `src/owner/thread.rs:344` |
| A structurally incompatible checkpoint is refused, and the caller falls back to cold start rather than failing | (`dec:durability:structural-compatibility`) | `src/persistence/recovery.rs:100`, `src/lib.rs:676` |
| A numerical parameter change is permitted across a restore: the structural check reads no numerical field | (`dec:durability:structural-compatibility`) | `src/persistence/recovery.rs:216`, the configuration parameter being unread |
| Every field the restore validates is a field the checkpoint structure declares | (`cav:durability:restore-field-mismatch`) | `src/persistence/recovery.rs:221`, `:231`, `:251` against `src/persistence/checkpoint.rs:219`, `:253`, `:257` |
| Replay resumes strictly above the checkpoint's mark, entries at or below it being filtered out | (`dec:durability:checkpoint-journal`) | `src/persistence/recovery.rs:178` |

Two of these rows deserve a sentence rather than a line, because in both cases the corpus said in terms that it was not claiming the property.

The field-mismatch row discharges an obligation the record explicitly declined to assert. The caveat names a legacy restore that validated against two checkpoint fields its own checkpoint structure did not contain, states that what a rewrite must check instead is that every validated field is a declared one, and closes by saying that the record "states the obligation and does not claim it is currently discharged" (`cav:durability:restore-field-mismatch`). It is discharged. The restore validates three properties — the anchor width, the signal schema and the interaction templates — and all three are fields of `CheckpointPayload`. The caveat's preferred remedy, that the property become a test rather than a sentence, is not met: nothing mechanically enforces it, and a field added to the validation and not to the payload would not compile, which is the accident that currently holds the property rather than a guard.

The permitted-numerical-change row is carried by an unused parameter, and that is worth saying plainly because an unread argument usually reads as an oversight. Here it is the decision: `validate_structural_compatibility` takes the configuration and never consults it, which is exactly what (`dec:durability:structural-compatibility`) asks for when it permits an operator to retune and restart without discarding learned state. This is the one place in the campaign so far where an ignored parameter is the conformance rather than the defect.

**Entry (The journal entry is stripped of what two of the seventeen label steps need)** · `entry:assayer:wl-persistence-stripped-context`

**Locus** `src/pending/entry.rs:223`, the journal-side context type, reached through `src/persistence/journal.rs:45` where it is the entry's payload; the two steps that cannot run from it are `src/owner/label_path.rs:746` and `src/owner/label_path.rs:880`. No labelled notice stands at any of the three, and the item is new.

**Disposition** divergent-code-wrong against (`dec:durability:checkpoint-journal`), whose self-containment half is stated as the load-bearing one in terms: the journal's entries "are self-contained — each carries enough to replay without the state that produced it", and the record then argues that a journal of deltas would be smaller and worse because "the entry that is a whole submission depends on nothing but itself". The shipped entry is not a whole submission. It carries the entity, the per-Sentinel extractions, the identity coordinates and active cells, and the merged signal features, and it omits the risk basis and the outcome predictions — the type's own name for itself is the journal-*stripped* version of the pending assessment.

**The observable consequence.** The label path branches on whether it is replaying, at two points and only two. Step fourteen, which pushes the calibration entry that (`constr:platt:buffer`) fixes the contents of, is skipped during replay, because the blended score and the anchor weight it would record are on the risk basis the entry does not carry. Step sixteen, the drift accumulators, is skipped for the same reason: (`alg:monitoring:drift-cusums`) requires the residual be taken against the *assessment-time* prediction, and the assessment-time prediction is on the risk basis too. So every replayed label receives fifteen of the seventeen steps, permanently and silently, and the two it does not receive are the two the corpus builds the most machinery around.

The skip is not the defect and should not be repaired as one. Given an entry without the risk basis, skipping step sixteen is the *correct* reading of (`alg:monitoring:drift-cusums`) — recomputing the prediction from the restored model and calling the difference a residual would be a worse answer than none. The defect is upstream, at the entry's contents, which is precisely what self-containment means and precisely what the record chose.

**The corollary this falsifies.** (`cor:durability:replay-exactness`) concludes that a recorded assessment replays to the same output and adds that "nothing further is required of the replay machinery: it is exact because the two things that could make it inexact, a time-dependent factor applied a variable number of times and a boundary transformation applied twice, are each excluded by a decision above". Both exclusions hold and are conformant rows above. A third way of being inexact exists and the corollary does not contemplate it: an entry that lacks the inputs for a step, so that the step does not run. The corollary's enumeration is therefore not exhaustive, and the audit records that as a finding against the code rather than against the record, because the record's premise — self-containment — is the thing the code does not supply.

**Severity** high. It is bounded, and the bound must be stated with it: only labels between the last checkpoint and the crash are replayed, so the loss is at most one checkpoint interval's worth of calibration entries and drift residuals. It is argued to high rather than medium on three grounds. The first is that the loss is unrecoverable and untraceable — no counter records a replayed label, and the calibration buffer cannot tell a label it never saw from one that never happened. The second is that it compounds with two registered items into a worst case neither has alone: the calibration buffer is not checkpointed either, so a restart loses the whole buffer *and* fails to rebuild the part the journal was holding, and the drift accumulators are discarded outright by the restore (`entry:assayer:wl-health-checkpoint-drops-drift`) *and* are not moved by the replay that follows. The third is that the corpus's derivation is unsound rather than merely unmet, which is a different and worse thing to leave standing: a reader of (`cor:durability:replay-exactness`) is told the machinery needs nothing further, and it needs something further.

**What conformance would mean** the journal context carries the risk basis and the outcome predictions as the original run computed them, at which point the two `is_replay` guards can be deleted and a replayed label runs the same seventeen steps the original would have. The record's own reasoning fixes that this is the repair rather than the alternative: a replayed label must apply what the original applied, which is why sanitisation precedes journalling (`conv:durability:sanitise-before-journal`), and recomputing the scalars against the restored model would reintroduce exactly the dependence on later state that the self-containment half was chosen to remove.

**Coverage** worse than none, and this is the campaign's sharpest instance of a claim arguing against its own corpus. The stripping is pinned by `to_context_strips_transient_fields` at `src/pending/entry.rs:307`, whose authored claim states two things. It states that "the model-derived scalars are recomputed on replay" — they are not recomputed on replay, they are skipped, and the same false assurance stands in the type's own documentation at `src/pending/entry.rs:208`, which lists the risk basis and the outcome predictions under "Recomputed at label time". And it argues the stripping from a principle the governing record decides the other way: that "persisting either would let a restored run disagree with the model it was restored into". The record's whole arrangement is built so that a restored run agrees with the *original* run rather than with the model it was restored into — that is what (`conv:durability:sanitise-before-journal`) is for and what (`cor:durability:replay-exactness`) concludes from it. So the corpus's own test claim names a mechanism that does not exist and prefers the design the corpus rejected, in the gloss a reader consults to learn why the type is shaped as it is. Repairing the entry turns that test red and requires its claim to be rewritten with it.

**The snapshot and pending tranche owns the type and adds three facts** (`rem:assayer:snapshot-pending-cross-cites`). The false assurance stands at four sites in the type's own file rather than at the two recorded here, and the two further ones are the type's documentation rather than a test's. The type's governing record condemns the omission independently of the durability record: (`dec:retention:journal-subset`) licenses a consumption-defined subset only where replay is exact, and subordinates itself to the corollary this entry falsifies, so the record a reader would cite in the code's defence is the record that makes the omission divergent. And the mechanism of the skip is narrower and quieter than a replay flag: a context accessor returns an option that is empty for the replay variant, so the two steps are passed over rather than refused. The disposition and the severity are unchanged.

**Repaired** in wave five at `1839c671`, as the record's own reasoning fixed: the journal context carries the risk basis and the outcome predictions as the original run computed them, the option-returning accessors return values for both context kinds, and the two replay guards are deleted — a replayed label runs the same seventeen steps the original ran. The false assurance is gone from all four sites, and the family of tests defending the stripping is restated inverted: the strips-transient-fields pin now establishes that only what is owned elsewhere is omitted, the two replay-skip pins became runs-in-replay pins (a calibration entry pushed, a drift step banked against the journalled prediction), and the R17 exactness invariant strengthened to include the buffer — the replayed run arrives at the live run's twenty entries where it used to assert the divergent ten. The journal's entry layout changed under the repair, which is why the format version guard the wave adds to the journal file (`8bd6f866`) lands beside it: a pre-repair journal is refused on the version and discarded by design, never misread.

**Entry (The whole-state checkpoint carries the models and little else)** · `entry:assayer:wl-persistence-partial-payload`

**Locus** `src/persistence/checkpoint.rs:213`, the payload structure, against the owner's working set at `src/owner/thread.rs:60`. A notice stood over the payload asking for the complete Core-owned set, and the wave-five repair took it with the gap it disclosed.

**Disposition** divergent-code-wrong against (`dec:durability:checkpoint-journal`), which fixes what survives a restart as a periodic **whole-state** checkpoint. The owner tranche reached this defect from the write site and registered the half it could see from there — that the Ledger and identity payloads are passed as empty collections (`entry:assayer:wl-owner-checkpoint-truncation`). Read from the payload, the gap is considerably larger than two empty vectors, and the audit owes the enumeration.

The payload declares the three core models, the per-axis models, five scalars, the sequence mark, the checkpoint timestamp, the dimension map, the two structural metadata lists, the concordance tracker's state, and the two collections that are always empty. The state the model owner holds and the payload has no field for at all is: the calibration buffer, the Platt convergence tracker, the identity convergence trackers, the running feature means and variances, the feature class vector, the label counter, and the health counters. To those the health tranche's restore finding adds the drift accumulators, which the working copy holds and the payload also has no field for (`entry:assayer:wl-health-checkpoint-drops-drift`).

The notice standing over the payload is unusually good and is worth crediting before it is faulted: it names the calibration buffer, the Platt and identity trackers, the eligible-label counter, the standardisation phase and classes, the integrity and degradation counters and the discrimination metrics, which is most of the list above and is a disclosure rather than a silence. What it omits is the drift accumulators — the one item on the list that another tranche has already registered as a defect in its own right — so the notice a reader would consult to learn what the checkpoint is missing does not mention the missing thing the corpus has already been told about.

**Severity** high, and the argument is that the consequences are not uniform and the worst of them is not the biggest. Losing the Ledger and the identity state costs a deployment its long-memory spatial and per-range reputation at every restart, which the suite's own claims say takes as long to rebuild as it took to learn; that is the largest quantity lost and it is a medium, because the layers degrade to neutral rather than to wrong. Losing the calibration buffer is worse per unit, because until the buffer refills past the sample floor no refit happens at all (`req:platt:minimum-samples`) and the probabilities a host reads as frequencies are the cold-start mapping. And losing the standardisation statistics is worst of all, for a reason that has nothing to do with their size and is registered separately immediately below.

**What conformance would mean** the payload carries the Core-owned state the notice enumerates, plus the drift accumulators the notice omits, and the write site captures the Ledger and identity state rather than passing empty vectors. The format version is already the guard that makes this safe to do — it has been bumped eight times and a mismatch cold-starts by design — so the obstacle is the capture and the coordination the write-site notice names, not the format.

**Coverage** inverted, and this is the reason the gap has survived being so plainly disclosed. Six tests establish that Ledger and identity state cross the checkpoint faithfully — `checkpoint_with_ledger`, `checkpoint_with_identity`, `checkpoint_with_cell_outcome_state`, the two decay-on-restore tests, and `checkpoint_complete_round_trip` — and every one of them constructs the payload by hand and passes it populated. The production write site passes empty vectors and the two restore callers drop the result, so no test in the workspace exercises the path a deployment takes. The claims are correspondingly confident: one of them says the Ledger "is a long-memory structure built from many assessments; rebuilding it from scratch after a restart would take as long as it took to learn", which is a true sentence about a transfer that does not happen. A reader auditing this suite would conclude the Ledger is checkpointed. This is a shape the campaign has not met before and it is worth naming: not a test that pins a divergence as its expected value, but a test that establishes a capability the production path declines to use, which hides the divergence more effectively than no test would.

**Sequencing** this entry is the parent of four. The write site's empty Ledger and identity vectors (`entry:assayer:wl-owner-checkpoint-truncation`), the absent drift accumulators (`entry:assayer:wl-health-checkpoint-drops-drift`), the three missing per-axis fields (`entry:assayer:wl-persistence-axis-fields`) and the warm start's falsified premise (`entry:assayer:wl-persistence-warm-start-standardisation`) are each a hole in this payload seen from a different cluster, and each closes when the payload gains the field it names. They are four repairs to one structure and should be one wave, not four. This entry's own repair has a prerequisite outside it: the standardisation statistics cannot join the payload until they move onto the working copy the payload is built from (`rem:assayer:snapshot-pending-cross-cites`), which is the relocation (`entry:assayer:wl-snapshot-lifecycle-standardisation`) also needs.

**Repaired** in wave five at `cb0271d0`, with the children landing beside it in their own commits. The payload gains every field the notice enumerated that exists to carry — the calibration buffer with its rows, the Platt and identity convergence trackers, the label counter, the health counters with their discrimination metrics — plus the drift accumulators the notice omitted and the standardisation vectors the wave-four relocation made reachable; the write site captures the Ledger and identity state; and the format version bumps eight to nine with a test refusing the prior version by name. The calibration rows cross carrying the assessment-time uncertainty, which is the structural half of (`entry:assayer:wl-empirical-coverage`) the wave-nine note routes here, landed at `0f3dcd10`. The inverted coverage gains the test the suite was shaped not to hold: with no payload built by hand, a running instance's acknowledged checkpoint carries a distinctive ledger rate value for value, the identity graph at the live snapshot's energy, the buffer row for row and every counter — and the rebuilt instance serves the restored ledger and counts its next label from the captured totals, where before the restart the counters restarted at zero and the collections crossed empty. One boundary the repair could not cross is recorded on the remaining-divergences table below: the restored identity payloads await the re-registration that alone can supply the encode closure.

**Entry (A warm start switches off standardisation's fast path on a premise its neighbour falsifies)** · `entry:assayer:wl-persistence-warm-start-standardisation`

**Locus** `src/lib.rs:559`, the branch, argued by the comment immediately above it at `src/lib.rs:556`; the statistics it speaks of are constructed neutral at `src/owner/thread.rs:155` and `src/owner/thread.rs:156`, and the payload that would carry them is `src/persistence/checkpoint.rs:213`. No labelled notice stands at the branch, and the item is new.

**Disposition** divergent-code-wrong against (`req:standardisation:timing`), which names three mechanisms that feed the standardisation statistics, and against (`alg:standardisation:batch-initialisation`), whose purpose is that the class priors are replaced wholesale by empirical statistics after the first hundred assessments.

The construction path disables the batch initialisation on a warm start, and its comment states the ground: "restored `feature_means`/`variances` are already empirical". They are not restored. The checkpoint payload declares no field for either, no restore path writes either, and `ModelOwner::new` initialises the means to zero and the variances to one on every construction, warm and cold alike. The premise is falsified three lines below the sentence that states it, by the same comment block: "NOT checkpointed: class priors are adequate on restart".

So the code's own documentation says, in adjacent lines, both that the statistics were restored and that they are not checkpointed, and the branch acts on the first. This is the shape the register named as the campaign's highest-severity generator — a function defensible read alone and wrong about what its neighbour left behind (`reg:assayer:audit-tranches`) — met here between two comments in one block rather than between two files.

**The observable sequence.** Run an instance until its standardisation statistics are empirical. Checkpoint, stop, restart. The restored instance comes up with every mean at zero and every variance at one, which is the neutral pair that standardises a position as though it were already standardised, and with the accumulator that would replace them after a hundred assessments absent and its active flag false. The tracking average is then the only mechanism left, and it is the slowest of the three: the corpus's own estimate is about one standardisation half-life, some three thousand five hundred labels, against the hundred assessments the batch initialisation is specified to take.

**Severity** high. Nothing is corrupted in the sense of a wrong stored number, but every feature every model reads is mis-scaled for the whole of that half-life after every restart, and the mechanism specified to end the wait early is switched off rather than merely unavailable. The compounding argument is the one that settles it against medium. The feature tranche established that of the three mechanisms (`req:standardisation:timing`) names, the per-Sentinel bootstrap never runs (`entry:assayer:wl-feature-bootstrap-unrun`) and the batch initialisation can be discarded permanently when a channel is full (`entry:assayer:wl-owner-batch-init-drop`), leaving the tracking average. This entry removes the second of the three by the ordinary act of restarting, and it does so on every restart rather than on a contended one. A deployment that restarts more often than a standardisation half-life — a weekly deploy cadence against three and a half thousand labels — never has empirical standardisation at all, and the priors it waits out are not even its own classes' priors but the generic one (`entry:assayer:wl-feature-cold-start-classes`).

**What conformance would mean** the running means, variances and classes join the checkpoint payload, which is the repair (`entry:assayer:wl-persistence-partial-payload`) already names and the payload's own notice already asks for, after which the warm-start branch's premise becomes true and the branch becomes correct. **The snapshot and pending tranche adds the prerequisite this repair and the payload's share** (`rem:assayer:snapshot-pending-cross-cites`): the statistics cannot join the payload in the conversion's present shape, because the payload is built from the working copy and the working copy does not hold them. They must first move onto it, which is also what this tranche's own headline entry needs (`entry:assayer:wl-snapshot-lifecycle-standardisation`), so one relocation serves three registered repairs. Failing that, the branch leaves the batch initialisation active on a warm start and the comment stops asserting a restore that does not happen. The first is what (`conv:assayer:audit-measure`) presumes, because the record requires the checkpoint to be whole-state.

**Coverage** none, and none is reachable from outside. No test observes the statistics the owner constructs, and the end-to-end restart test `checkpoint_round_trip_end_to_end` asserts that a rebuilt instance registers, assesses and returns a finite risk — all of which hold under neutral standardisation, since the neutral pair is a division by one.

**Sequencing** child of (`entry:assayer:wl-persistence-partial-payload`), and downstream with it of the standardisation relocation described at (`rem:assayer:snapshot-pending-cross-cites`). The relocation serves three registered repairs — this one, the payload's, and (`entry:assayer:wl-snapshot-lifecycle-standardisation`) — and doing it once serves all three.

**Repaired** in wave five at `83713f4b`, the first of the conformance line's two options as the audit's presumption fixes: the running means, variances and classes join the payload, and the branch's premise becomes true rather than corrected — batch initialisation stays off on a warm start because the statistics really are restored, and the comment block that asserted a restore three lines above conceding none now states the ground the payload supplies. Wave four's neutral-fill comment in `from_checkpoint_payload`, which cited this entry as its subject, goes with the neutral fill. The observable sequence the entry states ran as the falsifier: labels moving the variances off neutral, an acknowledged checkpoint, a rebuild from the same directory — the restored instance published the neutral pair before the repair and publishes the captured vectors after it.

**Entry (Sequence numbering restarts below the mark the replay filter measures against)** · `entry:assayer:wl-persistence-seq-restart`

**Locus** `src/lib.rs:543`, the writer opened without the checkpoint's mark, against the constructor written for that purpose at `src/persistence/journal.rs:294`. A notice stood at the call site conceding the consequence in terms, and the repair took it.

**Disposition** divergent-code-wrong against (`dec:durability:checkpoint-journal`), which makes the journal the record of the labels in flight since the checkpoint, and against (`cor:durability:replay-exactness`), which requires a recorded assessment to replay. A label that is journalled and then filtered out of its own replay is neither.

The cluster ships two constructors. `JournalWriter::open` numbers from one above the highest sequence it finds in the file. `JournalWriter::open_after` numbers from one above the greater of that and the checkpoint's `last_processed_label_seq`, and its documentation says why: the checkpoint's mark "provides a lower bound". Production calls the first. The notice standing at the call site concedes the consequence in terms — `open()` "can restart below `last_processed_label_seq` after a checkpoint-truncated journal".

**The observable sequence.** Run to sequence five hundred. A checkpoint records five hundred as the mark and truncates the journal to nothing, which is what the truncation does today (`entry:assayer:wl-owner-checkpoint-truncation`). Crash before another label arrives. On restart the checkpoint restores at five hundred, the journal is empty so nothing is replayed, and the writer opens over an empty file and sets its next sequence to one. Every label the second run accepts is journalled at one, two, three, and acknowledged to the host. Crash again before the next checkpoint. On the third start the checkpoint still says five hundred, the journal holds the second run's entries, and the replay filter keeps entries whose sequence exceeds five hundred. It keeps none. The whole of the second run's in-flight work is discarded silently, and the artefact holding it is read, parsed, and passed over.

**Severity** high. It is silent, unrecoverable loss of labels the host was told were durable, on the path whose only purpose is that such labels survive. It is rarer than the truncation entry it depends on, needing two restarts with a checkpoint-truncated journal between them, and it is worse when it fires, because the truncation loses the labels appended during one checkpoint's window while this loses every label of an entire run. The two are one defect seen at two distances and should be closed together: sequence-aware truncation removes the empty-journal precondition, and `open_after` removes the consequence if truncation is left as it is.

**What conformance would mean** the construction path calls `open_after` with the restored mark, which is in scope at the call site — the restore returns a working copy carrying `last_processed_label_seq` some sixty lines above. The function exists, is exported, is correct, and is tested. This is the fourth time the campaign has found a specified mechanism written and not called (`rem:assayer:extraction-census`), and it is the cheapest of the four: one argument at one call site.

**Coverage** worse than none in the precise sense the campaign has been recording, and the claim is unusually explicit about it. `journal_writer_open_after_uses_max_of_checkpoint_and_journal` at `src/tests/persistence.rs:997` establishes the property in full, and its authored claim names the hazard this entry describes: the two marks "can disagree in either direction — a journal truncated after checkpointing lags, an unabsorbed tail leads — and only the maximum guarantees the next number is above everything the model has already seen". So the corpus's suite holds a test that proves the correct function correct and states why the incorrect one is dangerous, beside a production path that calls the incorrect one. Nothing tests which constructor construction uses.

**Sequencing** paired with (`entry:assayer:wl-owner-checkpoint-truncation`) for the reason argued above, and the pair is asymmetric. This half removes the consequence and can land alone — it is one argument at one call site, against a function that already exists and is already tested — so it need not wait for the truncation or for the payload (`entry:assayer:wl-persistence-partial-payload`) that the truncation discards the contents of. Closing the truncation without this one is the order that does not work, because the consequence stays reachable by the unabsorbed-tail route.

**Repaired** in wave one at `5ae28394`, the asymmetric half the sequencing says can land alone. The truncation half stands, and with this closed its remaining consequence is the one its own entry states rather than this one. The coverage line's gap is closed too: a test now restores over a mark of five hundred with the journal truncated away and reads the writer's next sequence, which was one before the repair.

**Entry (An outcome axis returns from a restart nameless and on the default eligibility)** · `entry:assayer:wl-persistence-axis-fields`

**Locus** `src/persistence/checkpoint.rs:184`, the per-axis checkpoint state, and `src/snapshot/working.rs:511` and `src/snapshot/working.rs:516`, where the restore substitutes an empty name and the default eligibility. No notice stands at any of the three, and the item is new.

**Disposition** divergent-code-wrong against (`schema:registry:axis-record`), which fixes seven fields for a registered axis. The checkpoint carries four of the seven: the identifier as the map key, the initial compression scale, the forgetting rate and the spatial-feature policy. Absent are the name, the description and the training-eligibility mode. It is divergent rather than unspecified because (`dec:durability:checkpoint-journal`) makes the checkpoint whole-state and the restore path's own documentation states that runtime-registered entities "are restored from the checkpoint" (`src/persistence/recovery.rs:212`), so the corpus and the code agree that this state is meant to cross and it does not.

The three absences are not of one kind and only one of them is serious.

The eligibility mode is the serious one, because it decides what the axis model learns from. (`def:axis:training-target`) says an axis model "trains on the labels its eligibility mode admits", and the label path reads the field to gate each axis update at `src/owner/label_path.rs:658`. An axis registered on the all-labels mode comes back on the eligible-only mode, which is the enumeration's default, so from the restart onward it is trained on a strictly smaller and differently-selected population than the host declared — silently, in learned parameters, and with no surface reporting the mode it is actually running under.

The name breaks a stated property rather than a computation. The schema requires the name to be "unique across currently registered axes", and after a restore with two or more axes every axis carries the same empty name, so the uniqueness the schema fixes fails at the moment there is more than one axis to fail it. The name is also live on the output surface: it is read at `src/assessment.rs:1086` and copied into every outcome prediction at `src/assessment.rs:1098`, so a host consuming assessments after a restart receives axis outcomes labelled with an empty string until it re-registers.

The description is inert — nothing in the workspace reads it — and is recorded only because the schema names it.

**Severity** high, on the eligibility mode alone. It corrupts learned parameters rather than a diagnostic, it is silent, it is permanent for the life of the restored process, and it fires on every restart of any deployment that set the mode away from its default. It is bounded to such deployments, which is a real bound and must be stated: a host that leaves every axis on eligible-only sees nothing. The name and the description would be a low on their own.

**What conformance would mean** `CheckpointAxisState` carries the name, the description and the eligibility mode, and the restore takes all three from the payload rather than substituting. The neighbouring evidence says this was an omission rather than a decision: the same structure already carries the spatial policy, which is the schema's other per-axis policy field, so one of the two policies was thought about and the other was not.

**Coverage** none. `checkpoint_empty_outcome_models` covers the case where there are no axes at all, and `checkpoint_complete_round_trip` registers one axis and asserts its spatial flag survives — the field that does cross — while asserting nothing about the two that do not. No test in the workspace registers an axis on the all-labels mode and checkpoints it.

**Sequencing** child of (`entry:assayer:wl-persistence-partial-payload`). The three absent fields are three absent payload fields, on the per-axis structure rather than the whole-state one, and the repair is the same repair on a smaller type.

**Repaired** in wave five at `f5987384`, all three fields, and the description's repair went one type deeper than the entry could see from the checkpoint: no live state held a description at all — the registration record itself lacked the schema's field — so the registration gains it, the working axis model holds it, and the CP5/CP6 revert preserves it across `from_snapshot` the way wave four's repair preserves the class vector. The coverage line's missing test now exists: an axis registered on the all-labels mode with a name and description crosses the checkpoint and is read back off the restored working copy with all three values equal, where the restore substituted an empty name and the default eligibility before.

**Table (The persistence cluster's remaining divergences)** · `tab:assayer:persistence-remaining`

| Divergence | Authority | Locus | Severity |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Both construction paths drop the restored Ledger and identity payloads before they reach runtime infrastructure, so the fields the payload declares would not survive even if the write site populated them | (`dec:durability:checkpoint-journal`) | `src/lib.rs:673`, `src/lib.rs:759` | medium |
| The payload's notice enumerates what the checkpoint is missing and omits the drift accumulators, which is the one absence another tranche has already registered | (`tab:monitoring:drift-resets`) | `src/persistence/checkpoint.rs:207` | low |

The first row needs its relation to the payload entry stated, because the two are easy to read as one. They are independent breaks on the same path and either alone would suffice: the write site passes empty collections, and both readers discard the collections they receive. Both stood disclosed by notices at the two callers, and wave five took both: the restored Ledger state now attaches to the runtime outcome ledger, and the identity half — blocked on an encode closure no checkpoint can carry — stands under the successor notice (`todo:code:reattach-restored-identity-state-when`). It is registered at medium rather than folded into the payload entry because closing that entry without this row would produce a checkpoint that captures the state correctly and a restore that throws it away.

**Remark (What the census turned up in this cluster, and the gaps the corpus leaves)** · `rem:assayer:persistence-census`

The extraction tranche predicted from fan-in that this cluster would repay the census question hardest (`rem:assayer:extraction-census`), and the health tranche corrected the prediction's reason rather than its aim — the yield comes from a cluster's contract, not its shape. Both readings are borne out here, in opposite directions.

By call-site census the cluster is almost clean. Every public function has a production caller: the scheduler is spawned at `src/lib.rs:481`, the restore is called from both construction paths, the checkpoint writer and the journal truncation from the owner's checkpoint handler, the journal reader from the restore and from the writer's two constructors, and the writer's append from the label surface. One constructor is the exception, and it is the whole of the census's yield: `JournalWriter::open_after` has no production caller, and the corpus wants it to have one, which is the finding at (`entry:assayer:wl-persistence-seq-restart`). One accessor, `next_seq`, is reached only by tests; it is accessor surface on a public type in a library crate and is not registered, on the reading the health tranche settled (`rem:assayer:health-census`).

The census's real yield here is of a kind the question was not built to find, and it is worth naming because two later clusters will meet it. Thirty-odd lines of `src/persistence/recovery.rs` — the per-entry Ledger decay at `src/persistence/recovery.rs:126` and the per-cell identity decay at `src/persistence/recovery.rs:144` — are reached on every restore and iterate over collections that are empty in every deployment, because the write site never populates them and both readers discard them anyway. Asking who calls this returns the right answer, twice: they are called, and their input is always empty. A function that runs and does nothing is invisible to a call-site census and to a compiler alike, and only reading the write path establishes it. The cluster carries no module-wide dead-code suppression, which makes it the second cluster after `extraction/` where the compiler could have been trusted — and the one case the compiler could not have caught is exactly this one.

Four gaps the corpus leaves are recorded here rather than as entries, because in each the specification and the records settle nothing and what the code does is therefore neither conformant nor divergent.

The checkpoint file's integrity envelope is **unspecified**. Magic bytes, a format version and a payload checksum are the code's own arrangement; no record requires an envelope and the specification does not know the file exists. The same holds of the version-mismatch policy — a mismatch cold-starts with no migration path — and of the atomic write protocol, temporary file to sync to rename. All three are good engineering and none is a corpus obligation, so the audit records them as choices rather than as rows.

The journal reader's failure policy is **unspecified** by the corpus, and the code now settles each of its branches on the merits at the restore in `src/persistence/recovery.rs`. A truncated trailing record is dropped and every complete record before it is returned, because that is the single label the append protocol allows a crash to cost. A read failure that is not a torn tail fails the restore: the entries past the checkpoint's mark are the only copy of every label acknowledged since the capture, so proceeding with no replay would discard them under a report of a successful start. A journal of another format version keeps the discard the version policy already gives it, so that an upgraded binary meeting the previous deployment's journal cold-starts instead of refusing to run. The audit records the three as choices rather than as rows, because no corpus text fixes them. One narrower fact belongs with it, and the refusal does not reach it: a corrupt length prefix in the middle of the file makes the computed payload end exceed the file length, which the reader treats as tail truncation and stops on, so a mid-file corruption of the four framing bytes still discards every record after it under the branch written for the tail.

A failed periodic checkpoint is discarded without a warning, a counter or a health field, and this is **unspecified** with a severity worth stating anyway. The scheduler sends its request with no completion channel (`src/persistence/scheduler.rs:69`), and the handler binds the write's result and drops it when there is no channel to send it on (`src/owner/thread.rs:331`). So a deployment whose checkpoint directory has filled, or whose permissions have changed, writes no checkpoint from that moment on and nothing anywhere says so — the restore simply finds an old file, or none, at the next start. Nothing in the metrics catalogue names a checkpoint, so the gap has no export half either. It is registered as unspecified rather than divergent because no corpus text fixes how a failed durability write is surfaced; (`dec:concurrency:no-silent-drop`) is the nearest thing and it is about channel overflow rather than about I/O. It is recorded because the shape — a mechanism that has stopped working and reports a reassuring silence — is the one the health tranche argued up twice, and because closing it is a few lines beside a repair the cluster already needs.

Two dead-code exemptions in the cluster name a state of affairs that has passed and one names a state of affairs that never was, which is the false-assurance shape the ledger tranche registered at its absence cast (`entry:assayer:wl-ledger-absence-cast`) in its mildest form. The journal's append helper and its file opener are exempted as "used by model owner in Phase 1+" and the writer as "used by `label()`"; both callers now exist, so the exemptions are stale rather than wrong. The checkpoint error's structural-mismatch variant is exempted as "used by recovery module" and the recovery module does not use it — the structural check returns its reasons as strings and the variant is constructed nowhere in the workspace. All three are low and none is registered; they are recorded so that a reader who checks an exemption's justification is not the first to find it false.

**Remark (The items terminating in this cluster, cross-cited, and one correction to a prior tranche)** · `rem:assayer:persistence-cross-cites`

Four registered items terminate in or cross this cluster. None is re-registered; all were re-verified at their recorded lines and all stand, and one carries a correction that belongs to this cluster to make.

(`entry:assayer:wl-owner-checkpoint-truncation`) stands, and the owner tranche's account of it is exact on every particular this cluster can check. The two notices it names stood where it says they are — the identity-coordination one above the payload construction and the sequence-aware-truncation one above the truncation call, both since taken by the wave-five repair — and the empty vectors are passed at `src/owner/thread.rs:350`. This cluster owns the truncation site and adds three facts rather than a disposition. The first is that the site carried a *third* notice, at `src/persistence/journal.rs:206`, asking for the same sequence-aware truncation the call site's notice asked for, so one defect stood disclosed at both its call site and its implementation — repaired at both by the same wave. The second is that `truncate_journal` does what its name says and nothing more — it opens the file truncating, syncs and closes, taking no lock and consulting no sequence — so there is no partial coordination to build on. The third is the one that changes the entry's weight rather than its substance: the truncation's own consequence compounds into a second and larger loss through the sequence numbering, which is registered separately at (`entry:assayer:wl-persistence-seq-restart`), and the two should be scheduled together for the reason argued there.

(`entry:assayer:wl-health-checkpoint-drops-drift`) stands at `src/snapshot/working.rs:534`, where the restored working copy binds an empty drift map under the comment the health tranche quotes. This cluster owns the other half of it and confirms the half the health tranche inferred: the drift state is discarded on restore because `CheckpointPayload` declares no field for it (`src/persistence/checkpoint.rs:213`), so the restore is not choosing to drop evidence it holds — it has none to bind. That sharpens the entry's conformance line rather than changing its disposition. The health tranche wrote that the drift state should join the checkpoint payload, and this cluster confirms that is the whole of the repair on the persistence side: there is no format obstacle, the version field having been bumped eight times already, and the state sits on the working copy the payload is built from. One fact the health tranche could not see from its side is registered above at (`entry:assayer:wl-persistence-partial-payload`): the payload's own notice enumerates seven kinds of absent state and the drift accumulators are not among them, so the disclosure a reader would rely on does not cover this. And a second compounding is registered at (`entry:assayer:wl-persistence-stripped-context`): the replay that follows the restore does not move the accumulators either, because step sixteen is skipped, so the evidence is lost at the restore and not rebuilt by the labels that were being held for exactly that purpose.

(`entry:assayer:wl-empirical-coverage`) does not have its locus here, and the health tranche's correction to it terminates here. **That correction's added schedule fact is wrong and is corrected in place, in the direction that makes the repair smaller.** The health tranche found the entry's conformance line an underestimate and added that conformance "does mean widening a persisted type, since the calibration entry is serialised into the checkpoint, and that is a schedule fact the entry as written conceals" (`rem:assayer:health-cross-cites`). The calibration entry is not serialised into the checkpoint. `CheckpointPayload` declares no calibration field of any kind, no persistence surface in the workspace reads or writes a `CalibrationEntry` or a `CalibrationBuffer`, and the payload's own notice lists the calibration buffer among the state it does *not* yet carry (`src/persistence/checkpoint.rs:207`). What is true, and is presumably what the reading rested on, is that `CalibrationEntry` derives the serialisation traits under the same feature gate the checkpoint is behind (`src/risk/calibration.rs:131`) — the type is serialisable and is not serialised.

The substance of that entry is untouched: the absence of the empirical coverage diagnostic stands, and so does the health tranche's sharper finding that the calibration row carries no uncertainty for the standardised residual to divide by. What changes is the schedule, and it changes twice over. Widening `CalibrationEntry` today costs nothing at the persistence boundary — no format version bump, no compatibility question, no migration — so the repair is a field threaded from a structure already in hand and nothing more, which is what the founding entry said before the correction. And a real ordering fact stands where the wrong one did: the calibration buffer *is* required to join the payload, by (`dec:durability:checkpoint-journal`) and by the payload's own notice, and whenever it does it should be added carrying the uncertainty. The two repairs are therefore ordered rather than independent, and doing them in the other order pays for the format twice.

This is the second time the campaign has corrected a tranche's cross-citation from the cluster that owns the surface, and it is the first time the correction has run toward a *smaller* repair after a previous tranche had enlarged it. The register's advice — re-check an entry's repair against the cluster that owns the mechanism, in both directions (`reg:assayer:audit-tranches`) — is exactly what found it, applied to a correction rather than to a founding entry, and that is a narrowing worth carrying: a correction is a claim about the code like any other and is not carried on faith from the wave that made it (`conv:assayer:audit-evidence`).

**Remark (What the clock lane should take from this cluster)** · `rem:assayer:persistence-clock-lane`

Both files the clock lane's list names are in this cluster and each holds exactly one production reading of the wall clock, as the list has it. This tranche adds facts and no disposition, as the extraction and health tranches did (`rem:assayer:extraction-cross-cites`), (`rem:assayer:health-clock-lane`), and the facts here are milder than either of theirs.

The journal's reading is at `src/persistence/journal.rs:61`, where an unsequenced entry stamps the present into its timestamp field. It is reached from the label surface on every journalled label. Its exposure is small and should be measured rather than assumed: nothing reads the field back. The restore filters on the sequence rather than on the timestamp, the replay path does not consult it, and no test asserts on it, so the reading is currently a stamp that is written and never used. The lane should still take it, because it is reached from a hot path and because the field's purpose — knowing when an entry was journalled — is one a sequence-aware truncation would plausibly want.

The restore's reading is at `src/persistence/recovery.rs:113`, and it is the more consequential of the two by a wide margin, because the whole of the bulk decay is computed from it. It is also the better behaved: it goes through the timestamp interface, so it is clamped at both ends by the type rather than by the caller, which is the conformant row above and the exact contrast with (`entry:assayer:wl-staleness-raw-clock`). What it costs is that the amount of decay a restore applies is a function of when the test ran, so a harness cannot drive a restore to a known decayed state and assert on it. That is why `recovery_decay_applied_to_precision` asserts a direction — precision lower, covariance higher — rather than a value, which is the strongest oracle available without injection and is weaker than the arithmetic deserves.

The third fact is one the lane should have and this cluster cannot fix: the owner's checkpoint timestamp is already injected (`src/owner/thread.rs:344`), so the write half of this path is clocked and the read half is not. The two halves are compared directly at `src/persistence/recovery.rs:115`, subtracting an injected stamp from a raw reading, which is the same seam the extraction tranche found half-repaired at ingestion and warned about. Here the seam carries no comment asserting it is closed, which makes it better than that one.

**Remark (What this cluster's tests pin, and a shape the campaign has not met)** · `rem:assayer:persistence-pins`

Every test driving this cluster was read against its authored claim, and the result is the campaign's most divided so far: this is simultaneously the best-written suite the audit has read and the one whose coverage is furthest from what its claims imply.

The authorship axis holds without exception. Every test in the suite carries a claim, the module's test-index table carries all of them, and the claims are unusually substantive — several state the failure mode the test exists to prevent and argue why it matters, which is more than the policy requires.

One test deserves recording as a positive result, because the campaign has had few. `concurrent_label_preserves_journal_channel_order` drives eight threads at the label surface and asserts three things together: that every submission appears in the journal, that the sequences are strictly increasing, and that the mark the owner subsequently checkpoints at is exactly the last sequence on disk. Its claim states the ordering contract and the failure it excludes — that were the mutex released between writing a record and handing it on, "a thread holding an earlier sequence could be overtaken, the owner would raise its mark past that entry, and a later crash would skip the overtaken label on replay without any trace of having done so". That is an oracle, on a concurrency property, matched to a claim that names the hazard exactly. It is the strongest test the audit has met and it is worth saying so in a document that has mostly had to say the opposite.

Against it stand four pins, and one of them is a shape the campaign has not met.

The stripping claim is argued at (`entry:assayer:wl-persistence-stripped-context`) and is the worst of the four: a claim that names a recomputation that does not happen and argues for the design principle the governing record rejects.

The `open_after` claim is argued at (`entry:assayer:wl-persistence-seq-restart`): a test that proves the uncalled function correct and states why the called one is dangerous.

The third is `journal_truncate_resets_to_zero`, whose claim says the discard "must be total — anything left behind would be replayed a second time on top of a model that already contains it". Read against (`dec:durability:checkpoint-journal`) that argument is half of a true one and the other half is missing: entries below the checkpoint's mark must indeed go, and entries above it must indeed stay, and the claim gives the reason for the first as though it settled the second. So the corpus's suite states the case for whole-file truncation in the one place a reader would look to learn why the journal is emptied, over a defect two notices in the tree already ask to have repaired. It is the extraction tranche's per-axis shape (`entry:assayer:wl-extraction-ledger-axis-grouping`) met once more: a divergence written up as a design decision rather than caught.

The fourth is the new shape, and it is registered at (`entry:assayer:wl-persistence-partial-payload`) rather than here. Six tests establish that Ledger and identity state cross the checkpoint faithfully, every one of them by constructing a populated payload by hand, while the production write site passes empty collections and both readers discard what they are given. None of the six asserts a divergence — each is correct about the function it calls. What they do is establish a capability that production declines to use, and the effect on a reader is stronger than a pinned divergence would be: a pinned divergence at least shows the wrong value, where this shows the right one on a path nothing takes. The campaign's coverage vocabulary has three entries — none, worse than none, and inverted — and this is a fourth: **hollow transfer**, a suite that proves a mechanism works without ever asking whether it runs. It belongs with (`entry:assayer:wl-weak-tests`) as a class rather than as an item, and the question it generalises to is the census question asked of a test rather than of a function: not what does this test establish, but does the path a deployment takes go through it.

**Remark (Two defects in the corpus's own prose)** · `rem:assayer:persistence-prose-defects`

Two are recorded for the record pipeline. Prose defects sit outside the disposition grammar by construction, and neither of these changed a disposition, but the first changed what authority was available to this tranche and is the larger of the two.

The durability record's replay corollary cites a specification invariant about a different mechanism. (`cor:durability:replay-exactness`) derives, from the once-only decay and the sanitisation ordering, that "a recorded assessment replays to the same output", and names that "the specification's replay guarantee (`inv:guarantee:replay`)". The invariant it names is about the derivation landscape: given a stored risk assessment, the channel policy and the challenge posterior of that moment, the landscape is reproducible exactly *without access to any model state*, and the assessment chapter states the same thing in the same terms — "Replay needs three stored values and no component state". That is a property of the derivation surface's purity. The corollary's own premises are about the journal: they concern what a replayed *label* applies to a model, which is the opposite kind of thing, being entirely about model state. The two share a title and nothing else.

The consequence for this audit was concrete and is why the defect is recorded first. The specification states no label-replay guarantee anywhere; the only authority for replay exactness in the sense this cluster realises it is the durability record's own corollary. That is sufficient authority — a landed record is corpus (`conv:assayer:audit-measure`) — and the tranche's headline entry rests on it. But an audit that had taken the citation at face value would have gone to the guarantees appendix for the requirement and found a sentence about landscapes, and would have had to choose between reporting the cluster unspecified and reporting the specification wrong. Neither would have been right. This is the fifth instance of the class the feature tranche opened and the health tranche hardened — a corpus sentence reporting what another part of the corpus says is evidence to be checked, never evidence to be cited — met here between two records rather than between a chapter and the code.

The second is smaller and is the scope map's, recorded at (`rem:assayer:persistence-scope-map`) rather than restated here: a row naming a specification chapter that does not discuss the cluster's subject at all. It joins the health tranche's two rows as evidence that the map's specification column has not been checked against the specification since the chapters were rewritten, and the two tranches together have now found three of its rows wrong. Whoever amends the map should check the column entire rather than the rows this campaign happened to reach.

## Ninth tranche: publication and the pending buffer · `sec:assayer:audit-tranche-snapshot-pending`

The clusters are `src/snapshot/` — the published snapshot type, the shared holder that swaps it, and the working copy that projects into it and is restored from it — and `src/pending/` — the entry types, the journal-stripped context, and the concurrent buffer that holds an assessment until its label arrives. They were taken together because one record governs both and because the two meet at the working copy: the payload conversions that carry state across a restart are `src/snapshot/`'s, and the type those conversions strip is `src/pending/`'s.

They were also taken because three items terminate here. The published snapshot's cold-start constructor was flagged for this tranche by the feature tranche (`rem:assayer:feature-flagged-snapshot`); the journal context's contents were registered by the persistence tranche from the replay side and the type is this cluster's (`entry:assayer:wl-persistence-stripped-context`); and the checkpoint payload's write and read conversions are both in `src/snapshot/working.rs` (`entry:assayer:wl-persistence-partial-payload`). All three are cross-cited below rather than re-registered, and one of them changes a prior tranche's repair.

**Remark (One of these two rows is wrong and the other is incomplete)** · `rem:assayer:snapshot-pending-scope-map`

The scope map gives `snapshot/` the concurrency and ordering records and *the concurrency operations chapter*, and `pending/` the retention record and *the temporal operations chapter* (`tab:assayer:audit-scope-map`). The tranche checked its own rows before trusting them, as the register asked, and neither survives unamended.

The `snapshot/` row is right as far as it goes and is incomplete in the half that matters most. The concurrency chapter does govern this cluster — it defines the published snapshot, it fixes the staleness bounds the snapshot's timestamp is read against, and it states the three guarantees a reader is owed — and both named records govern it too. What the row omits is the **retention** record, which is the one that decides the snapshot's allocation shape, its two exclusions and the second swap beside it (`dec:retention:monolithic-snapshot`). A tranche reading only the row's three sources would have adjudicated this cluster's central design question against the specification alone and would have got it backwards, for the reason recorded at (`rem:assayer:snapshot-pending-prose-defects`). The map assigns retention to `pending/` and nowhere else; it governs both.

The `pending/` row is wrong on the specification side in the same strong sense the persistence tranche found its own row wrong (`rem:assayer:persistence-scope-map`): the temporal operations chapter does not mention the pending buffer, the pending entry, eviction or expiry, and the word *pending* does not occur in it in any casing. What governs this cluster on the specification side is the **label pipeline** chapter, which defines the entry field by field (`def:runtime:pending-entry`), fixes the storage precision (`def:runtime:storage-precision`) and states the capacity rule and the eviction policy (`req:runtime:buffer-capacity`); the concurrency chapter adds the requirement that neither path may delay the other (`req:publication:pending-buffer`); and the configuration reference tabulates the parameters (`tab:config:pending-buffer`). This tranche read those and the retention record, and audited against them.

That makes four of the map's rows now known wrong on the specification side and one known incomplete on the record side, found by three tranches, and none of the five was found by noticing a gap — each was found by going to the named source and reading it. The map's record column has not been checked either, which the `snapshot/` row is the first evidence of. Whoever amends the map should check both columns entire rather than the rows this campaign happened to reach. The audit does not amend the map.

**Table (What the two clusters conform to)** · `tab:assayer:snapshot-pending-conformant`

Recorded first, because an audit that lists only its findings misrepresents what it read. Twenty-eight rows were checked across the two clusters and hold, and the table is the whole of that set rather than a selection from it.

| Checked | Authority | Evidence |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Learned state reaches a reader in exactly one way: an immutable snapshot installed by a single store | (`dec:concurrency:snapshot-swap`) | `src/snapshot/shared.rs:50`, stored at `src/owner/label_path.rs:1012` |
| The snapshot is built whole in one allocation rather than as independently swapped parts | (`dec:retention:monolithic-snapshot`) | `src/snapshot/working.rs:257` |
| The snapshot's fields are the specification's, and they stand in the specification's order | (`def:publication:model-snapshot`) | `src/snapshot/published.rs:106` |
| The precision matrix is absent from the published snapshot; the mean and the covariance are what cross | (`dec:retention:precision-excluded`) | `src/snapshot/published.rs:115` |
| The path that needs the precision matrix reconstructs it from the covariance rather than reading it | (`dec:retention:precision-excluded`) | `src/snapshot/working.rs:300` |
| The checkpoint carries the precision matrix the snapshot excludes, so a restore from a checkpoint needs no inverse and cannot fail on one | (`rem:retention:exclusion-asymmetry`) | `src/snapshot/working.rs:481` against `src/snapshot/working.rs:294` |
| Health is published through a second, independent swap on the same holder rather than inside the model snapshot | (`dec:retention:health-independent`) | `src/snapshot/shared.rs:60` |
| No Companion state appears in the snapshot, on any cadence | (`dec:retention:companion-excluded`) | `src/snapshot/published.rs:106`, by absence |
| A reader loads without taking a lock, and the guard keeps its snapshot alive for the whole of the call | (`cor:concurrency:reader-obligations`) | `src/snapshot/shared.rs:50` |
| Per-axis state crosses into the snapshot carrying its own scale, its own forgetting rate and its own eligibility mode | (`def:publication:model-snapshot`) | `src/snapshot/published.rs:36`, filled at `src/snapshot/working.rs:245` |
| Extension appends across every full-dimension model and marginalisation excises across the same set, the anchor excluded from both | (`def:dimension:anchor-projection`) | `src/snapshot/working.rs:573`, `src/snapshot/working.rs:601` |
| A change to the anchor's fixed width is asserted in debug and warned in release rather than passing unremarked | (`def:dimension:anchor-projection`) | `src/snapshot/working.rs:556` |
| Marginalisation reports its fallback to the caller and names the model that failed rather than returning a bare error | (`alg:dimension:compaction`) | `src/snapshot/working.rs:601` |
| The production cold start derives the model dimension from the dimension map, so the map and the parameters agree at construction | (`inv:dimension:version-consistency`) | `src/snapshot/working.rs:213` |
| An assessment awaiting its label is held in a concurrent map, with the eviction ordering maintained in a separate structure beside it | (`dec:retention:pending-map`) | `src/pending/buffer.rs:84`, `src/pending/buffer.rs:86` |
| Insertion takes no lock over the entry map, and the eviction lock is released before any entry is removed | (`req:publication:pending-buffer`) | `src/pending/buffer.rs:131`, released at `src/pending/buffer.rs:239` |
| Eviction happens on insert, is capped at a fixed count per insert, and no sweep and no background pass exists | (`dec:retention:lazy-eviction`) | `src/pending/buffer.rs:51`, `src/pending/buffer.rs:140` |
| Beyond capacity the oldest entries are the ones evicted, never the newest | (`req:runtime:buffer-capacity`) | `src/pending/buffer.rs:232` |
| Stale identifiers that ordinary labelling leaves in the eviction queue are skipped and are counted against the cap, so the pass terminates | (`dec:retention:lazy-eviction`) | `src/pending/buffer.rs:250` |
| Removal consumes the entry, and a label naming an assessment the buffer no longer holds returns an error rather than an acknowledgement | (`req:runtime:label-interface`) | `src/pending/buffer.rs:152`, `src/api/label.rs:109` |
| A label arriving after its entry was evicted fails the call rather than being silently absorbed | (`cav:limitation:buffer-eviction`) | `src/api/label.rs:109` |
| In-flight features are held at reduced precision on both the per-Sentinel and the signal side | (`dec:retention:reduced-precision`) | `src/pending/entry.rs:64`, `src/pending/entry.rs:168` |
| The entry stores the per-Sentinel extractions separately rather than the assembled vector | (`def:runtime:pending-entry`) | `src/pending/entry.rs:162` |
| No channel field and no stored landscape stands on the entry | (`def:runtime:pending-entry`) | `src/pending/entry.rs:152`, by absence |
| The journal form is a strict subset of the entry rather than the entry | (`dec:retention:journal-subset`) | `src/pending/entry.rs:223` against `src/pending/entry.rs:152` |
| The guidance read neither consumes the entry it returns nor takes the eviction lock | (`req:publication:pending-buffer`) | `src/pending/buffer.rs:161`, `src/pending/buffer.rs:172` |
| The buffer keeps no eviction counter and offers no take-and-clear read, which is what the deferral records | (`entry:retention:eviction-counter`) | `src/pending/buffer.rs:87` |
| Buffer health carries capacity and utilisation and nothing else, which is what the deferral records | (`entry:retention:eviction-visibility`) | `src/api/health.rs:272` |

Three of these deserve a sentence rather than a line.

The whole-allocation row is the tranche's central adjudication and it is recorded as conformance only after the corpus was read on both sides. The specification's snapshot definition describes a componentised publication — independent shared pointers per block, structurally unchanged components shared across versions rather than copied — and the code does none of that: every publication clones the dimension map, all three parameter sets and the drift map into one fresh allocation. Read against that paragraph alone the cluster's most central function is a large divergence. Read against the records it is exact conformance, because the retention record decides the monolith in terms and names the componentised design as the alternative it rejected (`disc:retention:split-swap-alternative`), and the concurrency record states the division of authority that settles which governs: the specification defines the snapshot and states the guarantees, the record decides the arrangement that delivers them. The chapter's paragraph is therefore a prose defect and is recorded as one below; the code is conformant.

The two deferral rows are recorded as conformance with a stated absence, in the sense the health tranche's last four rows established: a record that says a counter does not exist is not contradicted by the counter not existing. Both were re-verified independently of the deferral pass, and both of that pass's pins (`tab:assayer:deferral-pins`) stand at the lines the owning record names.

And the checkpoint's inclusion of the precision matrix is recorded because it is the exact complement of the snapshot's exclusion, and the two are easy to read as an inconsistency. They are not one. Publishing and persisting are different questions and the record separates them explicitly; the consequence in the code is that the restore from a snapshot can fail on an inverse and returns a result (`src/snapshot/working.rs:294`) while the restore from a checkpoint cannot and does not (`src/snapshot/working.rs:470`), which is the asymmetry working as designed.

**Entry (A lifecycle event publishes neutral standardisation over the empirical statistics it has just maintained)** · `entry:assayer:wl-snapshot-lifecycle-standardisation`

**Locus** `src/snapshot/working.rs:267` and `src/snapshot/working.rs:268`, where the projection fills the standardisation vectors with zeros and ones rather than with anything; the publication that does not repair the fill is `src/owner/lifecycle.rs:150`, storing at `src/owner/lifecycle.rs:151`. A labelled notice stood over the fill, asking for the projection to be made canonical, and the wave-four repair took it with the defect it disclosed.

**Disposition** divergent-code-wrong against (`def:publication:model-snapshot`), which fixes the standardisation means and variances as contents of the published snapshot, and against (`inv:guarantee:lifecycle-publication`), whose closing sentence is the one this breaks in terms: structural changes publish exactly as parameter changes do, "and the standardisation entries a lifecycle event creates move under the same publication".

The projection cannot carry the statistics, and that is the structural root rather than an oversight at one call site. The working copy does not hold them: they live on the model owner beside it (`src/owner/thread.rs:81`), so `to_snapshot` has nothing to project and writes the neutral pair sized to the map. Every publication site is therefore obliged to patch the result afterwards, which the notice says in exactly those words. Three of the four production sites do — the label path at `src/owner/label_path.rs:967`, the batch initialisation at `src/owner/thread.rs:477`, and the cold-start publication at `src/lib.rs:403`, where neutral is the correct value. The lifecycle path does not.

**The observable sequence.** Run an instance until its standardisation statistics are empirical. Register a Sentinel. The lifecycle path extends the owner's standardisation vectors correctly for the new positions (`src/owner/lifecycle.rs:309`), resizes them to the new width (`src/owner/lifecycle.rs:135`), asserts that the map and the model agree, and publishes a snapshot in which every mean is zero and every variance is one. From that store until the next label completes, every assessment standardises every feature by dividing by one and subtracting nothing, and scores the result against models trained on standardised positions. The interval is bounded by nothing the package controls: it is however long the deployment takes to receive its next label, which for a host that registers a Sentinel during a quiet period is arbitrarily long.

**The compounding that settles the severity.** The neutral values do not merely sit in the published snapshot until a label overwrites them. A checkpoint-five or checkpoint-six revert restores the owner's live statistics *from the last published snapshot* (`src/owner/label_path.rs:1086` and `src/owner/label_path.rs:1089`), guarded only on the lengths matching — which they do, both being the map's width. So a revert firing on the first label after a lifecycle event copies the neutral pair back over the owner's empirical accumulators, and the loss stops being transient and becomes permanent for the life of the process. Three functions, each defensible read alone: a projection that neutral-fills because it has nothing to project, a publication that does not patch, and a revert that trusts the published snapshot as the authority on state the published snapshot was never given. This is the shape the register names as the campaign's highest-severity generator (`reg:assayer:audit-tranches`), met across three files with a corpus requirement between them.

**Severity** high. It is silent, it fires on an ordinary host operation the concurrency chapter promises is free to perform at peak traffic, and it mis-scales every feature every model reads for its duration. It is argued above medium on the revert path alone: a transient window closed by the next label would be a medium, and a window that can be made permanent by a mechanism whose whole purpose is recovery is not. It compounds with the two registered standardisation items in the same direction — (`entry:assayer:wl-feature-bootstrap-unrun`) removes the per-Sentinel bootstrap and (`entry:assayer:wl-persistence-warm-start-standardisation`) removes the batch initialisation on every restart — so the mechanism left to repair the damage is the slowest of the three, and a deployment that registers entities as often as it restarts is never empirically standardised at all.

**What conformance would mean** the running means and variances move onto the working copy, beside the dimension map they are indexed against, so that the projection is canonical and no call site patches anything. That is what the notice asks for, and it is the same relocation two persistence entries need for a different reason, argued at (`rem:assayer:snapshot-pending-cross-cites`). Failing that, the lifecycle publication patches as its three siblings do, which is two lines and closes the window without closing the class.

**Coverage** none, and the suite argues the other way. No test in the workspace publishes from the lifecycle path and inspects the published standardisation vectors. What the suite does hold is `cold_start_optional_fields_present`, whose authored claim states that the feature statistics are "a full-length vector of zero means and unit variances sized to the layout they standardise" and argues that this is right because "standardising against these values is the identity, so the same code path serves a day-old model and a minute-old one". Every word of that is true of a cold-start snapshot and it is the corpus's own argument for the line that neutralises the statistics at every later publication, stated in the place a reader would look to learn why the line is there. Beside it stands `to_snapshot_scalars`, which establishes that the calibration scalars cross "as they stand, not as defaults" — the property the standardisation vectors do not have, asserted of their neighbours and asked of them nowhere.

**Sequencing** shares one prerequisite with (`entry:assayer:wl-persistence-partial-payload`) and (`entry:assayer:wl-persistence-warm-start-standardisation`): the relocation of the standardisation statistics from the owner onto the working copy (`rem:assayer:snapshot-pending-cross-cites`). Three entries registered as independent repairs are one relocation and three follow-ons, and scheduling them apart would do the relocation three times or block two of them behind the first.

**Repaired** in wave four at `408324c4`, by the relocation the notice asked for: the running means, variances and class assignments moved onto the working copy beside the dimension map they are indexed against, `to_snapshot` projects them canonically, and every call site that patched the published snapshot lost its patch. The falsifier read the published statistics through a lifecycle barrier after a completed Sentinel bootstrap blend — neutral before the repair, the blend preserved after — and the CP5/CP6 revert now restores standardisation the published snapshot actually carries, which closes the compounding this entry's severity was argued from. One asymmetry the relocation could not remove: the snapshot carries no class vector, so the revert preserves the live classes across `from_snapshot` rather than restoring them, until the class assignment is derived from the map under the standing notice.

**Entry (The two swaps cannot deliver the ordering the record decides, and the type concedes it)** · `entry:assayer:wl-snapshot-health-skew`
