This is a to-do-adjacent documentation repair rather than a behaviour change, and the behaviour it describes is conformant as it stands.

**Coverage** none. No test submits a label against a failing journal and inspects what a second submission returns.

**Table (The two clusters' remaining divergences)** · `tab:assayer:api-guidance-remaining`

| Divergence | Authority | Locus | Severity |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The lifecycle wait discards the steward's own per-event result, so a deregistration whose marginalisation failed returns success and the infrastructure is removed anyway | (`dec:construction:blocking-deregistration`) | `src/api/lifecycle.rs:502` | medium |
| The scan limit's tabulated lower bound is enforced nowhere, while its sibling row's is sanitised in the same function | (`tab:config:guidance`) | `src/guidance/mod.rs:98` | low |
| Two figures these surfaces reason about are parameters of no group of the construction contract, against a record that claims every one of them is | (`cav:surface:no-local-figures`) | `src/guidance/mod.rs:51`, `src/api/lifecycle.rs:38` | low |
| The order the surface record states for the boundary — sanitise, then consume, then journal — is not the code's, which consumes first; and the record attributes the three-way order to a record that fixes only two of its terms | (`dec:surface:consume-at-boundary`) | `src/api/label.rs:106` against `src/api/label.rs:112` | low |
| A lifecycle send that fails after the maintenance thread has created the dimension reverts the local infrastructure and leaves the maintenance thread's copy standing, as a second path in the same method already concedes | (`dec:construction:two-phase-visibility`) | `src/api/lifecycle.rs:354`, conceded at `src/api/lifecycle.rs:337` | low |
| Three documentation sites state that persistence setup tests write access, and the function creates directories and tests nothing — succeeding on an existing directory that cannot be written | (`dec:construction:eager-validation`) | `src/api/builder.rs:370`, `src/api/builder.rs:9`, `src/api/builder.rs:111` | low |
| The pre-seed flush uses a checkpoint as its barrier and discards the checkpoint's own result, so a durable write the host did not ask for can fail inside a call that returns success | (`dec:durability:checkpoint-journal`) | `src/api/preseed.rs:151` | low |

The two discarded-result rows are the same shape at two weights and are tabled together for that reason. The lifecycle one is a medium because the value discarded is the answer to the question the call is blocking to ask: the method waits precisely so that it can remove the infrastructure knowing the model has stopped naming the dimension, and it then removes the infrastructure without reading whether it has. The pre-seed one is a low because the checkpoint is the barrier rather than the purpose, and no host asked for it.

The unenforced scan limit is tabled rather than registered because the surface record already fixes the conformant repair. Guidance "sanitises implausible parameters rather than refusing them" (`dec:surface:read-only-guidance`), and the starvation threshold is sanitised in the very function that leaves the scan limit alone, so the repair is one clause in an existing expression and the divergence is an asymmetry rather than an absence of policy.

**Remark (The census in all three forms, and the gaps the corpus leaves)** · `rem:assayer:api-guidance-census`

The register asked that the census question be put hardest at a cluster that moves data between every other, and that where a cluster hands two differently-shaped payloads to one consumer the question be asked of the narrower one (`reg:assayer:audit-tranches`). Both applied here and the answers divide by form in a way the campaign has not seen before: the first two forms returned almost nothing across nine modules, and the third and fourth returned two of this wave's five entries.

By call-site census `api/` is the cleanest cluster the campaign has audited, and the reason is structural rather than creditable. Every public method on it is `pub` on `Assayer` and is therefore reachable by definition; the cluster has no private machinery to be uncalled, because its private helpers are three — the label sanitiser, the pre-seed pair, and the two lifecycle senders — and all five are called from the method above them. `guidance/` answers the same way for the same reason: one public entry point and a scoring tree beneath it, every node of which the entry point reaches. `flush_label_channel` is the one function worth naming, and naming it is what the question's second half is for: it is crate-private, has exactly one production caller (`src/api/preseed.rs:116`), and is reached from some two dozen test sites and from the test harness's own world (`src/testing/world.rs:737`). It is not an uncalled surface and it is not a hollow one — pre-seeding genuinely calls it — but its production fan-in is one and its test fan-in is two orders larger, which is the profile of a function whose tests are exercising a synchronisation convenience rather than a specified path.

The third form — does anything ever put anything into it — returned the pre-seed finding above, and it returned it from the opposite direction to the ninth tranche's. There the field was filled and never read; here the fields are read, by the reconstruction the label path performs, and are never filled. A call-site census sees a helper that is called on every seeded entry and constructs a complete-looking value; only asking what is in the value establishes it.

The fourth form — does anything ever take anything out, and what happens on the path where the answer is no — returned the field the ninth tranche predicted it would. `PreSeedEntry.channel` (`src/api/preseed.rs:45`) is a public field on a public type, set by every construction site in the workspace including the test harness's own builder (`src/testing/preseed_spec.rs:35`), and read by nothing: neither of the two helpers that consume the entry names it, and no other module in the package or the suite reads it. The corpus is unusually direct about it — the pre-seeding algorithm closes by saying what ships takes its path "over an entry type carrying a channel field the Core is specified not to have" (`alg:host:pre-seeding`) — and the notice standing on the field concedes it (`todo:code:remove-this-field-core-pre-seeding`). It is recorded here rather than as an entry because the concession, the corpus sentence and the notice agree and the repair is a deletion; what the census adds is that the field is not merely unread but *written on every path*, so its removal is a source change at half a dozen sites and not one.

Six gaps the corpus leaves are recorded here rather than as entries.

The identity registration's ten-second timeout is **unspecified**, and so is the fact of the wait. The construction record decides that identity deregistration "is the one lifecycle call that waits on the steward" (`dec:construction:blocking-deregistration`), which holds exactly — no other method sends a completion channel. What the record does not reach is that registration also blocks, on a different thread, for up to ten seconds (`src/api/lifecycle.rs:326`), because the infrastructure the two-phase visibility decision requires to exist synchronously cannot exist until the maintenance thread has built it. That is a designed consequence of a decision the corpus makes, and nothing in the corpus bounds it or names the bound. A host sizing its lifecycle latency from the record's sentence will size it wrong, and the audit records the reader hazard without converting it into a divergence, because the record's own claim is about the steward and is true.

The guidance lock budget is **unspecified**. Five hundred and twelve read-lock acquisitions per Sentinel per call (`src/guidance/mod.rs:51`) is the code's own choice; the surface record requires that "every scan guidance performs is explicitly bounded, and so is every lock it takes" (`dec:surface:bounded-scans`) and names no figure, and no corpus text does. Two things about it are worth stating. The bound is on the *count* of acquisitions rather than on the duration of any hold or of any wait, and the record's word is "bounded" without saying bounded in what — so a guidance call contending with a report ingestion on the same Sentinel's Ledger waits an unbounded time, which the record neither permits nor forbids. And the exhaustion behaviour is conformant where it might not have been: an entry past the budget scores zero, a zero score is filtered before selection (`src/guidance/mod.rs:252`), and the result is therefore the shorter list the record asks for rather than a wrongly-ranked full one. That check came back negative and is recorded as one.

The guidance scan's selection remains **unspecified** and this cluster is the consumer half of the gap the ninth tranche recorded at the buffer (`rem:assayer:snapshot-pending-census`). The lock budget is a second mechanism by which the same non-determinism reaches the output: which Sentinels a scan can afford to consult depends on the hash order the buffer yielded, so two scans of one buffer may consult disjoint Ledgers as well as disjoint entries. Nothing changes about the disposition and the mechanism is recorded beside the first.

The poison policy is **unspecified**, and the two clusters answer it in opposite ways over the same lock. The health report takes a Ledger read lock and panics on poison (`src/api/health.rs:193`); guidance takes the same lock and recovers the inner value (`src/guidance/mod.rs:282`). The failure posture decides what happens when a *numerical* operation cannot complete (`dec:degradation:retain-and-flag`) and the error partition decides which failures are hard (`dec:degradation:error-partition`); neither reaches a poisoned lock, which is neither a contract violation by the caller nor a data-quality problem. The disagreement is the finding rather than either behaviour, and it is the same shape the ledger tranche recorded between two routing siblings (`entry:assayer:wl-ledger-rootless-disagreement`) — two functions over one structure answering the same impossible input incompatibly, with no documentation on which a caller could choose. The health method's panic is at least disclosed in its own panics section; the guidance method's recovery is not disclosed at all, and it is the one on the surface the corpus calls infallible.

The pre-seed result's failure count is **unspecified**. The field exists, is documented as "currently always 0 because failures are propagated as `LabelError`" (`src/api/preseed.rs:62`), and is filled with a literal (`src/api/preseed.rs:123`). The corpus fixes no result shape for pre-seeding at all — the algorithm states what the path does and not what it returns — so a field that is structurally constant is a gap rather than a violation. It is worth recording because it is the fourth surfaced constant the campaign has found on a public surface, and the only one of the four whose own documentation states that it is one.

And whether a pre-seed batch publishes once at its end is **unspecified in effect**, for a reason the corpus supplies itself. The algorithm says that "where the publication interval permits, a pre-seed batch publishes once at its end rather than once per historical label", and the code publishes per label because each entry travels the ordinary path. The antecedent is never satisfied: the publication interval is a surface the package does not offer, which the configuration reference concedes in terms and the concurrency chapter states outright — "No Core configuration carries this interval in what ships and the label path publishes unconditionally" (`req:publication:interval`). A conditional whose condition the corpus records as unreachable is not violated by the consequent not holding, and the audit records the conditional rather than manufacturing a row from it.

Neither cluster carries a dead-code suppression of any kind, per file or per module, which is the first time the campaign has been able to write that sentence about a cluster pair. The stale-exemption shape recorded three times by the persistence and pending tranches does not occur here.

**Remark (The items terminating in these clusters, cross-cited, and one repair sharpened)** · `rem:assayer:api-guidance-cross-cites`

Seven registered items terminate in or cross these clusters. None is re-registered; all were re-verified at their recorded lines, six stand exactly, one has two line citations corrected, and two carry facts this cluster is the first to be able to add.

(`entry:assayer:wl-health-sync-error-constant`) stands, and this tranche owns the drop site and adds the second one. The health tranche's account is exact on every particular this cluster can check: the literal is at `src/api/health.rs:51`, it is bound into the summary at `src/api/health.rs:71`, and the per-model precision health type carries no field for the quantity. What the entry could not see from the health cluster is that the drop happens **twice, in two tiers, for two different reasons**. The summary tier binds the literal one line below a fold that is the exact shape the sync error needs — `max_kappa_estimate` is computed as a maximum over the same per-model health map at `src/api/health.rs:47`, and the literal stands at `src/api/health.rs:51` immediately beneath it. The detailed tier iterates those same per-model records with the record in hand (`src/api/health.rs:137`) and declines to read the field under a second notice, ``todo:code:populate-sync-error``, which asks for the population "once `PrecisionHealthDetail` exposes them". So the entry's conformance line — "assembly takes the maximum across the models it already iterates" — is more nearly written than it reads: the iteration exists, the fold pattern exists one line from the literal, and what is missing is one field on one type. The repair is smaller than the entry states, which is the fourth time the campaign has narrowed a conformance line by reading the cluster that owns the mechanism, and the first time the narrowing has been to a single field.

(`entry:assayer:wl-sentinel-informativeness`) stands, and its locus needs one digit. The literal is at `src/api/health.rs:217` and the notice standing over it opens at `src/api/health.rs:213`; the entry records the locus as `src/api/health.rs:218`, which is the line below the value. Everything else in the entry is confirmed at those lines, including that the slot ranges and the weights the computation needs are both reachable from the loop the literal sits in. The deferral pass's own pin cites `src/api/health.rs:217` and is exact (`tab:assayer:deferral-pins`).

(`entry:assayer:wl-ledger-absence-cast`) stands on its disposition, its severity and its conformance line, and **two of its three line citations are corrected here, in the direction that leaves the finding stronger.** The entry says the builder's numerical pass is at `src/api/builder.rs:171` and reaches its one Ledger field at `src/api/builder.rs:266`. Neither is right against the tree this campaign audits: `src/api/builder.rs:171` is the notice above the template validator and `src/api/builder.rs:266` is the factorisation conditioning trigger. The pass itself begins at `src/api/builder.rs:195`, and the one Ledger field it reaches is the accumulator's decay at `src/api/builder.rs:290`. The substantive claim was re-derived rather than transcribed — every branch of the pass was read against the configuration type — and it holds exactly as written: one Ledger field is validated, the absence threshold is not among them, and the narrowing cast at `src/api/report.rs:83` stands under an exemption at `src/api/report.rs:81` whose justification names a guard that does not exist. The correction states its own falsifier: it is wrong if any branch of `validate_numerical_parameters` reads `config.ledger` other than at `src/api/builder.rs:290`, and none does.

(`entry:assayer:wl-inert-knobs`) stands at `src/guidance/mod.rs:80` and this tranche owns both halves of it — the flag on the public parameter type and the scorer that takes it with an underscore at `src/guidance/mod.rs:235`. One fact is added and it makes the entry's own characterisation half wrong in a way worth recording. The entry calls this knob inert *and disclosed*, on the ground that the legacy guidance record says the implemented scorer is the same proxy in both modes, and contrasts it with the calibration constant, which is inert and undisclosed. The disclosure is in the legacy record; the **specification conceals it**. The guidance chapter's interface signature tabulates the parameter set as two members, the scan limit and the starvation threshold, and closes: "The interface is implemented as specified, down to the field names" (`sig:guidance:interface`). The shipped parameter set has three members, the third is not in the table, and it is the inert one. So a reader consulting the corpus's authoritative half about this interface is told the field set matches and is not told that a field exists which selects nothing. The severity is unchanged — low, on the same reasoning — but the entry's disclosure argument now rests on a legacy record alone, and the disclosure difference it draws against the calibration constant is narrower than it was written.

(`entry:assayer:wl-ledger-starvation-window`) stands exactly at `src/guidance/mod.rs:42`, and this tranche confirms the two facts the ledger tranche established from the other side: the value is the tabulated two hundred, and the package offers no configuration field for it. Two are added. The constant has an integer twin at `src/guidance/mod.rs:45` used to cap the count before the division, so the repair is at two sites rather than one. And the entry's coverage line — that the score is tested against the constant, which would pass for any value the constant took — is confirmed and is true of two tests rather than one: `starvation_scorer_requires_divergence_and_starvation` asserts a score at a count of three and a saturation at a count of two hundred, and `starvation_scorer_reads_routed_ledger_entry` asserts the same figure through the routing. The second is the stronger test and it inherits the same blindness.

(`entry:assayer:wl-persistence-stripped-context`) stands, and this cluster holds the conversion's only production call site. The journal entry is built at `src/api/label.rs:133` from the sanitised payload and the stripped context, which is the one place in the package where the narrower of the two payloads is manufactured, and it is manufactured on the ordinary label path rather than on any special one. The ninth tranche's account of the type and the persistence tranche's of the replay are both confirmed from this end, and nothing is added beyond the call site's identity: the conversion is reached once per label, under the journal mutex, with the full entry in hand and about to be sent whole down the channel three statements later (`src/api/label.rs:140`). That adjacency is worth recording because it shows the cost of the omission is not the entry's construction — the full entry exists at the moment the stripped one is written — but the journal's own width.

(`entry:assayer:wl-batch-timestamp-split`) stands, and the half that lives here is conformant. The monotonic reading is taken once at the batch's head (`src/api/assess.rs:44`) and threaded to every request, which is exactly what (`dec:ordering:batch-timestamp`) requires and is the record's own cited site. The entry's locus is the persistent reading taken per request inside the assessment, at `src/assessment.rs:795`, which this tranche does not own and does not touch. The two halves being in two clusters is why the entry reads as though the record were kept: at this cluster's site it is.

**Remark (What these clusters' tests pin, and a claim that outlives its body)** · `rem:assayer:api-guidance-pins`

Every test driving either cluster was read against its authored claim. The authorship axis holds without exception in both clusters — every test carries a claim, and both modules with inline suites carry test-index tables listing all of theirs — and the picture the two clusters give is the campaign's most divided so far.

`guidance/` is the best-covered cluster the campaign has audited, and the judgment is meant literally rather than as a comparison of counts. Its six inline tests reach six different functions, each asserts a value recomputed from the chapter's own formula rather than from the code, and between them they establish the three scoring criteria, the routing, the ordering and the cross-membership policy — which is the whole of what the guidance chapter states of this cluster bar the interface's field list. Two of the six are worth naming for shape rather than for defect. `starvation_scorer_requires_divergence_and_starvation` asserts one positive score and both of the two zeroing conditions, which is the corpus's own argument for the product form tested as three cases rather than asserted as one. And `cross_membership_marks_other_selected_categories` constructs the membership state directly and asserts both directions of the exclusion, so the "never itself" clause of (`dec:guidance:no-deduplication`) is pinned rather than implied. The hollow-transfer question was asked of both clusters and both answer cleanly: every function either cluster's tests exercise is on a path a deployment takes, the guidance entry point is reached from the public method and from four suites, and the scoring functions the inline tests call directly are the same functions the entry point calls.

`api/` answers the authorship question as cleanly and the strength question far worse, and the divide falls exactly where the specification's weight does. The four label tests are the cluster's only inline suite and they are sound: each asserts a repaired value against an independently stated expectation, and their claims are accurate about what the bodies establish. Everything else this cluster holds is covered from the external suites, and the coverage is of the shape the founding worklist's (`entry:assayer:wl-weak-tests`) names. Of the eleven pre-seed tests, seven assert only that the call returned success or that the processed count equals the number of entries submitted, which is a tally of the loop rather than of anything the loop did; the three lifecycle-integration tests that go further are the three read against (`entry:assayer:wl-preseed-degenerate-vector`) above.

One claim outlives its body in a way that deserves the register's attention, because it is a different failure from the two the campaign has classified. `pre_seed_then_assess_reflects_learning` carries the claim that "pre-seeded history is genuinely learned from rather than merely counted, which is what makes it worth loading at all". The body seeds two engines with opposite valences and asserts that the adversely seeded one returns the higher probability. The assertion holds, the claim's first clause is true of the intercept, and the claim's second clause — the reason it gives for why the property matters — is false of the shipped path, because what makes pre-seeding worth loading is the feature-space learning the entry above establishes does not happen. This is neither a hollow transfer, which proves a mechanism that does not run, nor the sufficiency fiction the ninth tranche separated from it, which proves a mechanism that runs and is not enough. It is a third thing: a claim whose assertion is established, whose stated significance is not, and which therefore reads as evidence for a property no test in the workspace touches. The register should expect it wherever a claim explains why a property matters, because the explanation is the part no assertion checks.

Two smaller pins. `pre_seed_before_sentinel_registration` pins half of that same entry's defect as its expected behaviour, in its claim rather than only in its body, which is the seventh instance the campaign has met of a test defending a divergence against repair. And `flush_label_channel_idempotent` asserts that three consecutive flushes succeed under the claim that "a host that flushes defensively before reading state pays nothing for the flushes that turn out to be unnecessary" — the body establishes that they return success and observes nothing about what they cost, and each of the three sends a checkpoint request through the command channel, which is not nothing.

No duplicate pair was found in either cluster. Every test named for a function in `api/` or `guidance/` was read against every other, and the campaign's largest axis of failure at the previous tranche does not occur at this one.

**Remark (Three defects in the corpus's own prose, one of which would have inverted this tranche's largest verdict)** · `rem:assayer:api-guidance-prose-defects`

Three are recorded for the record pipeline. The first is the second-largest the campaign has met, measured by what trusting it would have cost, and it is the second instance of the class the ninth tranche opened.

**The concurrency chapter requires a label-queue overflow policy that two records forbid, and argues against the policy the code has.** Its label-queue requirement states that the queue "is first-in-first-out and drops the oldest entry on overflow", and it argues the choice: "Dropping the oldest rather than refusing the newest is the right choice under a non-blocking discipline: refusing would push back on the caller, and a caller that must not be blocked cannot be pushed back on" (`req:publication:label-queue`). The code refuses the newest. A full channel returns an error to the submitter and nothing already queued is disturbed (`src/api/label.rs:162`, and `src/api/label.rs:149` on the journalled path).

Read against that paragraph alone the label surface's overflow handling is a divergence against a requirement, and the requirement's own closing sentence would have made it worse rather than better: it concedes that "the drop accounting is not [shipped], so the one signal that would tell a host it is losing labels is the part that is missing", which under refuse-newest is exactly backwards — the host is told, per call, by a typed error.

Read against the records it is exact conformance, and two records say so independently. The concurrency record decides that the label queue does not drop silently (`dec:concurrency:no-silent-drop`), and the degradation record names that decision as the deliberate exception to its own posture and gives its reason: "The label queue is the deliberate exception and is decided elsewhere: overflow there returns an error because a dropped label is a submitter's loss and the submitter can act on it" (`rem:degradation:queue-face`). The surface record's own design depends on the same answer: the pending entry is consumed before the enqueue (`dec:surface:consume-at-boundary`), so a label dropped from the queue after acknowledgement would have had its entry destroyed and its host told success, which is precisely the silent loss the acknowledgement's meaning is defined to exclude (`dec:surface:async-label`). Three records agree and one chapter requirement disagrees.

What settles it is the division of authority the ninth tranche derived from the corpus's own statement of it and the register adopted as a rule: where a specification chapter describes an arrangement rather than requiring a property, the record that owns arrangements is the authority (`rem:assayer:snapshot-pending-prose-defects`). The property this chapter is protecting is non-blocking assessment, and the chapter's own argument shows that the property does not distinguish the two designs — it treats "refusing" as equivalent to "pushing back on the caller", and a non-blocking refusal returns immediately and pushes back on nobody. The arrangement is the record's to decide and the record decided it in the opposite direction, with a stated reason the chapter does not address. The disposition is conformance and the chapter's paragraph is a prose defect.

It is recorded first, and at this length, because it is the exact shape the register warned the remaining tranches to expect and because the cost of missing it would have been symmetrical with the ninth tranche's: there, trusting a chapter would have reported the cluster's most central function as its largest finding; here, trusting a chapter would have reported the label surface's error handling as a divergence against a requirement, when the error handling is the thing three records built the surface around. It is emphatically **not** a spec-stale disposition and does not claim to be one: no supersession is argued, the code is not offered as the measure, and the burden (`conv:assayer:audit-measure`) places on a finding that would call a document stale is not discharged and is not invoked. What is invoked is the corpus's own allocation of which of its halves decides an arrangement.

**The interface appendix reproduces the defect it was rewritten to remove.** Its opening paragraph names three interfaces — "the one between a Sentinel and the Core, the one between a Sentinel and the graph it observes into, and the one between the Core and the host" — and criticises the superseded text for "describing itself as a complete interface map while enumerating two of its three interfaces", restoring the missing one for the stated reason that "an inventory that omits a boundary cannot be checked against the invariant it exists to support" (`app:spec:interface-map`). The appendix then tabulates what the Core consumes from a Sentinel, what it does not consume, the graph interface, a symbol correspondence, and a six-row boundary verification — and the Core-to-host interface, the third of the three it names, is enumerated in none of them. Its six verification rows are Core-to-Sentinel, Sentinel-to-graph, derivation-to-Core, derivation-to-Companion, Companion-and-Core, and Core-to-a-Sentinel-graph; the boundary across which every call this tranche audits passes appears only in a sentence of prose observing that "the only cycle in the system passes through the host". The consequence for this campaign is concrete rather than aesthetic and is argued at (`rem:assayer:api-guidance-scope-map`): the scope map sent this tranche to the appendix because the appendix is the document's interface inventory, and the one interface it does not inventory is this cluster's.

The third is smaller and is the guidance chapter's, and it is the fourth instance of a corpus sentence reporting what the code does being wrong. "The interface is implemented as specified, down to the field names" (`sig:guidance:interface`) closes a signature whose parameter group tabulates two members against a shipped parameter type carrying three, the third of which reaches no behaviour and is already registered (`entry:assayer:wl-inert-knobs`). The sentence is the strongest form of the assurance class — not merely that the interface is implemented, but that it matches down to the names — attached to the one place in this cluster where the field set does not match. The rule the health tranche hardened applies unchanged: a corpus sentence reporting what the code does is evidence to be checked, never evidence to be cited.

## Eleventh tranche: the posterior and its substrate · `sec:assayer:audit-tranche-model-linalg`

The clusters are `src/model/` — the model type, the Sherman–Morrison update, the Schur marginalisation, the recomputation policy and the parameter snapshot — and `src/linalg/` — the symmetric wrapper, the backend bridge, the conversion helpers and the hand-written persisted form. They were taken together because the posterior record governs both and cites across the boundary in four of its decisions, and because every quantity the first cluster computes is produced by an operation the second owns. Two thousand eight hundred and twenty-seven lines, below the read-whole threshold, so both clusters were read entire and no reconnaissance lane was opened.

This is the campaign's mathematical core, and the tranche's method was adjusted for that: where a claim turned on algebra, the algebra was recomputed rather than compared. That produced two findings the reading alone would have missed and refuted two candidate findings the reading alone would have produced, and both directions are recorded below because an audit that reports only its survivors has not shown its method working.

**Remark (Both scope-map columns fail again, and the record column fails twice)** · `rem:assayer:model-linalg-scope-map`

Four tranches had checked their own rows and four had amended them; this is the fifth and sixth, and both fail on the record side while one fails on the specification side as well.

The `model/` row names the governing records as posterior and calibration. The posterior record governs the cluster and governs it densely — eleven of its fifteen decisions cite a file in it. The calibration record governs no line of it: its decisions cite `src/risk/calibration.rs`, `src/risk/blend.rs`, `src/numerics.rs` and `src/owner/lifecycle.rs`, and not one cites `src/model/`. A tranche trusting the row would have read a record with nothing to say about its cluster and would have missed the two that do — the substrate record, whose symmetry and dual-tracking decisions are the premise every model operation assumes, and the degradation record, from which the posterior record inherits its failure posture by explicit citation rather than restating it (`rem:degradation:numerics-face`). The row's specification column names the risk core-model chapters, which do hold the update algorithm and the leverage bound; what they do not hold is the marginalisation, the extension, the regularised Schur complement, the positive-definiteness requirement, the replenishment floor or the recomputation trigger, all of which are the Gaussian foundations chapter's. The row is right about where a third of the cluster's authority lives and silent about the rest.

The `linalg/` row names substrate and the Gaussian foundations chapter, and both are correct as far as they go. What the row omits is the posterior record, which cites `src/linalg/bridge.rs` in four decisions — the repair cascade, the cascade's terminus, the one inversion utility, and the debug oracle — so the record that decides the bridge's whole behaviour is absent from the row that governs the bridge. This is the second row found wanting on the record side by omission rather than by error, and the shape is the same as the `api/` row's.

The row also bundles `numerics.rs`, which this tranche did not take. The file's governing decision is the calibration record's shared-transition decision, which cites it by name; so the scope map has assigned `numerics.rs` to the record that does not govern it and the record that does govern it to the cluster it does not touch, in adjacent rows. Whichever tranche takes `config/` should take `numerics.rs` with it, and the register's completeness check should not read this tranche's row as closing the bundled file.

**Remark (What the algebra returned that the reading would not have)** · `rem:assayer:model-linalg-recomputation`

Four claims in these clusters turn on algebra rather than on control flow, and recomputing them separated two real findings from two apparent ones.

The Sherman–Morrison update is exact. Step six's shipped rearrangement — a scale-and-rank-one call at $s = 1/\gamma$ and $\alpha = -w/(\gamma(\gamma + wh))$ — expands to $\frac{1}{\gamma}\bigl(\Sigma - \frac{w vv^\top}{\gamma + wh}\bigr)$, which is the specified step six character for character, and step seven's coefficient $w(r - \hat\phi^\top\mu)/(\gamma + wh)$ against the prior product $v$ is the posterior covariance times the weighted residual with the identity substituted, since $\Sigma_\text{post}\hat\phi = v/(\gamma + wh)$. The identity the posterior record names as looking like a transcription error (`dec:posterior:identity-substitution`) is present and is exact, and the residual is computed before the mean is mutated so that the prior mean is the one used. Conformant, and the record's warning to a reader comparing code against specification is well earned.

The half-solve is exact. $W = L^{-1}B_{rk}$ gives $W^\top W = B_{kr}(LL^\top)^{-1}B_{rk} = B_{kr}(B_{rr} + \delta I)^{-1}B_{rk}$, which is the correction the regularised form specifies (`alg:gaussian:regularised-schur`), and the Gram arrangement is positive semi-definite by construction as the code claims. Conformant.

The rank-one condition estimate is not a defect and its comment is. On the single-index path the estimate is set to unity for any positive finite pivot, which is correct — a scalar's condition number is one — so the guard cannot fire there on conditioning and fires only on a non-positive or non-finite pivot, while still reporting itself as a conditioning skip. The estimate is right; the diagnostic label attached to it is wrong, and the completion trigger the deferral names would count these as conditioning skips.

The general condition estimate's comment inverts its own direction. The estimate is $\max_j B_{jj} / \min_j B_{jj}$, and for a symmetric positive-definite matrix $\lambda_\max \geq \max_j B_{jj}$ and $\lambda_\min \leq \min_j B_{jj}$, so the estimate is a lower bound on the true condition number, as the comment at `src/model/recompute.rs:299` says. What follows from a lower bound is the opposite of what the comment concludes: an estimate that never exceeds the truth can never raise a false alarm and can miss a real one, so the trigger fires late or not at all, never early. The comment states "may trigger early, never late". The estimate itself is the specified one (`def:monitoring:condition-number`) and is conformant; the sentence describing its failure direction is a defect against the arithmetic, and it is the sentence a maintainer deciding whether the trigger is safe would read.

**Remark (The knot at the covariance after marginalisation)** · `rem:assayer:model-linalg-refactor-knot`

The corpus's two halves state incompatible arrangements for the covariance after a dimension leaves, and untying the knot produced the tranche's clearest code finding and its clearest record finding at once. It is recorded as a remark rather than as an entry because it resolves into one of each.

The record is absolute. After a marginalisation the covariance is recomputed by factorisation and "is never taken from the block extracted during the correction, even where that block is numerically the same quantity" (`dec:posterior:always-refactor`), on a provenance argument, and its cost premise is that refactoring "costs one factorisation per lifecycle event, which is rare".

The specification is two-branched. It scales the remedy to the operation: for general marginalisation of larger blocks, recompute $\Sigma' = (B')^{-1}$ by Cholesky; for low-rank marginalisation with small $r$ — "competitive cell exit being the case that occurs often" — the covariance path may extract $\Sigma_{kk}$ directly, the extra discrepancy being of the order of the regularisation and cleared by the periodic schedule (`rem:gaussian:dual-tracking`). Its cost table prices the two branches apart on exactly that basis, at $O(p'^3)$ for the general case and $O(p^2)$ for "Low-rank marginalisation of $1 + T_5$ dimensions; Competitive cell exit; rank 2 at the default $T_5 = 1$" (`tab:gaussian:operation-costs`).

The record is the half that is wrong, and it is falsifiable from the specification rather than from preference. Its premise that a marginalisation is rare is contradicted by the chapter that calls cell exit the case that occurs often, and its prohibition would erase a row the cost table states as a distinct cost class. Under the standing ruling the specification is the measure (`conv:assayer:audit-measure`), so the code's general path — a fresh Cholesky at `src/model/bayesian.rs:352` — is conformant with both halves, and the code's single-index path — an extraction at `src/model/bayesian.rs:450` — is conformant with the specification and divergent only from a record sentence that overreaches it. This is the third time the corpus's two halves have stated incompatible arrangements and the third time one half has been wrong; it is the first time the record rather than the chapter is that half, so the ninth tranche's division of authority should be read as "find which half the specification's own tables support", not as "the chapter is the unreliable half".

What survives as a code finding is narrower than either half and is registered at (`entry:assayer:wl-low-rank-exit-unbuilt`). The extraction path exists only for $r = 1$; the competitive cell exit the specification prices removes $1 + T_5$ dimensions, which is two at the tabulated default, and two takes the general path. So the code has neither the arrangement the record demands nor the one the specification prices: the frequent operation runs the rare operation's algorithm. The code says so itself in two notices, (`todo:code:add-a-small-r`) and (`todo:code:generalise-this`), and both name the $1 + T_5$ block explicitly.

**Entry (The competitive cell exit takes the general marginalisation path)** · `entry:assayer:wl-low-rank-exit-unbuilt`

**Locus** `src/model/bayesian.rs:335`, where the extraction path is entered only on a single removed index, and `src/model/bayesian.rs:450`, the extraction it guards. The notices are (`todo:code:add-a-small-r`) and (`todo:code:generalise-this`). **Disposition** divergent-code-wrong against (`tab:gaussian:operation-costs`), which states low-rank marginalisation of $1 + T_5$ dimensions as its own cost class at $O(p^2)$ and names competitive cell exit as what occupies it at rank two, and against (`rem:gaussian:dual-tracking`), which prescribes the extraction for that class. At the tabulated $T_5 = 1$ every competitive cell exit removes two dimensions and falls through to the general path, which performs a full Cholesky inverse of $B'$. **Severity** medium, and argued on frequency rather than on correctness. Nothing computed is wrong — the general path is the more accurate of the two, which is why no test and no health field can see this. What is wrong is the cost, on the one marginalising operation the corpus says happens often: against the chapter's own figures the general path's Cholesky recomputation is about fifty-six milliseconds at the reference dimension where the tabulated class is quadratic, and the corpus is explicit that registration and deregistration are deployment events "and these figures are to be read against that" while cell exit is not. The severity would be high if cell exit were on the assessment path; it is on the label path, so it costs latency and not an answer. **What conformance would mean** the extraction guard admits a removal block of $1 + T_5$ indices rather than one, taking the covariance submatrix for the whole block, with the general path retained for larger removals — which is what both notices already ask for. **Coverage** none of the divergence, and the suite's shape is why. The marginalise suite pins the rank-one extraction — `rank1_sigma_prime_is_covariance_submatrix` and `rank1_preserves_recompute_counter` — and pins the general path's fresh factorisation at `sigma_prime_from_fresh_cholesky`, so both implemented paths are covered and the unimplemented middle is the gap. A test at $r = 2$ would pass today and would pass after the repair, which is the shape that makes this invisible.

**Entry (The marginal precision is verified on its diagonal, not its spectrum)** · `entry:assayer:wl-schur-diagonal-verification`

**Locus** `src/model/marginalise.rs:260`, on the general path, and `src/model/bayesian.rs:445`, on the single-index path. Both read the minimum diagonal entry and accept $B'$ when it is positive. **Disposition** divergent-code-wrong against (`req:gaussian:positive-definiteness`), and this is the item in the two clusters where the corpus argues the audit's case most completely. The requirement fixes the test as one of the spectrum — "the minimum eigenvalue of $B'$ must be strictly positive" — and then states, in its own prose and unprompted, why the shipped test is the wrong one: a positive minimum diagonal is necessary and not sufficient, a matrix with strictly positive diagonal can carry a negative eigenvalue through its off-diagonal structure, and "that is exactly the regime the regularisation exists to protect — the correction term is large relative to $B_{kk}$ precisely where the off-diagonal blocks matter most". It closes: "A diagonal test in this position passes the cases it was meant to catch." The code's module docstring states the diagonal test as stage four of its own algorithm, so the divergence is disclosed rather than accidental. **Severity** high. This is the last check before a marginalised precision matrix becomes the posterior. A $B'$ with positive diagonal and a negative eigenvalue is adopted at `src/model/marginalise.rs:257` and installed at `src/model/bayesian.rs:356`, and a precision matrix with a negative eigenvalue is a posterior with negative variance along that direction — the quantity the guarantee on precision is stated about (`inv:guarantee:precision`). The failure is partly netted downstream, because the subsequent inverse of $B'$ must factor it and a non-definite $B'$ will drive the cascade into its regularised phase; but the diagnostics report `bprime_verification_passed` as true on exactly that path, so the one field that would distinguish a verified marginal from an unverified one records the weaker test's verdict, and the regularised inverse is accepted without a flag at `src/model/bayesian.rs:94`. The corpus's own severity argument is sharper than any that could be constructed here, and it is that the cheap test fails precisely in the regime the expensive machinery around it exists to serve. **What conformance would mean** the verification becomes a spectral test — the factorisation the path has already performed makes a Cholesky attempt on $B'$ the cheapest available witness of definiteness, so the requirement can be met without an eigendecomposition — and the single-index path's guard, which is defensible on its own algebra but not on the corpus's, either adopts the same test or records the assumption it rests on. **Coverage** worse than none, and this is the third instance of the campaign's pattern. The general path's failure is pinned by `bprime_verification_failure` in `src/tests/model_marginalise.rs`, whose authored claim is (`claim:bayes:a-correction-that-would-drive-a-precision-diagonal-negative-is-rejected-in-favour-of-the-bare-submatrix`) — the diagonal criterion, named in the claim, so the divergent test stands as this corpus's stated expectation in the one place a reader would look to learn what the verification does. The fixture drives a diagonal entry to about minus a hundred, so the test exercises the easy case and never the case the requirement exists for. The suite's standing definiteness oracle is a helper named for the same weaker property. Repairing the verification fails this test and requires its claim to be rewritten with it.

The single-index guard deserves its own sentence, because its comment makes an argument that is mathematically sound and still does not clear the bar. It reasons that for a positive-definite parent and a positive scalar denominator the regularised scalar Schur complement is definite, and that is true: adding $\delta > 0$ to the pivot shrinks the subtracted rank-one term, so $B' = B_{kk} - c\,uu^\top \succeq B_{kk} - b_{11}^{-1}uu^\top$, which is the exact Schur complement and is definite whenever the parent is. The premise is that the parent is definite, and the corpus declines to make it — the posterior record records in terms that nothing claims the posterior stays well-conditioned (`cav:posterior:conditioning`) — which is the whole reason the requirement puts a verification at this point rather than a precondition. An argument in a comment is not corpus, and this is the strongest such argument the campaign has met.

**Entry (The synchronisation error is a diagonal maximum under the specified name)** · `entry:assayer:wl-sync-error-diagonal`

**Locus** `src/model/recompute.rs:353`, the function, and `src/model/recompute.rs:407`, its one call site inside the recomputation cascade. **Disposition** divergent-code-wrong against (`def:monitoring:synchronisation-error`), which fixes the quantity as $\lVert B\tilde{\Sigma} - I\rVert_F$, a Frobenius norm over the whole matrix, and which anticipates this exact substitution: it states that the definition has one site deliberately, "because a norm over a matrix and a maximum over that matrix's diagonal are different quantities that a restatement can silently interchange", and then reports that the shipped system computes the diagonal maximum. The shipped function computes $\max_j |(B\Sigma)_{jj} - 1|$, which is bounded above by the norm — $\lVert M\rVert_F \geq \sqrt{\sum_j M_{jj}^2} \geq \max_j |M_{jj}|$ — and can therefore stay small while off-diagonal error grows without limit, as the definition says. **Severity** was medium at the audit cut and high on the deferral's own completion. Nothing acted on the value at that cut: it was stored on the model and, per (`entry:posterior:sync-visibility`), reached no report, and the interval-shortening rule that would consume it was unwritten and stood as a notice at ``todo:code:accept-sync-error-threshold-and-halve``. What makes it more than a dormant defect is the ordering. That deferral's completion trigger is that the computed error be surfaced per model and in aggregate and that clean high-error recomputations shorten the interval; discharged against the shipped function it would export a different quantity under the specified name and shorten the interval on it. The computation must be repaired before the export is built, or the deferral closes onto the wrong number, and the two are therefore sequenced rather than independent. **What conformance would mean** the function returns the Frobenius norm of $B\Sigma - I$ — and the conformance cost here is close to nothing, because that function is already written in this repository. `frobenius_deviation_from_identity` at `src/tests/model_marginalise.rs:65` computes exactly the specified quantity, as a test helper, over the same two matrices. The specified norm and the shipped substitute stand about nine hundred lines apart in one package. **Coverage** the substitute is covered and the specified quantity is not. The recompute suite exercises the diagonal function directly; nothing compares it against a norm, which is what would have made the interchange visible.

**Repaired** in wave one at `ce98eb39`, and the function renamed with it: the definition's whole argument is that these two quantities must not stand under one name. The comparison the coverage line found missing is now a test — a pair with an exact unit diagonal and a half off it reads √1.5 under the norm and exactly zero under the diagonal maximum. The two existing tests pass under both measures, which is why they never saw the interchange, and their glosses say so. The three test-helper copies of the specified norm stay where they are: they are independent oracles for the code they check, and pointing them at the function they exist to verify would cost more than the duplication.

The later visibility completion consumes this repaired quantity, exposes it for every live model and in aggregate, and applies the specified fixed-threshold shortening rule. The sequencing risk this entry recorded is therefore resolved.

**Sequencing** this entry comes first among the synchronisation-error repairs and the order is not a preference. The quantity stored here is not the quantity the corpus names, so exporting it — which is what (`entry:assayer:wl-health-sync-error-constant`) asks for — would publish the wrong number under the specified name, more visibly than not publishing it at all. The same ordering reached outside the worklist: the synchronisation-error visibility deferral (`entry:posterior:sync-visibility`) could not then be closed as its completion trigger was written without exporting that same wrong quantity. Its prerequisite in this entry (`rem:assayer:model-linalg-pins`) is now satisfied and the deferral has completed.

**Entry (The debug oracle is reachable, and fatal, in release builds)** · `entry:assayer:wl-debug-oracle-release-reachable`

**Locus** `src/linalg/bridge.rs:326`, the activation predicate, and `src/linalg/bridge.rs:341`, the assertion it guards. **Disposition** divergent-code-wrong against (`dec:posterior:debug-oracle`), whose whole second half is a cost claim: the oracle "is compiled out of release builds and costs nothing there". Neither half holds. The predicate is `cfg!(debug_assertions) || tracing::enabled!(tracing::Level::DEBUG)`, and `cfg!` is an expression rather than an attribute, so the oracle's body, the full solve it calls and the assertion it ends in are all compiled into the release binary; and the disjunction means that a release process with a `DEBUG`-level tracing subscriber installed runs the oracle on every marginalisation, paying a full solve and a dense product. The code's own documentation carries the qualifier the record drops, saying "in debug builds (or with a `DEBUG`-level tracing subscriber)" and "zero cost in release builds without a DEBUG subscriber", so this is a record that has lost a condition its subject states. **Severity** high, on the failure mode rather than on the cost. The oracle ends in an `assert!`, which is a panic in every build profile. Raising the log level is a supported operational action, and taking it converts a numerical disagreement between two arithmetically equivalent routes into a process abort on a deregistration — in a package whose governing posture is that numerical pathologies are retained and flagged rather than failed (`dec:degradation:retain-and-flag`) and that the core does not fail at all (`dec:degradation:infallible-core`). A panic is further from that posture than the error channel the posture already rejects. **What conformance would mean** the oracle is placed behind `#[cfg(debug_assertions)]` so that the record's sentence becomes true of the code, or the tracing disjunction is kept and the assertion is demoted to a logged divergence so that the release path cannot abort — and the record's cost sentence is corrected to whichever is chosen. The first is what (`conv:assayer:audit-measure`) presumes. **Coverage** none of this property. `half_solve_equiv_full_solve` exercises the two routes' agreement, which is the oracle's subject; nothing exercises the oracle's activation, and no test runs in release with a subscriber installed, which is the one configuration that would show it.

**Repaired** in wave one at `ee8125c3`, by the first of the two offered conformances — the attribute — which is the one (`conv:assayer:audit-measure`) presumes. The tracing disjunction went with the expression rather than being kept beside a demoted assertion: the two halves are one mechanism, and a release binary that cannot reach the oracle cannot be made to abort on it by an operational action. The record's cost sentence is now true of the code and needs no correction.

The one configuration the coverage line names is now a test, and it exists only in that configuration: a release-profile test that installs a `DEBUG` subscriber, checks the subscriber took, and passes the oracle a correction unlike the full solve by eight orders of magnitude. Before the repair it aborted with the oracle's own message. The absolute tolerance recorded in the paragraph below stands unrepaired, being a second defect the conformance sentence does not reach.

The oracle carries a second defect that is independent of its reachability and is recorded here rather than as its own entry. Its tolerance is absolute at $10^{-12}$, applied per element to a correction whose entries scale with the precision matrix. The conditioning the guard above it admits is up to $10^8$ (`alg:gaussian:regularised-schur`), and a triangular solve at that conditioning carries a relative forward error of order $\kappa\varepsilon_\text{mach}$, about $2\times10^{-8}$ — so on a correction entry of order one the two routes may legitimately disagree some four orders of magnitude beyond the tolerance. The oracle is therefore tightest exactly where the regularisation it checks is doing the most work, and in a debug build it aborts there. A relative tolerance would be the instrument; the absolute one makes the oracle's own admitted operating range unreachable.

**Entry (Marginalisation hands a factorisation failure upstream as an error)** · `entry:assayer:wl-marginalise-error-channel`

**Locus** `src/model/bayesian.rs:95`, where the cascade's `Failed` verdict is converted into a `MarginaliseError`, and `src/model/bayesian.rs:353`, the call that propagates it out of `marginalise`. **Disposition** divergent-code-wrong against (`dec:posterior:cascade-never-fails`), which says that nothing upstream is handed an error from a factorisation, and against (`dec:posterior:infallible-correction`), which says that marginalisation does not fail and that "there is no error path out of a dimension leaving the model". Both are false of the shipped entry point: `marginalise` returns a `Result` whose error arises from a factorisation and from nothing else. The correction itself is infallible as the record says — `compute_schur_complement` has no failing exit and the record's citation to `src/model/marginalise.rs` is accurate — so the divergence is not where the record looks. It is one level up, in the covariance recomputation the correction is followed by. **Severity** medium. The failure is unreachable at the shipped regularisation configuration and is reachable at a configuration the package permits, which the suite demonstrates: `cholesky_inverse_failed` reaches the cascade's `Failed` verdict by setting both regularisation parameters to zero. The state consequence is benign — the propagation happens before any field is assigned, so a failed marginalisation leaves the model exactly as it was rather than half-transformed — and the cost is that a caller must handle a case the corpus promises does not exist, and that a deregistration silently does not happen. **What conformance would mean** the terminus retains and flags at this level as it does one level down: the uncorrected block is adopted, the covariance is left as the maintained one, and the outcome travels in the diagnostics rather than in a `Result` — which is the arrangement (`dec:degradation:retain-and-flag`) describes and which the diagnostics type is already shaped to carry. **Coverage** none. Nothing constructs the double failure through `marginalise`.

**Repaired** in wave eight at `c61dd6ba`, by the arrangement the conformance line names. The error channel is gone: `marginalise` returns its diagnostics unconditionally, and a factorisation failure in the covariance recomputation retains and flags at this level as the cascade does one level down — the uncorrected kept block is adopted, the covariance is the maintained one's kept block, and the outcome travels in the diagnostics as a flagged factorisation failure. The error type is deleted with its only producer, and the four lifecycle callers lose their early-return arms, so a deregistration always completes flagged rather than silently not happening — enforced by the compiler, since no error arm remains to return through. The double failure the coverage line said nothing constructs is now constructed: a negative-definite precision under zero regularisation, driven through `marginalise`, completes the removal with the maintained covariance retained value for value and the terminus flagged (`test:crate:marginalise-terminus-retains-and-flags`).

**Remark (The census, asked in four forms as a set)** · `rem:assayer:model-linalg-census`

The four forms were asked together, as the public-surface tranche's advice requires, and the two clusters split between them almost exactly as the fan-in prediction says they should: `linalg/` is reached by seven modules and holds most of the uncalled machinery, `model/` is reached by three and holds the reads that go nowhere.

*Uncalled.* Four functions in `src/linalg/serde_support.rs` — the serialise and deserialise pair for the backend's column type and the pair for its matrix type — are public, complete, and called from no production site. They exist to be named by field attributes, and no field names them: the only reachable implementation in the module is the one on the wrapper, which the checkpoint uses. The module's own docstring advertises all three. `cholesky_with_context` at `src/linalg/bridge.rs:139` is public, wraps the plain factorisation in model context, and has no caller either; the context-carrying error type it exists to produce is constructed directly at the two sites that need one.

*Corpus-wants-called.* `leverage_bound_fired` at `src/model/update.rs:56` is the predicate that answers whether the leverage bound was the binding constraint, and nothing calls it. The corpus asks for exactly that measurement in two places: the specification tabulates measured firing rates as data (`data:gaussian:binding-frequency`) and reasons at length about how the binding constraint shifts over a deployment's life (`disc:weighting:leverage-interaction`). The quantity those passages describe as measured is computable at every update site — the weight and the bound are both in hand at `src/owner/label_path.rs:536` — and is measured nowhere. This is the second-form finding the first form would have reported as a clean uncalled function to delete.

*Complete-looking but empty.* `ModelPrecisionHealth` at `src/model/recompute.rs:171` is a public type with a default, a constructor, an outcome recorder and an interval updater, and its own docstring calls it a unit-test helper. No production path constructs it. Production carries a different type of the same name at `src/health/published.rs:137`, re-exported from `src/health/mod.rs:68` and built at `src/owner/label_path.rs:1166`. Two types share a name across two clusters, one of them a fixture; the model type's interval updater is documented as sharing a single source of truth with the real update path, which it does, so the fixture and the production path agree today by construction and the corpus has no way to say which of the two a later reader means. A third reference at `src/health/summary.rs:244` names it in a comment about state that does not yet exist. `SchurConfig` at `src/model/marginalise.rs:52` is the second of this shape: a public, serialisable configuration struct with public fields, whose two consumers both construct it as `SchurConfig::default()` and whose values therefore cannot be configured.

*Written, never read.* `SchurDiagnostics` carries four fields, written at all seven construction sites across the two marginalisation paths. Production reads exactly one of them: `fell_back_to_bkk`, at `src/snapshot/working.rs:608`. The other three — the conditioning estimate, the correction outcome, and the verification verdict — are read nowhere. That is the sharpest result the census returned in these clusters, because of what it says about a deferral, and it is taken up at (`rem:assayer:model-linalg-cross-cites`).

**Remark (What the tests pin, and what the suite has no test for)** · `rem:assayer:model-linalg-pins`

Three findings, and the first two follow the campaign's established shapes while the third is new.

A test pins a divergence as its expected value for the third time in the campaign, and it is `bprime_verification_failure`, recorded in full at (`entry:assayer:wl-schur-diagonal-verification`). Its authored claim names the diagonal criterion, so the weaker test is the corpus's stated expectation and the repair carries a claim rewrite with it. The suite's shared definiteness helper asserts the same weaker property across the marginalise cluster, so the pinning is not confined to one test body.

A duplicate pair joins the byte-identical class, and it is the first pair within a single file. `from_storage_rejects_asymmetric` at `src/tests/linalg_symmetric.rs:222` and `debug_audit_from_storage_rejects_corrupt` at `src/tests/linalg_symmetric.rs:951` build the same five-by-five fixture, perturb the same element, call the same constructor at the same tolerance and assert the same error. The perturbations differ — one part in ten billion against one in a hundred million — and both are so far above the tolerance that they exercise one branch. The second cites the claim the first mints, so the register's arithmetic counts one claim as established by two tests and it is established by one, which is the defect the health tranche found in its three pairs and which this pair reproduces without the inline-and-external excuse. The class in (`entry:assayer:wl-weak-tests`) stands at five pairs.

The new shape is a test whose oracle disappears in release. `debug_audit_passes_valid_mutations` at `src/tests/linalg_symmetric.rs:920` has no assertion in its body at all; its oracle is the debug-only audit inside each operation it calls, and that audit is a `debug_assert!` stripped under `--release`. Run in a release test profile the test executes ten operations and establishes nothing, while its authored claim continues to assert that no legitimate operation trips the audit. Its enumeration was checked and is complete and correct — all four mutating methods, all four derived constructors and both absorbing constructors are exercised, and the two constructors that do not audit are correctly absent — so this is not a hollow test but a conditional one, and the condition is invisible from the claim, from the name and from the body. A test whose oracle is a debug assertion should say so, and none of the profile's three carriers has a place to.

Against those, the coverage the suite does hold is worth naming, because two of this tranche's conformant verdicts rest on it: `half_solve_equiv_full_solve` pins the two solve routes' agreement across block sizes, removal positions and coupling strengths, and `symmetric_operations_at_p638` pins the symmetry invariant at the reference dimension rather than at a toy size, which is the one shape that could hold at five and fail at scale.

The gap the tranche found is a decision with no test rather than a test with no oracle. `cholesky_inverse_two_phase_cascade` at `src/tests/linalg_bridge.rs:208` accepts a `Regularised` verdict or a `Clean` one and rejects only a failure, and its authored claim — that a barely indefinite matrix is rescued rather than failing — is exactly what the body establishes, so the test is not weak against itself. It was handed to this tranche as a weak cascade test and it is not one; what is missing is a test for a different decision. The cascade record's load-bearing choice is the ordering — the regularised phase runs only on the plain phase's failure — and its stated reason is that a factorisation which always regularised "would make the distinction between a healthy matrix and a repaired one unavailable to everything downstream, including the recomputation policy, which reads exactly that distinction" (`dec:posterior:repair-cascade`). The recomputation policy does read it: `compute_new_interval` halves the interval on `Regularised` and leaves it on `Clean`. So the distinction the record calls load-bearing drives the adaptive cadence, and the one test named for the cascade is indifferent to it by design, its own table prose saying so. No test in either cluster pins that the second phase runs only on the first's failure, and none pins that the verdict is the quantity the interval policy consumes. The finding is a coverage gap in the authoring wave's territory, not a repair, and it is minted here rather than written.

**Remark (Two conformant verdicts that a shallower reading would have inverted)** · `rem:assayer:model-linalg-cleared`

Both are recorded because the audit reports its clean checks explicitly, and both were within one line of becoming findings.

The combined decay factor conforms, and the mechanism that makes it conform is a guard rather than the arithmetic. The model type offers two decay entry points — `apply_time_decay`, which scales the precision and inverse-scales the covariance, and the update, which takes a combined factor — and the record forbids applying elapsed-time and label decay "in sequence as two separate multiplications" (`dec:posterior:combined-factor`). Both entry points are called on the label path, at `src/owner/label_path.rs:696` and inside the update at `src/owner/label_path.rs:541`, which read alone is the forbidden arrangement. It is not: the combined factor is formed once at `src/owner/label_path.rs:530` as $\gamma_\text{opr}\cdot\gamma_t^{\Delta t}$, and each standalone decay call is guarded on the model *not* having received an update, so the two are mutually exclusive per model per label and neither model is decayed twice. Conformant. The falsifier that would overturn this is a model reaching both branches in one label, and the flags that partition them are set at the update sites themselves.

The anchor's invariance conforms, and it was checked at the site that would show a breach rather than by reading the anchor. The substrate record fixes that no lifecycle event extends or marginalises the anchor (`dec:substrate:anchor-invariant`). The working copy's marginalisation at `src/snapshot/working.rs:607` removes dimensions from the operational model, the sister model and every outcome-axis model, and the anchor is absent from the list. An absence is weak evidence read alone, which is why it is recorded with what makes it strong: the three members named are named individually rather than iterated, so the anchor's omission is a written choice at the one site where including it would be a single line.

**Remark (What the two clusters return to the deferral register)** · `rem:assayer:model-linalg-cross-cites`

Two of the three surfaced-constant pins the deferral pass recorded belonged to the posterior record and terminated in these clusters. The synchronisation pin remains open on its narrower current trigger; the Schur pin completed and is retired. Their shared audit history is worth recording as a pattern rather than twice as an incident.

The Schur skip counter (`entry:posterior:skip-counter`) was pinned still-deferred at this audit cut: the marginalisation path returned diagnostics without touching a counter, and the health field was a literal. What the entry did not say, and what the census established, was that the path already computed and returned the distinction the counter would count. Every skip set `correction_outcome` to a variant naming its cause and handed it to the caller, which read a different field and dropped the rest. The completion trigger therefore needed a read and an accumulator, not an instrumentation of the marginalisation path — the same shrinking that the ledger sweep and the label-time reconstruction both underwent, and the third instance of a registered repair being smaller once the cluster that owns the mechanism was read. The following repair note is the current disposition and supersedes that historical pin.

**Repaired** on the marginalisation quality lane at `78d1f3b5`, and the tranche's reading of the shape was right: what was needed was a read and an accumulator, not an instrumentation. The working copy carries a ledger, the marginalisation helper folds every model's diagnostics into it before taking the fallback flag it used to take alone, and the health report's skipped-correction field is fed from that ledger instead of from a literal. The same fold carries more than the count the entry asked for, because the same lane gave every event a computed error to report (`req:gaussian:marginalisation-error-reported`), so what a host distinguishes now is not merely a run with skips from a run without but a run losing a hundredth of its corrections from one losing a millionth. The entry's half-right citation is corrected where it is owned rather than here.

The skip counter carries one hazard the entry cannot see and this tranche can. On the single-index path the conditioning estimate is unity for any healthy pivot, so a conditioning skip there is never a conditioning skip; it is a degenerate or non-finite pivot reported under the conditioning variant's name. A counter built against the completion trigger as written would aggregate two different events, and the aggregate would report conditioning skips a deployment has not had. **Repaired** on the marginalisation quality lane at `78d1f3b5`, and the tranche's warning is why it was looked for: the single-index path now reports a degenerate pivot as the factorisation failure it is, and the guard variants — which the same lane split into the host's and the package's — are reached there only by a ceiling a deployment has declared below one. The variant is also misreported in the other direction on the general path, where a failed verification returns `correction_outcome` as `Applied` — the variant whose own documentation reads "Full correction applied. Information transferred." — while `fell_back_to_bkk` says the opposite. The one field production reads is the correct one, and the two the deferral would come to read are not.

At the audit cut, synchronisation-error visibility (`entry:posterior:sync-visibility`) was pinned still-deferred and "further along than its original text suggested", with the per-model error computed and stored and everything downstream missing. The pin could not say, because the pass measured records against code and not the computed quantity against its definition, that the stored value was not the value the completion trigger named. That defect was registered and repaired at (`entry:assayer:wl-sync-error-diagonal`). With that prerequisite satisfied, the specified quantity now reaches every live model and the aggregate maximum, and clean high-error recomputations shorten the interval; the deferral has completed and is retired.

**Remark (What the corpus's own prose got right, and the four numbers it made checkable)** · `rem:assayer:model-linalg-prose-defects`

The configuration reference's parameter table for the core risk model closes with the sentence "All eleven ship as tabulated", and eight of the eleven have their realisation in these clusters. All eight were measured rather than transcribed and all eight hold: the two label-indexed forgetting rates, the replenishment floor at a thousandth, the leverage safety factor at five, the recomputation interval at a thousand with its floor at a hundred, the condition threshold at ten to the fifth, the importance ceiling at a hundred, and the Schur regularisation. The tranche began by registering the last of those as a divergence and withdrew it, and the withdrawal is the entry that matters here.

The specification fixes the Schur regularisation as a relative epsilon, $\delta = \lambda_\text{prior}\cdot\varepsilon_\text{Schur}$ at $\varepsilon_\text{Schur} = 10^{-4}$ (`alg:gaussian:regularised-schur`), and the package holds an absolute constant of $10^{-5}$. Read against the algorithm alone that is a structural divergence — a value specified as a product shipping as a scalar. The configuration reference adjudicates it in its own prose, in a note written for exactly this reader: "the package holds the absolute regularisation term rather than the relative epsilon, and the term it holds is the product of the prior precision and the epsilon above, so the two agree". At the tabulated prior precision of a tenth the product is $10^{-5}$ and the two do agree, so the finding is refuted and the disposition is conformant on the number.

What survives is narrower and is recorded rather than registered, because it is exactly the recent-window shape of (`entry:assayer:wl-shipped-defaults`). The prior precision is a configured parameter, threaded into the model at construction and at every extension; the Schur configuration is not threaded anywhere, both call sites taking `SchurConfig::default()`. So the agreement the note asserts is an agreement at one value of a tunable, and a deployment that raises the prior precision moves $\lambda_\text{prior}$ while $\delta$ stays put, at which point the corpus's sentence becomes false of the code without anything in the code changing. The corpus's note is right about today and states its agreement without the qualifier that makes it true, which is the third time in the campaign that leaning on a sentence rather than on a table has nearly produced a false finding and the first time the sentence was right and incomplete rather than wrong.

**Repaired** on the marginalisation quality lane at `78d1f3b5`. The Schur sub-configuration is threaded from the top-level configuration through the four lifecycle deregistration handlers and the working copy's marginalisation helper to both call sites, general and rank-one alike, so neither builds a default any longer. The configuration reference's qualification retires with the defect: its closing sentence states the threading rather than the departure, and the same table gains the host's posture ceiling as a row, that being the second Schur parameter a deployment now sets. The falsifier marginalises one trained model twice from identical copies at two factors and requires the surviving precisions to differ, which they cannot do unless the configured value made the journey (`test:crate:host-schur-configuration-reaches-the-marginalisation`). What this record did not anticipate is that the threading turned out to be the smaller half of its own repair: the same lane split the condition guard in two and gave the host the upper one, and a host-declared guard that never reached the computation would have shipped dead on arrival, so the debt this paragraph recorded as narrow was load-bearing for a ruling that had not yet been made.

Three smaller prose defects sit outside the disposition grammar and are recorded for the record pipeline. The posterior record's guard table names four guards by site and all four resolve at the sites named — the leverage floor in `src/model/update.rs`, the pivot threshold in `src/linalg/bridge.rs`, and the Schur offset and conditioning threshold both in `src/model/marginalise.rs` — which is the first table in the campaign to verify at every row, and it verifies because it declined to transcribe the values. Against that, two of the same record's decisions cite the wrong file: the identity substitution and the leverage bound both cite `src/model/update.rs`, which holds the weight helpers and the constants and no part of the update, the seven steps standing in `src/model/bayesian.rs:738` under a note saying so. And the two Gaussian chapters disagree about the recomputation trigger's shape — the foundations chapter opens its adaptive procedure with "rather than recomputing on a fixed interval", the risk chapter's update algorithm says "every thousand labels, or sooner when the condition number crosses its threshold" — and the code and the posterior record both implement the second. Two corpus statements to one is not a divergence, and the first chapter's phrasing would read as one to a tranche that met it alone.

**Remark (The backend isolation, and the two dead-code suppressions that hide its cost)** · `rem:assayer:model-linalg-isolation`
