The substrate record decides that no caller names the backend's types, that the wrapper is the only place they appear, and draws from that the consequence that "replacing the dependency is a change to one module rather than to every site that touches a matrix" (`dec:substrate:backend-isolation`). The linear-algebra module's own docstring states the same rule twice, once as "no direct imports are permitted outside this module (enforced by convention; CI lint planned)" and once, in the bridge, as the flat assertion that no other module in the crate imports the backend's types directly.

Measured across the package, one production module outside the isolation layer names them: `src/model/bayesian.rs`, which imports the backend's column and matrix types and carries the column type in the model's mean field, in the public accessor for it, in the public checkpoint constructor's parameter list and in the public update's argument list. Six test files name them too, which the convention as stated also forbids. So the rule holds at eleven of twelve production modules and fails in the one cluster the record's neighbouring decisions govern.

The larger point is that the consequence would not follow even if the rule held. The wrapper's own public surface is stated in the backend's types — it takes and returns the column type on the matrix-vector product and the rank-one update, and returns a reference to the backend matrix from its inner accessor and a raw backend matrix from the cross-block extraction — so replacing the dependency changes the wrapper's signatures and therefore every caller, whether or not the caller writes the dependency's name. The isolation the record decides is an import isolation; the cost claim it draws is about a type isolation, and the wrapper does not provide one. This is not a defect to repair so much as a record sentence that promises more than the mechanism beneath it delivers, and it matters because the tabulated operation costs are said to be meaningful only on this condition (`rem:substrate:cost-ownership`).

Both cluster modules suppress dead-code analysis wholesale — every module declaration in `src/model/mod.rs` and in `src/linalg/mod.rs` carries the suppression, under a comment in each file explaining that the modules are crate-private and consumed only by internal callers and tests. The modules are declared public in both files, so the comment describes a visibility neither has. The consequence is the one this tranche's census had to work around: the compiler's own answer to the first census question is switched off across both clusters, which is why five public functions and two configuration types could stand uncalled without anything reporting it. The campaign's standing preference for a tool over a discipline (`goal:assayer:tools-over-discipline`) applies with unusual directness here, because the tool exists, is on by default, and was turned off.

**Remark (Three smaller findings, recorded without entries)** · `rem:assayer:model-linalg-minor`

The regularised phase's two parameters stand in a relation nothing fixes and nothing tests. The replacement value is $10^{-7}$ and the threshold below which a pivot is replaced is $10^{-3}$, so a pivot anywhere below the threshold is set four orders of magnitude beneath it. In the cascade's actual regime this is harmless and is arguably the point — the phase runs only after a non-positive pivot has failed the plain factorisation, and raising a non-positive pivot to a small positive value is what regularisation means — but the backend applies the replacement to every pivot below the threshold during that factorisation, not only to the one that failed, so a healthy pivot at a ten-thousandth elsewhere in the matrix is lowered by three orders of magnitude and the resulting inverse is inflated accordingly. The corpus fixes neither value and says so deliberately, so the disposition is unspecified; what is registered is that the ordering of the two is load-bearing, undocumented, and unpinned.

The lower-to-upper mirror is implemented twice. The wrapper's private mirror is the one the substrate record's convention describes, and a second copy stands in the bridge as a private helper over a raw backend matrix. Both mirror in the specified direction, so the convention (`conv:substrate:mirror-direction`) is conformant and the reverse direction appears nowhere in either cluster, which is the check the record says the corpus previously failed. The second copy is nonetheless redundant at its only call site: it is applied to a computed inverse which is then handed to the absorbing constructor, and that constructor mirrors again. The clean phase of the same function skips the helper entirely and reaches the same constructor, which is what shows the call has no effect. Two agreeing copies are not sharing (`rem:calibration:sharing-rationale`), and this is the shape that remark describes with the added wrinkle that one copy is already dead.

The wrapper's diagonal accessor is named for the ascending order and returns the descending one — `diagonal_min_max` returns the maximum first. All four call sites across the two clusters unpack it correctly, two of them by discarding the first element to reach the minimum, so nothing is wrong today; it is registered because the name is the documentation a fifth call site will read. Beside it, the matrix-vector product's comment bounds the reference dimension at six hundred and twenty-two where the specification's cost table and the campaign's own dimension arithmetic both state six hundred and thirty-eight, so a comment reasoning about cost at scale reasons from a dimension the corpus does not have.

## Twelfth tranche: the identity layer and host signals · `sec:assayer:audit-tranche-identity-signal`

The clusters are `src/identity/` — the dimension configuration, the competitive cell types and their index, the per-cell mutable state, the observation channel, the maintenance thread and the graph snapshot — and `src/signal/`, the schema index, the value shapes and their encoding, and the per-entity cache. They were taken together because the specification chapter that governs the first also holds the table that governs the second, which the scope map does not say and which is the correction below.

The tranche read the memory record for the identity cluster, the retention record for the signal cache, the key-space identity chapter and the host-signals chapter for both, the concurrency and degradation records for the publication, overflow and infallibility postures, the clock record for the two timestamp domains, the metrics record for the signal cache's deferral, and the upstream API record for the graph boundary. The substrate record was read and discarded: it governs neither cluster, which is the second half of the scope-map correction.

**Remark (Both of the scope map's columns fail again, and the specification column fails in a new way)** · `rem:assayer:identity-signal-scope-map`

The `identity/` row names memory and construction. Memory governs the cluster and governs it almost entirely — nine of its decisions and five of its deferrals are about this code. Construction does not: it is read for the registration surface and decides nothing the cluster realises. What the row omits is the degradation record, which the overflow posture defers to in terms (`dec:memory:overflow-degrades`), and the concurrency record, which owns the publication discipline the same record cites. The row's specification column says "the identity data-model chapters", plural, and there is one.

The `signal/` row is wrong in both columns and the record column is wrong in a way no previous row has been. It names the substrate record, which is about the matrix carrier of the posterior and mentions no signal anywhere in its body. This is the first row whose record column names a record that is neither corpus for the cluster nor legacy but simply unrelated — a worse failure than the guidance row's, because that row at least named a document about its subject.

What the row should name is the retention record, and this tranche had drafted a sharper claim than that which its own check refuted. The draft said the cluster has no governing record at all, the closest thing being a deferral in the metrics record naming the cache's then-absent gauge (`entry:metrics:signal-cache`). The falsifier is two decisions in the retention record that are about nothing else: the cache stores encoded rather than raw values (`dec:retention:cache-preencoded`) and it is not persisted and repopulates from traffic (`dec:retention:cache-losable`). Both were found by reading the record the map does *not* name for this cluster, which is the same move that produced the correction and is worth stating as such: a row can be wrong by naming the wrong record and wrong again by making the reader conclude there is none. Five rows are now known wrong on the specification side and five on the record side.

The specification column fails in a new way as well, and it is the direction the remaining tranches should watch. The row says "the signals data-model chapter", and that chapter is real, is about this cluster, and is not sufficient: the cache's capacity, entry size, eviction policy and memory budget are tabulated in the *identity* chapter (`tab:keyspace:signal-cache`), because the corpus places the table where it argues the cache's independence from the identity layer rather than where the cache is defined. A tranche reading only the named chapter would have found the persistence modes and no property of the cache to measure against, and would have reported the cluster as largely unspecified. Every previous specification-column failure was a chapter that said nothing; this one is a chapter that says a true half.

**Entry (The identity adverse rate averages the complement of the specified indicator)** · `entry:assayer:wl-identity-adverse-rate-inverted`

**Locus** `src/identity/cell_state.rs:192`, the single statement `let is_adverse = if update.is_positive { 0.0 } else { 1.0 };`. The value written is the field at `src/identity/cell_state.rs:123`, and the flag it reads is set at `src/owner/label_path.rs:1307` as `state.label.valence > 0.0` and handed over in the update constructed three lines below at `src/owner/label_path.rs:1321`. No labelled notice stands here and the item is new.

**Disposition** divergent-code-wrong against (`tab:keyspace:outcome-state`), whose first row makes the adverse rate average an exponential average over the positive-valence indicator, and against the sign convention that row cites (`conv:valence:sign`), which fixes positive valence as the adverse direction in the corpus's first statement of it. The indicator the corpus names is the indicator that the valence is positive; the code multiplies by one exactly when it is not.

The finding needs no argument this audit had to construct, because the sister store adjudicates it. Memory is one record for two stores because they raise the same questions (`dec:memory:key-types-distinct`), and the Ledger's twin of this function is line-for-line parallel to it: the same name, the same three steps, the same decay-then-average shape. At `src/ledger/entry.rs:179` that twin writes `let is_bad = if update.is_positive { 1.0 } else { 0.0 }`, under a comment on the line above stating the convention outright, and its doc comment renders the formula with the indicator unnegated (`src/ledger/entry.rs:155`). Both twins are fed from the same predicate on the same label. One of the two is the corpus's, and it is not this one.

**Severity** high, and the argument does not have to reach for the report-only posture because nothing here is a diagnostic. The value is read at `src/assessment.rs:1434` into the cross-dimension maximum, which the corpus places at the anchor model's eighth projection position (`tab:keyspace:cross-dimension-features`), so it is a coordinate of the feature vector the fixed-dimension model is defined over. What the models learn is therefore a weight on the rate at which a range's labelled requests came back *benign*, carried at the position the corpus reserves for the rate at which they came back adverse. The sign of the learned association is inverted wherever the feature carries signal at all, and the inversion is silent: the quantity stays in the unit interval, decays correctly, warms up correctly, and reads as a plausible rate at every point an operator could inspect it.

**What conformance would mean** the conditional's two arms exchange places, which makes the statement identical to its Ledger twin. Nothing else moves: the decay step above it, the smoothing below it and the field's type are all conformant.

**Coverage** worse than none, and pinned twice in two suites. The inline test `outcome_update_positive` (`src/identity/cell_state.rs:449`) constructs an update with the flag set, asserts the adverse rate is exactly zero, and carries an authored claim saying that a positive outcome contributes nothing to the adverse rate — which is true of the code and false of the corpus, since the corpus's positive outcome is the adverse one. The external suite pins the same inversion from the other side: `src/tests/identity.rs:159` sets the flag false under the comment "adverse outcome" and then asserts the rate moved. Repairing the code fails both tests, and both authored claims state the divergence as the expected behaviour rather than merely failing to catch it.

**Repaired** in wave two at `9464dccb`. The two arms exchanged places and nothing else moved, as the entry's conformance line predicted. Both pinning tests were restated with the change rather than after it: the inline pin now establishes the direction — a positive-valence outcome raises the rate, a benign one only decays it — under a claim arguing why the sign matters at the anchor position the feature feeds, and the external formula test presents the adverse case as the sign convention names it. The decay test was moved onto a benign outcome so its subject stays decay alone.

**Entry (Graph importance decays sixty times an hour at a rate specified per hour)** · `entry:assayer:wl-identity-decay-cadence`

**Locus** `src/identity/maintenance_loop.rs:58` and `src/identity/maintenance_loop.rs:61`, the two constants, applied together at `src/identity/maintenance_loop.rs:486` where the loop calls the graph's decay once per elapsed interval. No labelled notice stands here and the item is new.

**Disposition** divergent-code-wrong against (`tab:keyspace:decay-rates`), whose first row states the mechanism as spatial decay **per hour** at the dimension's declared spatial decay rate. Two independent departures stand in the two constants, and they compound.

The cadence is per minute. The decay interval ships at sixty seconds and the attenuation is applied once per interval, so the tabulated hourly factor is applied sixty times in an hour. The rate is also wrong as an hourly rate: the chapter's own prose fixes the intended timescale by saying that at the default rate an entity unseen for a fortnight has its importance halved, and halving over a fortnight of hours requires a factor near the 0.998 the same table states one row below for outcome decay, not the 0.999 that ships.

The consequence is arithmetic and is recomputed here rather than asserted. At 0.999 applied sixty times an hour the effective hourly factor is about 0.9417, and importance halves after roughly six hundred and ninety applications — some eleven and a half hours. The corpus's fortnight is three hundred and thirty-six hours. So a competitive cell loses standing about twenty-nine times faster than the chapter describes, and the constant would still be wrong by a factor of two in the other direction if the cadence were repaired alone, since 0.999 per hour halves in roughly twenty-nine days rather than fourteen.

**Severity** high. This is the parameter the chapter says governs how the contour evolves and who stays competitive, and every consequence it names follows from the timescale: an entity dropping past the cutoff exits the set, its features are marginalised out, and its cell is absorbed into its parent. The chapter budgets that cost against a set that "turns over a few times a day" (`alg:keyspace:cell-exit`), and prices one exit at roughly 1.2M operations at the reference dimension. At a half-life of half a day the turnover is not a few times a day, so the lifecycle cost the corpus calls negligible beside the label path is being paid at a multiple of the rate the estimate assumed, and every cell's learned weight is discarded and restarted from zero on that shortened cycle — which is the part that is not merely a cost, because a weight that never survives long enough to converge is a weight the models never learn.

**What conformance would mean** the attenuation is raised to the tabulated hourly rate and applied once per hour, or is converted to the per-interval root of the declared hourly rate so that the cadence becomes an implementation choice rather than a change of timescale. The second is the better repair and the corpus permits either, since the table states a rate and a period and not a schedule.

**Coverage** none. No test asserts a decay rate, a decay period, or an elapsed half-life; the only decay a test drives is the `#[cfg(test)]` forced path (`src/identity/maintenance_loop.rs:376`), which takes the attenuation as an argument and therefore cannot observe the shipped constant.

**Repaired** in wave two at `4177ae28`, by the second of the two repairs the entry offers and prefers: the default became the tabulated hourly rate and the per-minute attenuation is derived as its per-interval root, so the composition over an hour is the hourly rate exactly and the cadence is an implementation choice. The coverage gap closed with it — a test now pins that sixty per-minute applications compose to the tabulated rate and that the half-life sits inside the corpus's fortnight.

**Entry (Coverage depth is divided by a constant where the corpus names the dimension's own width)** · `entry:assayer:wl-identity-coverage-depth-divisor`

**Locus** `src/assessment.rs:911`, `f64::from(deepest_depth) / 128.0`. The divisor the corpus names is carried at `src/identity/dimension.rs:62`, written at registration (`src/identity/dimension.rs:95`) from a value the surface validates into the range one to a hundred and twenty-eight (`src/api/lifecycle.rs:276`). The locus is outside the identity directory and the field is inside it; the entry is registered here because the census question that found it is this cluster's.

**Disposition** divergent-code-wrong against (`tab:keyspace:dimension-features`), whose first row sources the coverage-depth feature from the receiving cell's depth over the domain width. The shipped expression is the receiving cell's depth over a literal.

**Severity** medium, and the argument turns on which consumer is reached rather than on the size of the error. Within one dimension the departure is a constant rescaling, which a linear model absorbs into the coefficient, so a deployment whose dimensions all declare the full width computes the specified number and a deployment with one narrower dimension merely learns a rescaled weight for it. The sharp consequence is the cross-dimension aggregate: the same value is reduced by maximum across dimensions at `src/assessment.rs:1429` into the feature the corpus names Maximum coverage depth (`tab:keyspace:cross-dimension-features`), and a maximum over quantities carried on different scales is not a rescaling of anything. A dimension declaring thirty-two bits — which the chapter names as a primary case in its own encoding discussion (`req:keyspace:encoding-contract`) — contributes a coverage depth a quarter of the specified one and can never win that maximum against a full-width dimension however deep its receiving cell sits.

**What conformance would mean** the divisor is the receiving dimension's `domain_bits`, which the assessment path already holds, since the same function reaches the dimension to encode the entity key (`src/lib.rs:891`).

**Coverage** none, and the corpus's own suites cannot see it: every registration in the test corpus declares a hundred and twenty-eight bits, at which the literal and the field agree, and the one test that declares otherwise declares zero to exercise the validation refusal (`src/tests/lifecycle_integration.rs:1115`).

**Re-verified at the crate glue** (`sec:assayer:audit-tranche-glue`). The finding stands and the line that must change is outside the cluster this entry is filed against: the divisor is at `src/assessment.rs:911`, inside the assessment pipeline's identity step, not in `src/identity/`. The adjudication is the identity tranche's and is unaffected; what the repair wave needs is the locus. **Falsifier** a second division by the key space's width elsewhere in `identity/`, of which the tranche found none.

**Repaired** in wave two at `c7e2fe6e`. The divisor is the dimension's declared `domain_bits`, read through a new shared-state accessor, and the division is a named function carrying a pin the coverage line said could not exist in the shipped suites: a narrow dimension's cell reads the same fraction of its own domain as a full-width dimension's cell reads of its, and at the full width the divisor coincides with the retired literal — the coincidence that had hidden the divergence from every full-width registration.

**Entry (A dropped lifecycle submission publishes a competitive set the models were never extended for)** · `entry:assayer:wl-identity-lifecycle-drop`

**Locus** `src/identity/maintenance_loop.rs:470`, where the submission is offered to the model owner without blocking and a failure is logged and discarded, and `src/identity/maintenance_loop.rs:478` and `src/identity/maintenance_loop.rs:481`, which publish the new index and advance the loop's own record of the set unconditionally afterwards. No labelled notice stands here and the item is new.

**Disposition** divergent-code-wrong against (`alg:keyspace:cell-entry`), which orders the extension of every model but the anchor first and the recording of the new membership last, and against (`alg:keyspace:cell-exit`), which orders the marginalisation first and the removal of the membership last. The shipped order is the reverse in the one respect that matters under failure: the membership is recorded — installed into the published index that assessment threads read — with no acknowledgement that the models were extended at all, because the submission is asynchronous by construction and its failure path returns to the same two lines.

The failure is not hypothetical and it is not transient. Advancing `dim.current_set` on the failing path is what makes it permanent: the next cycle compares the new set against itself, finds no difference, and never re-offers the lost events. So a single full moment on the model owner's queue leaves the published competitive set carrying cells no model has a coordinate for, and nothing in the package will ever notice, because the only mechanism that could notice is the comparison that has just been told the two agree.

**Severity** high. The corpus's whole reason for versioning the dimension map is that a consumer holding a stale layout must be a detected condition rather than a silently wrong one (`rem:vector:map-obligations`), and this path produces exactly the undetected version of that condition from the other end — not a stale map at a consumer, but a published membership the map was never rebuilt for. What follows downstream is an indicator whose feature index either does not exist or belongs to another feature, which is the failure mode the sole-resolver decision exists to make impossible (`dec:vector:sole-resolver`) arriving through the one door that decision does not cover.

**What conformance would mean** the two lines move behind the submission's success: the index is published and `current_set` advanced only where the submission was accepted, so that a refused send leaves the difference outstanding and the next cycle retries it. The corpus does not require the send to be infallible; it requires the membership to be recorded after the extension, and recording it only on success is the weakest change that obtains that.

**Coverage** none. Nothing in either suite fills the model owner's queue while a competitive set change is pending.

**Entry (Lifecycle events within one restructuring event are emitted in an order that varies per process)** · `entry:assayer:wl-identity-nondeterministic-event-order`

**Locus** `src/identity/maintenance_loop.rs:427` and `src/identity/maintenance_loop.rs:428`, where the exits and entries are collected from the difference of two `HashSet`s, and `src/identity/maintenance_loop.rs:456` and `src/identity/maintenance_loop.rs:460`, where they are pushed onto the event list in that iteration order. No labelled notice stands here and the item is new.

**Disposition** divergent-code-wrong against (`alg:keyspace:cell-entry`), whose batching preamble requires that where an event promotes or demotes several cells, every resulting extension and marginalisation is applied in deterministic event order. The shipped order is deterministic between the two groups — exits precede entries, which the code says it intends and which the corpus permits — and undetermined within each, because the standard hash set's iteration order depends on a per-process random seed.

**Severity** medium. Nothing is wrong within a single run: the same cells are extended and the same cells marginalised, and the models are correct at the end of the event whatever order the members arrived in. What the order fixes is which coordinate each new indicator receives, so two instances given identical traffic assign the same cells to different positions, and a parameter vector written by one does not mean the same thing to the other. That is the failure the canonical block order exists to prevent (`dec:vector:block-order`), reintroduced inside a block rather than between blocks, and it bites at exactly the moment the corpus cares about — a restore into an instance that is not the one that wrote the checkpoint.

**What conformance would mean** each group is sorted before the events are built. The cell identifier already carries the two fields a total order needs and already derives the comparison the index's own construction uses (`src/identity/competitive.rs:172`), so the repair is a sort at two lines and no new ordering to define.

**Coverage** none, and a test could not easily have found it: a single-process suite observes one seed per run, so the defect is invisible to any assertion that does not compare two processes.

**Entry (Three of the four measurement update rules depart from the table that defines them)** · `entry:assayer:wl-identity-measurement-rules`

**Locus** `src/lib.rs:960`, the one function that writes measurement state, whose four statements at `src/lib.rs:979`, `src/lib.rs:983`, `src/lib.rs:985` and `src/lib.rs:986` correspond to the table's four rows. The state written is this cluster's (`src/identity/cell_state.rs:85`). The locus is outside the identity directory and is registered here because the type and the governing table are inside it; the adjudication of `src/lib.rs` as a cluster belongs to the tranche that takes it.

**Disposition** divergent-code-wrong on three rows and conformant on the fourth, against (`tab:keyspace:measurement-state`).

| Row | Specified | Shipped |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Per-Sentinel alarm average | The request's composite alarm from that Sentinel | One composite scalar written into every reporting Sentinel's entry |
| Suspicion | Derived: the mean of the clipped normalised alarms | A stored exponential average of the same composite scalar |
| Volatility | Exponential average over the squared change in the alarm average | Exponential average over the squared change against the previous suspicion |
| Step count | Incremented once per assessment | Incremented once per assessment |

The first row is the one that empties the structure of its purpose. The corpus holds the alarms per Sentinel so that, in the words of the cluster's own authored claim, a cell can be loud on one Sentinel's axis and quiet on another's without the two blurring together; the shipped loop writes the identical value into every entry, so the map's entries differ only in which Sentinels have reported at all and the per-Sentinel resolution the map exists to carry is not present in it.

The second is a departure in kind rather than in value. The table marks suspicion **Derived**, and a derived quantity is computed from the alarms at the moment it is read; the shipped field is a fourth accumulator advanced beside them. The two disagree whenever the per-Sentinel set changes, and the shipped one carries its own independent warm-up, which the table does not provide for.

The third is a magnitude, and it is recomputed here. The change in an exponential average over one step is the smoothing complement times the innovation, so the squared change the table names is the square of that product; the shipped expression squares the innovation alone. At the smoothing complement the code ships — one twentieth, at `src/lib.rs:970` — the shipped volatility is larger than the specified one by the reciprocal of that complement squared, a factor of four hundred. Nothing clips it, so the feature the corpus normalises into a bounded indicator is fed a quantity two orders of magnitude outside the range the table implies.

**Severity** medium to high. All three feed features rather than diagnostics: suspicion and volatility reach the per-dimension block and then the cross-dimension maximum at the anchor's seventh and twelfth projection positions (`tab:keyspace:cross-dimension-features`), so a volatility inflated four hundredfold is a model input at a named anchor coordinate. It is argued below high only because the inflation is a fixed factor at a fixed smoothing constant, so the anchor's weight can absorb the scale in a way it cannot absorb the coverage depth's cross-dimension incomparability.

**What conformance would mean** the per-Sentinel loop takes each Sentinel's own alarm rather than the composite; suspicion is computed from the entries at read time and the field is removed; and the volatility step squares the change in the average rather than the innovation.

**Coverage** worse than none on the first two rows. The two inline tests named for them — `measurement_update_alarm` and `measurement_suspicion_tracks_aggregate` (`src/identity/cell_state.rs:393` and `src/identity/cell_state.rs:425`) — write the smoothing step out by hand in their own bodies and assert on their own arithmetic, so they are tautological in the sense the weak-test entry defines (`entry:assayer:wl-weak-tests`) and neither reaches the production function at `src/lib.rs:960` at all. The third row is uncovered outright.

**Re-verified at the crate glue** (`sec:assayer:audit-tranche-glue`), which recomputed the departure and confirms it. The three departing rules are written in that cluster rather than in `identity/`: the smoothing constant at `src/lib.rs:970`, the suspicion and volatility updates at `:983` and `:985`, and the step count at `:986`, all inside the shared-state implementation. **Falsifier** a second measurement update path in `identity/` that applies the tabulated rules, which the tranche looked for and did not find.

**Repaired** in wave two at `be23e338`, all three rows in the conformance line's terms: each entry now averages its own Sentinel's composite alarm — the assessment path hands the update per-Sentinel pairs rather than one folded maximum — suspicion is derived at read time from the entries and the stored field is removed, and volatility squares the change in the derived average rather than the innovation. The tautological suspicion test was restated into a pin of the derivation with an independent oracle; the alarm-row tautology still hand-rolls its smoothing and stays for the weak-test wave, and the production update remains reachable only end-to-end, which that wave should weigh.

**Entry (The observation channel type is complete, tested, exported and reached by no production path)** · `entry:assayer:wl-identity-observation-channel-unused`

**Locus** `src/identity/observation.rs:65`, a public type with ten methods, a drop counter, a default capacity constant at `src/identity/observation.rs:49`, its own `Default` and `Debug` implementations, and six inline tests carrying six authored claims. The production observation queue is constructed instead as a bare bounded channel at `src/api/lifecycle.rs:301` and handed to `src/identity/mod.rs:152`, which reimplements the enqueue, the refusal and the counted drop at `src/identity/mod.rs:182`.

**Disposition** unspecified, and the sorting is the point the disposition grammar was written for. The corpus requires a bounded per-dimension channel that drops and counts on overflow without blocking or erroring (`dec:memory:overflow-degrades`), and the shipped production path does exactly that. It does not say which type realises it, so a second complete implementation standing unreached is a gap in the corpus rather than a violation of it.

Three things follow that are worth registering under a disposition that closes nothing. The record's code evidence points at this module for both the observation surface and the overflow posture (`dec:memory:observation-surface`), and the behaviour those decisions describe is realised in the neighbouring file — the citation resolves to a module rather than a line, so it is not wrong, but a reader following it lands on the copy nothing runs. The default capacity constant here has no production reader: the shipped capacity comes from the budget's own field (`src/types.rs:241`), whose default is the same hundred thousand written a second time in a second file. And the two implementations do not agree. This one counts a drop only when the queue is full and returns silently on a disconnected channel (`src/identity/observation.rs:115`); the production one counts both as drops (`src/identity/mod.rs:185`). The corpus speaks to the full case and not to the disconnected one, so neither is divergent, but the divergence between two copies of one mechanism is what makes the duplication a defect rather than an untidiness.

**Severity** medium, as an amplifier and a coverage illusion rather than a defect of its own. Six tests and six authored claims stand against the copy nothing runs, including the two claims that most directly state the degradation posture; the production copy's own overflow behaviour is covered separately at `src/identity/mod.rs:303`, so the corpus is not uncovered here, but its test register counts twice what is established once and attributes half of it to unreachable code.

**What conformance would mean** one of the two is removed. If the type stays, the registration surface constructs it and hands out its sender rather than building a channel by hand; if it goes, its six claims move to the production copy and the duplicate constant goes with it.

**Coverage** not applicable in the usual direction; the finding is that the coverage exists and is aimed at the wrong copy.

**Remark (What the four census forms returned, and the two that found the flagship)** · `rem:assayer:identity-census`

Asked as a set rather than as a sequence, as the public surface tranche now requires (`rem:assayer:api-guidance-census`), the four forms split unevenly and the split is instructive because the cluster's fan-in does not predict it.

The first form returns one large result and several small ones. The large one is the observation channel above, and it is the biggest block of unreached machinery the campaign has met inside a single file. The small ones are the graph snapshot's five read-side accessors — `is_empty`, `total_energy`, `terminal_count`, `pewei` and `validate_total_sum` at `src/identity/snapshot.rs:82` through `src/identity/snapshot.rs:124` — of which only the construction half is production reachable, through `extract_snapshot` at `src/identity/maintenance_loop.rs:223`. That one is not a finding: the snapshot is written for a restore path to read, the restore path reads it through the persistence cluster, and a producer whose consumers are elsewhere is the ordinary shape.

The second form clears both of those and convicts neither. The corpus wants a bounded counted channel and has one; it wants a checkpointable graph extraction and has one. Asked in the first form alone this cluster would have reported two findings where it has one, which is the mistake the register warned against after the metrics wave.

The third form — does anything ever put anything into it — returns the measurement state's four fields, and the answer is yes but from outside the directory, which is how the entry above came to be written against a locus in `src/lib.rs`. A reader of `src/identity/` alone would have concluded that the type is complete and empty, because nothing in the directory writes it and the two tests that appear to exercise it write their own arithmetic instead. That is the form's first false negative in the campaign, and it was caught only by asking the form outward rather than inward.

The fourth form returned the tranche's two sharpest results and neither is a counter. `IdentityDimension.domain_bits` is written at registration, validated against a range, and read in production by nothing but the type's own `Debug` implementation — and it is the divisor the corpus names for the coverage-depth feature, which is the entry above. Beside it, `MeasurementState.step_count` (`src/identity/cell_state.rs:104`) is incremented on every assessment against every active cell and read in production nowhere: the corpus tabulates it as a component of measurement state and lists no feature it supplies (`tab:keyspace:dimension-features`), so it is an honest write with no reader rather than a defect, and it is recorded because the cluster's own authored claim says the count is what lets a reader tell calm from never-looked-at and no reader exists to be told.

The general lesson is the one the health tranche drew and this cluster sharpens: the yield came from the corpus, not the shape. Two fields written and unread sit side by side in the same directory, one is the campaign's cleanest small divergence and the other is nothing at all, and only the feature table separates them.

**Remark (Corrections to two prior tranches, and one that failed)** · `rem:assayer:identity-signal-cross-cites`

A prior tranche's cross-citation is a claim about code, checkable to the same standard (`conv:assayer:audit-evidence`). Three were checked here. Two hold and are sharpened; one correction this tranche was drafting did not survive its own falsifier and is recorded as the near miss it was.

**The health tranche's open question has an answer, and the repair shrinks to nothing in this cluster.** (`entry:assayer:wl-health-identity-unwired`) records that every call site of `record_change_event` in the workspace is a test, and leaves open who the corpus wants calling it. The corpus wants the set change rate reported per registered dimension (`tab:monitoring:dimension-health`), and the one place in the package that knows a set changed is `detect_and_emit_changes` at `src/identity/maintenance_loop.rs:418`, which computes the exits and the entries at `src/identity/maintenance_loop.rs:427` and `src/identity/maintenance_loop.rs:428` and passes both to a `debug!` macro at `src/identity/maintenance_loop.rs:434` and to nothing else. The three arguments the recording method takes (`src/health/identity_tracker.rs:110`) are exactly the entries, the exits and the current set, and all three are live at that line.

That is not where the repair goes, and this is the part that shrinks it. The maintenance loop holds no handle on the trackers — they are created and destroyed on the owner's side (`src/owner/lifecycle.rs:644` and `src/owner/lifecycle.rs:670`) — and it does not need one, because the owner already receives the entries and the exits as the lifecycle events it handles at `src/owner/lifecycle.rs:235` and `src/owner/lifecycle.rs:239`. Everything the tracker wants is already delivered to the process that owns the tracker. The repair is therefore a call on the owner's side over data it already has, and it requires no change to the identity cluster whatsoever. That makes it the fourth registered repair to shrink once the owning cluster was read, after the ledger sweep, the label-time reconstruction and the Schur skip counter, and the first to shrink to a change in a cluster other than the one that was read.

**The deferral pass's identity pins were correct at the audit cut and are now overtaken by delivery.** The threshold trigger proved as precise as this audit argued: the public budget now adds exactly the split, create, evict and spatial decay inputs the pin found absent, and the formerly derived and fixed values are the explicit host-overridable defaults (`entry:memory:identity-thresholds`). The description and coordinate semantics cross the same registration into diagnostic health and nowhere into Core behaviour (`entry:memory:audit-metadata`).

The state-side pins closed together. The competitive index publishes the same graph total the feature formerly reconstructed (`entry:memory:total-importance`); the assessment-time outcome reads use an exact pure decay view through the shared clock functions (`entry:memory:decay-view`); and competitive exit now resets only measurement state, retaining outcome state across re-entry until a full graph interval census shows that eviction removed the interval (`entry:memory:warm-start`). Each register citation moved to the implemented retirements, while the historical finding above remains the evidence that the gaps were real at the audit cut.

**Remark (Three defects in the cluster's own comments, and one that refutes its neighbour)** · `rem:assayer:identity-prose-defects`

Rustdoc is not corpus, so a doc comment contradicting the code is a finding against the comment (`conv:assayer:audit-measure`). Three stand here and the third is a shape the campaign has met between files and between clusters but not yet inside four lines.

The competitive cell's own definition states its interval twice and wrongly both times. `src/identity/competitive.rs:53` says the cell covers `[lo, hi)` and gives `hi = lo + 2^(128 - depth)`; the shipped `hi` is one less than that and the interval is closed at both ends, which the containment test three lines further on implements (`src/identity/competitive.rs:91`) and which eight of the module's own tests pin. The neighbouring type in the shared types module states the closed convention explicitly and says it departs from the specification's half-open notation deliberately (`src/types.rs:432`), so the corpus's position is disclosed and correct and this one comment is the only place that contradicts it. Nothing is wrong today; the comment is the documentation the next author of a boundary condition will read.

The maintenance loop's decay helper refutes itself across three lines. At `src/identity/maintenance_loop.rs:384` a labelled notice asks for the test helper's selectivity argument to be reconciled with the production contract, naming that contract as one; the comment immediately beneath it at `src/identity/maintenance_loop.rs:387` states that the helper's zero "matches the production `apply_decay` path"; and the production path four dozen lines below passes one (`src/identity/maintenance_loop.rs:491`). The upstream record agrees with the notice and not with the comment. So a reader is told the two paths agree by a comment, told they do not by the notice above it, and can settle it only by reading a third file. The persistence tranche recorded this shape inside one comment block as a caution about how little distance the failure needs (`entry:assayer:wl-persistence-warm-start-standardisation`); here the distance is three lines and the refuting statement is the notice that was written to fix it.

And the module header carries a status line that stopped being true. Every file in the cluster opens with a comment saying the module is layer-two data structures to be wired into the engine at layer three, above a crate-level attribute suppressing dead-code and unused-import warnings for the whole module (`src/identity/mod.rs:15`). The wiring happened: the cluster is reached from the registration surface, the assessment path and the label path. What survives is the suppression, and it is load-bearing for this tranche's census — the compiler would have named the observation channel's unreached methods without it, so the allowance is the reason the first census form had to be run by hand.

**Remark (What was cleared, and the falsifier that would overturn each)** · `rem:assayer:identity-cleared`

Six checks came within a line of becoming findings and are recorded with what would overturn them, since a clean check reported as silence is not reported at all.

The competitive set takes both terminal and transition cells from the depth-limited extraction (`src/identity/competitive.rs` via `src/identity/maintenance_loop.rs:201`), and the obvious reading is that transitions are internal nodes and should be filtered out. The corpus forbids the filter in terms: the set is the graph entries within the cutoff and there is "no further filtering: no state filter, no minimum width, no coverage requirement" (`def:keyspace:competitive-set`). Conformant. It would be overturned by any corpus sentence distinguishing the two cell kinds, and none exists.

The early return when the graph has seen nothing (`src/identity/maintenance_loop.rs:195`) is a filter the same sentence appears to forbid. It is cleared because at zero total importance the graph has no entries for the extraction to return, so the branch changes the cost and not the answer. It would be overturned by an upstream extraction that returns a root cell from an unobserved graph, which the upstream record does not describe.

Single ownership of the graphs (`dec:memory:graph-owner`) holds by construction: the graph is a private field of a private struct held only in the loop's own vector (`src/identity/maintenance_loop.rs:123`), and no accessor exposes it. It would be overturned by any method returning a reference to it, and there is none.

The feed-forward boundary holds at the two places the corpus puts it. The surface accepts a coordinate and nothing else (`src/identity/mod.rs:182`), which is (`dec:memory:observation-surface`); the increment is fixed inside the loop at `src/identity/maintenance_loop.rs:408`, which is step two of (`alg:keyspace:observation-protocol`). It would be overturned by a magnitude reaching either, and none does.

Per-dimension publication holds (`dec:memory:competitive-publication`): the loop iterates dimensions and swaps each dimension's own pointer (`src/identity/maintenance_loop.rs:478`), so no dimension waits on another. It would be overturned by a single shared index, and the ordering defect registered above does not touch this — the independence is conformant and the sequencing within one dimension is not.

And the loop's decay reads an injected clock rather than the standard library's (`src/identity/maintenance_loop.rs:487`), which is the property (`entry:assayer:wl-staleness-raw-clock`) found violated elsewhere and is conformant here, with the field's own comment giving the reason. It would be overturned by a direct instant reading, and the only one in the file is the parameter the injected clock supplies.

The identity layer's other two decay rates were composed with their cadences as the graph rate was, and they part company. The outcome averages' rate ships at the tabulated 0.998 (`src/config/types.rs:297`), is host-configurable, and is applied once per elapsed hour by the lazy write decay (`src/identity/cell_state.rs:179`) — so the row that carries the fortnight half-life is conformant in both its factor and its period, which is what makes the graph row's departure a defect of that row rather than of the layer's arithmetic. The measurement averages' smoothing factor is tabulated at 0.95 and ships as the complement of that at the writing site (`src/lib.rs:970`), also conformant in value.

What is not settled is the outcome averages' *smoothing* factor, and it is recorded as **unspecified** rather than cleared. The decay table gives that row a decay rate and no smoothing factor, and the outcome-state table names its components exponential averages without stating one (`tab:keyspace:outcome-state`), so the corpus fixes how fast identity outcome history ages and not how fast it responds. The shipped value is 0.95 at `src/owner/label_path.rs:232`, hardcoded under a comment conceding that no configuration source exists for it, and it is the number the corpus states for the measurement row rather than one derived for this one. Nothing here is divergent; what is registered is that a host can tune the rate at which a cell forgets and cannot tune the rate at which it learns, and that the constant it cannot tune was borrowed from a different mechanism's table.

One check is cleared with a qualification rather than a falsifier. The active-cell lookup returns the containment chain the corpus specifies and returns it in the order the corpus specifies, but it obtains it by scanning every cell in the index and sorting the survivors (`src/identity/competitive.rs:188`), where the corpus says in terms that step three "is a walk rather than a search" and prices it at the receiving cell's depth (`alg:keyspace:observation-protocol`). The answer is identical, so no feature is wrong; what is false is the corpus's cost statement, and the code's own comment concedes it by naming the linear scan and the set size. It is not registered as an entry because the set size is bounded in practice at the ten to twenty-five the corpus itself states, and because the corpus fixes that size by observation rather than by configuration, so the two cost models differ by a factor nobody can configure into a problem.

**Entry (A signal declaration's shape parameters are never validated, and two of them reach the assessment path)** · `entry:assayer:wl-signal-declaration-unvalidated`

**Locus** `src/signal/schema.rs:106`, the schema constructor, whose only refusal is a duplicate name at `src/signal/schema.rs:111`; and `src/api/builder.rs:128`, where that constructor *is* the builder's signal validation, under a comment saying so. The two consequences land at `src/signal/value.rs:370`, the clamping helper, and at `src/signal/value.rs:318`, the ordinal arm. No labelled notice stands at any of these and the item is new.

**Disposition** divergent-code-wrong against (`dec:degradation:error-partition`), which fixes the partition as total: a structural contract violation — a caller supplying something the contract does not admit — is a hard error, because it is the caller's defect and the caller can fix it. A clipping interval whose lower bound exceeds its upper is exactly that, supplied at construction, at the one surface in the package that is fallible by design. It is admitted.

What it becomes instead is the second half of the divergence, and it is against (`dec:degradation:infallible-core`), which says there is no error channel on the assessment call and the call returns an assessment, always. Under a reversed interval it does not return at all: the clamp the encoder reaches panics. This was verified by recomputation rather than read off the standard library's documentation — the helper's own two lines, run against a reversed interval and against an interval with a non-finite bound, abort in both cases with the message naming the ordering.

A third departure rides with them and is smaller. The encoder's five other arms sanitise their result, and the module's own contract at `src/signal/value.rs:271` states that non-finite values are replaced by zero; the ordinal arm at `src/signal/value.rs:318` clamps without sanitising, so a maximum of zero against a value of zero produces a quotient that is not a number and the clamp passes it through unchanged. The same recomputation confirms it, and confirms that the neighbouring cyclic arm's identical hazard is caught because that arm does sanitise.

**Severity** high, and the argument is about which contract is broken rather than about how likely the input is. The corpus draws its error partition by who can act (`dec:degradation:error-partition`), and this defect inverts the answer on both sides: the host, which could fix its declaration immediately, is told nothing at build time; and the assessment call, which the corpus promises the host may treat as infallible, is the place the defect surfaces — as a panic, which is worse than the error channel the corpus removed, because it unwinds the assessing thread rather than returning a degraded result the host was told to expect. The not-a-number path is quieter and reaches further: nothing downstream sanitises again, so the value enters the signal block, the standardisation pass and every interaction formed over it, and a single such coordinate makes the assembled vector unusable without any flag being raised.

**What conformance would mean** the schema constructor refuses a clipping interval whose bounds are reversed or non-finite, a divisor, maximum or period of zero, and a hashed or vector width of zero, returning the build error the surface already has; and the ordinal arm sanitises its quotient as its five siblings do. The first is the repair the partition requires and the second is the belt the module already claims to wear.

**Coverage** none on either half. The signal suite is large and exercises every shape at well-formed parameters; no test declares a reversed interval, a zero divisor, a zero maximum or a zero width, so nothing in the corpus observes either behaviour.

**Repaired** in wave eight at `c2440f5e`, both halves as the conformance line asks. The schema constructor refuses every shape parameter the encoding cannot honour — reversed or non-finite clipping bounds, a divisor, maximum or period of zero, a hashed or vector width of zero — under a new build-error variant naming the declaring signal, so the structural violation lands on the fallible surface where the caller can fix it (`test:crate:schema-refuses-malformed-shape-parameters`) and surfaces at the builder (`test:crate:builder-refuses-malformed-signal-declaration`); the boundary shapes the constraint does not name — equal bounds, negative divisor, maximum and period — stay admitted (`test:crate:schema-admits-well-formed-boundary-shapes`). And the ordinal arm wears the belt its five siblings do: the quotient that is not a number encodes to zero per the module's own contract (`test:crate:ordinal-arm-sanitises-nan-quotient`).

**Remark (What the four census forms returned in the signal cluster)** · `rem:assayer:signal-census`

The cluster is small, its fan-in is narrow — one construction site, one assessment call, one health query — and on the extraction tranche's prediction from fan-in it should hold nothing. It holds one thing in the first form and one in the fourth, and the corpus disposes of both.

The first form returns the schema's bulk encoder (`src/signal/schema.rs:173`) and three of its neighbours: the signal count, the name iterator (`src/signal/schema.rs:244`) and the value's own finiteness predicate (`src/signal/value.rs:143`), none of which any production path calls. The bulk encoder is the interesting one, because it is not redundant machinery but an earlier design: the cache encodes signal by signal at `src/signal/cache.rs:274` precisely to avoid allocating the full block per signal, and says so in its own comment. So the second form clears it — the corpus requires the block to be filled and it is filled, by the other route — and what remains is a public function documented with an example, exercised by the doc-test and by the external suite, that no shipped path reaches. The name iterator has no caller anywhere in the workspace, not even a test, and is the one item in either cluster that nothing at all reaches; the notice immediately above the schema type (`src/signal/schema.rs:66`) asks for a declaration-order iteration that the standardisation classes need, which is very nearly this function, so the item is recorded as a surface that was built for a consumer that was written to want something adjacent.

At the audit cut, the fourth form returned the cache health type while four of its five fields were constructed on every health query and dropped one line later: assembly took the eviction count alone. The two methods that read the other four, `hit_rate` and `utilisation`, had no production caller. That was the same shape the posterior tranche met in a diagnostics type (`rem:assayer:model-linalg-cross-cites`) and was not a new finding because the metrics record's deferral stated it in terms and named both loci (`entry:metrics:signal-cache`).

The register's prediction has since paid: the report schema now carries hit, miss, eviction, size and capacity, and the full-report mapper calls the two existing derived methods. The activation added no cache behaviour and the pin is completed and retired. This is exactly the repair the audit forecast — schema carry and mapping around quantities the cache already computed.

The third form returns nothing: every field of every type in this cluster is written by something, and the cache's counters are written on the path that reads them.

**Remark (What the signal cluster got right, and the two near misses)** · `rem:assayer:signal-cleared`

This is the most conformant cluster the campaign has audited, and saying so is part of the measurement. Seven checks cleared, two of them within a sentence of becoming findings, and each is recorded with what would overturn it.

All seven shapes encode as the declaration schema states (`schema:signal:shapes`), checked arm by arm against the table's third column: the clipped value, the signed logarithm of one plus the scaled magnitude, the half threshold, the unit normalisation, the sine and cosine of the phase, the indicator at the hashed bin, and the per-element clipping. All seven widths match the width table (`rem:signal:shape-widths`), including the cyclic shape's two, which is the row that table exists to warn about. The block's width is the sum of the declared widths in declaration order (`src/signal/schema.rs:125`). It would be overturned by any arm returning a length other than its shape's width, and none does.

The two persistence modes are realised as the chapter defines them: the entity-persistent positions are merged from the cache and written back, the request-scoped positions are overlaid without being cached, and a signal absent under either mode leaves its positions at zero (`src/signal/cache.rs:151`). This also settles the ambiguity the persistence tranche flagged when it found the word used in two senses across two clusters: the sense here is scope across requests within a process, and the sense there is durability across restarts, and the two do not meet — the schema is checkpointed for the compatibility check (`src/persistence/checkpoint.rs:253`) and the cache is not checkpointed at all.

The cache's own properties clear against the table the identity chapter holds (`tab:keyspace:signal-cache`). The capacity is configurable and its default is the tabulated hundred thousand (`src/config/types.rs:545`), which is worth naming because the founding worklist's six shipped defaults all depart from their tables and this one does not. Eviction is least-recently-used by construction. Values are cached in encoded form, which is (`dec:retention:cache-preencoded`), and the cache is neither persisted nor checkpointed and repopulates from ordinary traffic on a miss, which is (`dec:retention:cache-losable`). The two rows nothing computes are the entry size and the total memory, and those are the deferral above rather than a divergence.

The first near miss is the schema's stored offset. (`req:signal:schema-fixed`) ends by saying that no position is stored per signal, and the schema entry carries an offset field (`src/signal/schema.rs:73`), which reads as a flat contradiction. It is not one: the offset is within the signal block, not into the feature vector, and the requirement's sentence is about composing the block's start with the declaration order — the block's start is resolved by the dimension map, as the sole resolver requires (`dec:vector:sole-resolver`), and the offset is the prefix sum of the declared widths in declaration order, computed once. What makes it safe is the requirement's own first sentence: the schema is fixed at construction, and the type has no method taking a mutable reference to itself, so the cached derivation cannot drift from the order it was derived from. It would be overturned by any mutation path on the schema, and there is none. This is the fifth time the campaign has nearly produced a finding by reading a corpus sentence rather than the environment it sits in.

The second near miss is the eviction counter's hit branch (`src/signal/cache.rs:180`), which tests whether the cache shrank across a lookup-and-replace and can never be true, because replacing an existing key does not reduce the size. It is dead rather than wrong, and it is dead in the safe direction: the branch that does the counting is the miss branch at `src/signal/cache.rs:200`, whose condition — the size unchanged and equal to the capacity — is exactly the condition under which an insertion evicted something. The counter is correct, the exported total is correct, and the alert the catalogue prescribes over it (`src/metrics/catalog.rs:27`) has a sound input. It would be overturned by an eviction path that leaves the size below capacity, which the underlying structure does not have.

**Remark (What the two clusters' tests pin, and a class the weak-test entry does not carry)** · `rem:assayer:identity-signal-pins`

Four results for the authoring wave, and the fourth is a class the weak-test entry has no row for.

A fourth and a fifth test pin a divergence as their expected value, and unlike the three before them these two pin the same divergence from opposite sides. The inline `outcome_update_positive` (`src/identity/cell_state.rs:449`) asserts that the flag set leaves the adverse rate at zero; the external suite's counterpart (`src/tests/identity.rs:159`) asserts that the flag unset moves it, under a comment calling that case the adverse one. Both authored claims state the inversion as the intended behaviour, and the second states it in a comment that contradicts the corpus's sign convention outright, so a reader learning what the field means from this corpus learns the opposite of what (`conv:valence:sign`) fixes.

The tautological row gains two, and they are the clearest instances the campaign has met. `measurement_update_alarm` and `measurement_suspicion_tracks_aggregate` (`src/identity/cell_state.rs:393` and `src/identity/cell_state.rs:425`) are named for the two update rules the corpus tabulates, and each writes the smoothing step out longhand in its own body and asserts on the number it just computed. Neither calls the production writer at `src/lib.rs:960`, which is where those rules are actually implemented and where two of them are wrong (`entry:assayer:wl-identity-measurement-rules`). The shape matters beyond the row's usual one: these are not merely tests that cannot fail, they are tests whose existence is why nobody noticed that the module they are written in contains no update code at all.

The third result is a caution about the conditional-oracle class rather than an instance of it. The competitive cell's constructor guards its depth with a debug assertion (`src/identity/competitive.rs:77`), which is the ingredient the posterior tranche's new class is made of; it is not an instance, because no test in either cluster relies on that assertion as its only oracle, and because the release-mode behaviour is benign — the underlying interval helper saturates rather than overflowing at a depth past the domain width. It is recorded so that the next tranche meeting a debug assertion does not register it as the class by reflex: the class is about tests without assertions, not about assertions without release builds.

And the fourth is new. Six tests carrying six authored claims stand against the observation channel type that no production path constructs (`entry:assayer:wl-identity-observation-channel-unused`), and two of those claims — that the queue refuses work past capacity rather than growing, and that every refusal is counted — are the corpus's own statements of the degradation posture (`dec:memory:overflow-degrades`). The claims are true of the code they test and the code they test does not run. This is not a no-oracle test, not a tautology, not a vacuity and not a duplicate: the oracle is real and adequate, and it is pointed at the wrong artefact. Its consequence is the same as the duplication class's — the register counts coverage the shipped path does not have — but its cause is different, and the repair differs too, because a duplicate is deleted and this must be re-aimed. The production copy's overflow behaviour is separately covered at `src/identity/mod.rs:303`, so the corpus is not left uncovered by re-aiming; what it loses is a count.

## Thirteenth tranche: report reception, configuration and the numerics · `sec:assayer:audit-tranche-report-config`

The clusters are `src/report/` — the report index and its entry types, the per-Sentinel slot, the ingestion pipeline and the structural and cell-level validation — and `src/config/`, the configuration hierarchy and its defaults, together with `src/numerics.rs`, which the eleventh tranche declined and asked this one to take. Taking the three together was the right grouping for a reason the scope map does not record: the configuration reference governs the second cluster, adjudicates the third by name in a closing sentence, and supplies the tabulated bounds against which the first cluster's one shared parameter is measured.

The tranche read the architecture chapter's assumed-properties table and its report-validation precondition for `report/`, the encoding chapter for the coordinate lift, the extraction chapter for what the report is read for, the configuration reference whole for `config/`, the construction record's eager-validation decision for the builder's obligations toward it, and the risk core-model chapter's regime definition for `numerics.rs`. The Gaussian foundations chapter was read and discarded, which is the second half of the scope-map correction below.

**Remark (The specification column fails in a fourth way, and the numerics correction is confirmed positively)** · `rem:assayer:report-config-scope-map`

The `report/` row's specification column says "the assembly chapter". There is a document of that name — `docs/spec/assembly.md` — and it is not a chapter. It is the build manifest: a single table listing which parts assemble, in order, into the published specification, with no prose and no property of anything. Every previous specification-column failure was a chapter that said nothing about the cluster, a chapter that said a true half, or a plural where there is one. This row names a table of contents. A tranche following it would have found no statement to measure against and no way to tell that it had been sent to the wrong kind of document, because the row's phrasing gives no hint that the target is not prose at all.

What the row should name is the architecture chapter of the foundations, on two counts. The assumed-properties table (`tab:architecture:sentinel-properties`) fixes the six properties of the batch-report interface the Core relies on and does not check, and the report-validation precondition (`pre:architecture:report-validation`) fixes the two it does check and the three named errors it raises. Between them they state every checkable claim this cluster realises, and the second of the two is matched by the code line for line.

The `numerics.rs` correction the eleventh tranche recorded is confirmed, and confirmed positively rather than by elimination. That tranche argued the file is governed by the calibration record's shared-transition decision and not by the substrate record the row names. The falsifier that would have refuted it does not exist and the confirming text does: the calibration table's closing sentence names the arrangement in terms — "The transition steepness lives as a constant of the numerics rather than as a field of the calibration configuration" (`tab:config:calibration`) — and the definition it tabulates (`def:platt:regimes`) states the transition the file computes. The Gaussian foundations chapter mentions no function in `numerics.rs` at all. Six rows are now known wrong on the specification side and five on the record side.

The `config/` row is the campaign's cleanest. Its specification column names the configuration reference and the configuration reference is exactly what governs the cluster; its record column names construction and calibration, and both are read here for real work. It is recorded because the map has been wrong often enough that a row which is simply right is now worth naming.

**Entry (The corpus contradicts itself over who lifts a coordinate, and the code obeys the more specific text)** · `entry:assayer:wl-report-width-lift-contradiction`

**Locus** `src/report/ingestion.rs:117` and `src/report/validation.rs:72`, neither of which performs any width normalisation, against two corpus texts that cannot both be true.

The assumed-properties table's last row says coordinates and cell intervals "are interpreted in the Sentinel's declared domain width, which the Core normalises to its internal width on reception" (`tab:architecture:sentinel-properties`). The coordinate-lift algorithm says the opposite in terms: "The lift is the responsibility of the integration layer that delivers batch reports to the Core... The Core's report-reception surface accepts a post-lift report... the Core does not perform the lift, and has no way to tell a lifted coordinate from a natively wide one" (`alg:encoding:coordinate-lift`).

**Disposition** divergent-spec-stale, against the table row and not against the code. The burden (`conv:assayer:audit-measure`) places on this is discharged three ways. The row cites the width definition (`def:encoding:domain-width`) as its authority, and that definition says it is an upper bound checked at registration and that "the Core does not verify that reported coordinates lie within the declared domain" — so the row's own citation contradicts the row. The lift algorithm is the specific and mechanical text where the row is a summary line in a table whose stated purpose is to bind the Sentinel rather than the Core. And the disclosure remark (`rem:encoding:unchecked-width`) reasons at length about what happens when the lift shifts by the wrong amount, which is a question that could not arise if the Core performed the lift itself.

**Severity** low as a code matter and medium as a corpus one. Nothing is miscomputed: the code is right and every downstream routing path is consistent with it. What the row costs is a reader, and it costs one in the direction that matters — a host integrator reading the properties table to learn its obligations is told the Core will normalise for it, and the consequence of believing that is the silent whole-hierarchy misplacement the disclosure remark describes.

**What conformance would mean** the row's clause "which the Core normalises to its internal width on reception" becomes a statement that the host lifts before reception, citing the lift algorithm rather than the width definition.

**Coverage** none, and none is possible: the divergence is between two documents, and no test can be written against a sentence.

**Entry (The builder reaches nineteen of the forty-three tabulated parameters the configuration owns)** · `entry:assayer:wl-config-unreached-constraints`

**Locus** `src/api/builder.rs:195`, the numerical validation pass, whose own summary claims it "Checks every configurable parameter for its documented bounds", against `src/config/types.rs` and the tables of (`chap:spec:configuration`).

**Disposition** divergent-code-wrong against the third clause of (`dec:construction:eager-validation`). That clause was left registered rather than absorbed by (`entry:assayer:wl-builder-template-validation`), which named one parameter and two conceded notices and said the shortfall was "at least" that. This entry measures it. Counting only the scalar parameters the `config/types.rs` structures own — excluding the five re-exported external configurations, whose own tranches hold them — the module carries forty-three, and the validation pass reaches nineteen.

| Structure | Parameters | Reached |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `AssayerConfig` own scalars | 1 | 1 |
| `ModelConfig` | 8 | 8 |
| `TemporalConfig` | 3 | 3 |
| `LedgerConfig` | 4 | 1 |
| `CholeskyConfig` own scalars | 2 | 2 |
| `ConcordanceConfig` | 3 | 1 |
| `ConvergenceThresholdConfig` | 12 | 0 |
| `InfrastructureConfig` | 7 | 3 |
| `PersistenceConfig` own scalar | 1 | 0 |
| `BlendStatisticsConfig` | 2 | 0 |

What makes this a divergence rather than a gap is the chapter's own sentence about its constraint column: "the constraint column is part of the specification — a value outside it is a configuration error, not a tuning choice" (`chap:spec:configuration`). The corpus therefore states bounds, states that they are specification, and names the builder as the thing that enforces them before returning an instance. Twenty-four parameters escape that enforcement.

Three of the twenty-four sharpen the finding past a count, because in each the pass applies a rule and then declines to apply the same rule a few lines later. The concordance window's capacity is checked non-zero at `src/api/builder.rs:281` and the blend statistics window's capacity, a rolling-window capacity of the same shape shipping at the same five thousand, is not checked at all. Three of the seven infrastructure capacities are checked non-zero at `src/api/builder.rs:325`, `:332` and `:339`, and the health-event and identity-observation capacities, which fail in exactly the way the checked three would, are not. And `check_rate` — the pass's own helper for a value confined to the open unit interval — is applied to eight rates and not to the concordance percentile, which ships at 0.80 and is confined to that interval by nothing.

**The derivation tranche names the mirror of this entry and it is worth reading beside it** (`rem:assayer:resonance-cross-cites`). The builder has no place to put derivation configuration, which the construction record decides it should not (`dec:construction:core-only-config`) — and the configuration structure the builder builds carries the derivation layer's eight display constants and its reference posture anyway, under two notices conceding that both belong on the host-owned derivation configuration. So the count above excludes nine parameters that are in the structure, are not the Core's, and are validated by nothing at either end. They are not added to the count, because the repair is their removal rather than their validation.

**Severity** high, carried almost entirely by one row. The Ledger's absence threshold is tabulated at three with a constraint of at least one (`tab:config:ledger`) and is the subject of (`entry:assayer:wl-ledger-absence-cast`); this entry adds what that one lacked, which is the tabulated bound the missing check would have enforced. The remaining twenty-three are medium and below, and the twelve convergence thresholds have a real mitigation the others do not: the corpus says of that whole family that "none of them changes what the system does", which the monitoring table (`tab:config:monitoring`) argues of its own rows as well, so a wrong value there misleads a reader and nothing else. Even there the pass omits the one check that is not about a single row's bound — that the warming, converging and converged triples increase — and a configuration that inverts them is accepted.

**What conformance would mean** every row of the configuration chapter whose constraint column is non-empty acquires a check in the pass, and the pass's summary sentence becomes true rather than aspirational.

**Coverage** none. The builder suite asserts shipped defaults (`src/tests/builder.rs:139` asserts the Ledger threshold's three) and constructs successfully; no test supplies an out-of-bounds value to any of the twenty-four and observes that it is accepted.

**Repaired** in wave eight at `4ce1cc4d`. The pass acquires the checks the count measured missing — the Ledger's absence threshold at its tabulated floor with the collection horizon and floor beside it, the concordance interval and the percentile the pass's own rate helper never covered, all twelve convergence thresholds including the one relation no single row states (the warming, converging and converged triples increase), the remaining infrastructure capacities, the persisted checkpoint interval, the blend statistics pair, and the sister rate's tabulated relation above the operational rate — and the label queue is held to its tabulated floor of one hundred rather than merely non-zero. The pass's summary sentence now states its true scope: every scalar the configuration module owns, against the configuration chapter's constraint columns. Each family's refusal is driven through the builder and names its configuration path (`test:crate:validation-reaches-ledger-concordance-persistence-and-blend-rows`), (`test:crate:validation-reaches-the-remaining-infrastructure-capacities`), (`test:crate:validation-requires-the-convergence-figures-to-be-positive`), (`test:crate:validation-requires-sister-rate-above-operational-rate`). The first full-suite run under the new checks caught a live instance of exactly the defect this entry predicted: a persistence fixture declaring its concordance percentile as eighty rather than 0.80, accepted by nothing until now.

**Withdrawn in part** by the decision that deleted the eight generic stage figures rather than validating them (`cav:health:dead-convergence-thresholds`). The counts above are left as the audit measured them, because they are a measurement of the code at audit time and not a running total; what changes is that ten of the twelve convergence rows the wave-eight repair reached no longer exist to be reached, and the cross-stage relation the repair was proudest of — that the warming, converging and converged triples increase — went with the figures it compared. Eight were the generic stage figures. The other two were the Companion's, and they left in the same change by the other door: the sufficiency floor is the host's and moved to the record that owns it (`dec:challenge:sufficiency-floor-owned-here`), while the minimum-samples count named no operation anywhere in the package and was deleted outright. The study that sized the deletion is at [the convergence-thresholds decisions](../reports/convergence-thresholds/decisions.md), and its finding is the one this entry could not have drawn from a count: the rows were unreached by the pass because the specification tabulates no bound for them, and it tabulates none because it names no such stage at all. A check added to a surface the specification does not describe buys a reader nothing, which is why the repair here was the deletion and not a further check.
